The Sūtra of the Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha's Kindness Is Repaid
Tibetan
ཐབས་མཁས་པ་ཆེན་པོ་སངས་རྒྱས་དྲིན་ལན་བསབ་པའི་མདོ · Wylie: thabs mkhas pa chen po sangs rgyas drin lan bsab pa'i mdo · phonetic: Tabkhepa Chenpo Sangyé Drinlen Sabpé Do
Catalog
Toh 353 (Tōhoku catalogue; also cited as D 353 of the Derge edition) · Derge Kangyur · Sūtras (mdo sde) · Discourses (Sūtra)

About this text

The Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha Repays Kindness, in Tibetan Tabkhepa Chenpo Sangyé Drinlen Sabpé Do, is a Mahāyāna discourse that was translated into Tibetan from Chinese rather than Sanskrit, and so it carries a Chinese title in place of the usual Sanskrit one. Set on Vulture Peak near Rājagṛha before a vast assembly of monks and bodhisattvas, it unfolds over nine chapters on how the Buddha, through great skillful means, repaid the kindness he had received, with themes that include compassion, the arising of the mind set on awakening, and the duty to honor and repay one's parents and teachers. It ends with bodhisattvas pledging to uphold the teaching and asking that the sūtra be entrusted to them, and with the Buddha naming it, among other titles, The Compendium of All Grounds of Virtue. It is Toh 353 in the Kangyur, where it is placed among the general sūtras.

Translation

In the language of China: dei phāṅ bya na phhur pou in kyeng sū phim dei yir. In the language of Tibet: The Sūtra of the Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha's Kindness Is Repaid.

The first section.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was dwelling at Rājagṛha, on Vulture Peak Mountain, together with a great saṅgha of monks, twenty-eight thousand monks, all of whom had done what was to be done, had practiced the holy life, had entirely exhausted the fetters binding them to existence, and had attained mastery over their minds like great nāgas. They were Mahākāśyapa, Subhūti, Ājñāta Kauṇḍinya, Fair Goodness, Pūrṇa Maitrāyaṇīputra, Mahāpanthaka, Śāriputra, Mahākātyāyana, Ānanda, Rāhula, and other such thoroughbreds.

He was also together with thirty-eight thousand bodhisattva great beings. All these bodhisattvas, having long generated roots of virtue, had always practiced the holy life under countless hundreds of thousands of koṭis of buddhas. They had fully perfected great aspirations, had penetrated and realized hundreds of thousands of samādhis, had perfected the dhāraṇīs, never forsook great compassion, strove to benefit all great beings just as they are, sustained the lineage of the Three Jewels so that it would not be broken, raised high the banner of the Dharma, had become unbidden spiritual friends to ordinary people, and, having reached the shore of great wisdom, were utterly beyond counting. They were the bodhisattva Avalokiteśvara, the bodhisattva Mahāsthāmaprāpta, the bodhisattva Nityodyukta, the bodhisattva Sublime Qualities, the bodhisattva Melody of Qualities, the bodhisattva Lightning Light, the bodhisattva Universal Sameness, the bodhisattva Foremost Qualities, the bodhisattva King Meru, the bodhisattva Gandhahastin, the bodhisattva Mahāgandhahastin, the bodhisattva Holder of Strength, the bodhisattva Exalted in the Three Realms, the bodhisattva Ever Compassionate, the bodhisattva Ratnapāṇi, the bodhisattva Mighty Light, the bodhisattva Sublime Glory, the bodhisattva Ratnacandra, the bodhisattva Great Strength, the bodhisattva Boundless Intelligence, the bodhisattva Bhadrapāla, the bodhisattva Lion's Roar, the bodhisattva Lion's Deed, the bodhisattva Siṃhavijṛmbhita, the bodhisattva Fulfilled Aspiration, the bodhisattva Ratnakūṭa, the bodhisattva Maitreya, the bodhisattva Mañjuśrī the Youthful, and others. He dwelt together with this great retinue as well.

Besides these, countless hundreds of thousands of gods of the desire realm also came, bearing divine flowers and incense, and, playing divine music, they took their places in the sky above. Besides these, gods, nāgas, yakṣas, gandharvas, asuras, garuḍas, kinnaras, mahoragas, humans, nonhumans, and others came, each with a retinue of hundreds of thousands; they bowed their heads at the Buddha's feet and sat down to one side. At that time the Blessed One was surrounded by a great retinue, who served and worshiped him and, with reverence, praised him.

At that time, through the blessing of the Blessed One, Ānanda entered Rājagṛha in the morning and went for alms from house to house. At that time there was a brahmin, greatly devoted in his filial service to his parents, whose household had fallen to ruin so that he owned nothing at all. Carrying his aged mother on his back, he went begging from house to house. If he obtained food that was delicious and flavorful, or good fruit, he fed it to his parents. If he obtained poor, tasteless food, or dried fruit, he ate it himself. When Ānanda saw this, a mind of great joy arose in him, and he praised the man in pleasing words, saying, "Excellent! Excellent! Son of good family, it is most wonderful that you serve your parents with filial devotion and honor them. Well done!"

Then there was a man who inclined toward the teachings of the six teachers of the wandering ascetics and had become their ally. He was of sharp faculties, learned in the treatises and in the four Vedas, skilled in the stars and in calculation, sharp in reading omens of good and ill, able to know the minds of others through the sciences of divination and the treatises, a teacher of many, and honored by all. Attached to wrong views, he was destroying the sacred Dharma for the sake of gain and fame. Envious of the Buddha, the Dharma and the Saṅgha, and in order to disparage them, he spoke to Ānanda as follows:

"Your teacher, Gautama, son of the Śākya clan, says, 'I am virtuous and possess great qualities,' but that is merely a name, with no true substance. Your teacher Gautama is in truth an evil man. Seven days after he was born his mother died, and that shows that he is evil. While living in the royal palace, he fled from within it, so that his father the king was overwhelmed by grief. His mind disturbed, the king fainted and was sprinkled with water; when seven days had passed he regained his senses and, beating his breast, wept piteously, crying, 'Today I have lost my one divine son!' He was driven to lament, 'My land and my rule are for you alone to command, and you are my only son, so why have you abandoned us and gone off to the mountain hermitage?' Your teacher Gautama is ungrateful: heedless of everything, he rose up and left, and that shows that he lacks filial piety. His father the king built him a palace and took a wife for him, yet he did not do a husband's duty toward his wife but tormented her with grief, and that shows that he is shameless as well."

When Ānanda had heard those words that the man spoke, he was ashamed and embarrassed. Having collected his alms, he returned to where the Buddha was, bowed his head at his feet, and, standing to one side with palms joined, bowing low, he addressed the Blessed One as follows:

"Blessed One, in the Dharma taught by the Buddha, is there no such thing as filial service to one's parents?"

The Buddha said to Ānanda, "Who taught you to ask this question? Was it taught to you by a god, a human, or a nonhuman? Or did you come to ask the Tathāgata this from your own understanding?"

Ānanda replied, "No one taught me, neither god, nāga, preta, human, nor nonhuman. But this morning, when I went for alms, I met a naked wandering ascetic of the following of the six teachers, and he spoke harsh words to me." And he told it in full.

At that time the Blessed One smiled, and from his mouth there came forth light of many different colors, which illuminated, in the eastern direction, beyond countless hundreds of thousands of koṭis of buddha fields, a world system called Highest Supreme. In that world system dwelt the Buddha called King of Joy, the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, endowed with knowledge and conduct, the Sugata, knower of the world, unsurpassed guide of beings to be tamed, teacher, victor, the Buddha, the Blessed One, in the buddha field called Perfectly Arrayed.

That buddha field was perfectly level. Its ground was made of beryl and marked out in a checkered pattern with lines of refined gold. Trees of the seven precious substances grew there, and those trees were as tall as the range of an arrow's flight and beautifully adorned with flowers, fruits, branches and leaves. When they were stirred by a gentle breeze, a sweet sound arose, and ordinary beings who heard those sounds could never have enough of them. Here and there were springs, ponds and bathing pools, filled to the brim with water endowed with the eight qualities. The floors of the pools were strewn with golden sand. On the four sides of the pools, lotuses, puṇḍarika and pāruṣaka flowers with a sublime fragrance, and other flowers, blue, yellow, red and white, each as large as a chariot wheel, covered the surface of the water entirely. In that water birds of many kinds also sang sweet and most delightful songs. In the pools there were boats made of the seven precious substances, in which many beings played and enjoyed themselves as they pleased.

Between those jewel trees, lion thrones one league high and wide were set up and adorned with the seven precious substances. On a throne layered with divine cloth, wafting the scent of divine incense and strewn with divine flowers, the Tathāgata King of Joy was seated in the cross-legged posture. Countless hundreds of thousands of koṭis of bodhisattvas arrayed themselves before him, and joining their palms and bowing, each of them addressed that Tathāgata with the same words:

"Blessed One, through what cause and what condition has this excellent light, blue, yellow, red and white, brilliantly clear and beyond compare, shone from the western direction upon this great assembly, so that all whom the light touches become happy in body and mind? Out of your loving concern, may the Blessed One please explain it."

That Blessed One then spoke as follows: "Sons of good family, listen well and keep it firmly in mind, and I shall explain and set it out for you. Beyond countless hundreds of thousands of koṭis of world systems from here in the western direction, there is a buddha field called Sahā. There the Buddha called Śākyamuni, the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, endowed with knowledge and conduct, the Sugata, knower of the world, unsurpassed guide of beings to be tamed, teacher, victor, the Buddha, the Blessed One, surrounded by a great retinue, is teaching the whole great assembly the sūtra on repaying kindness through great skillful means. This light has shone for the sake of the benefit and happiness of all sentient beings; in order to pull out by the root the thorn of wrong views of all sentient beings; in order to make the beginner bodhisattvas irreversible and steadfast on the path to awakening; in order to establish all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas on the path of the one vehicle that leads to the ultimate; in order that the great bodhisattvas swiftly attain full awakening and repay the kindness of the Buddha, and so that all sentient beings remember great kindness; in order to deliver all sentient beings from the ocean of suffering; and because he wishes all ordinary people to serve their parents with filial devotion."

At that time, in that assembly, some ten thousand bodhisattvas, who had become leaders and teachers of great assemblies, rose from their seats, draped their upper robes over one shoulder, placed their right knees on the ground, and addressed that Blessed One as follows: "Blessed One, please bless us so that, through the blessing of the Blessed One, we may go to that Sahā world system, make offerings to and honor the Tathāgata Śākyamuni, and listen to the sublime Sūtra of the Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha's Kindness Is Repaid."

Then that Buddha said to those bodhisattvas: "Sons of good family, go to that Sahā world system, make offerings to and honor that Buddha, the Blessed One, and give rise to the perception that he is extremely hard to meet. Why is that? The Buddha Śākyamuni, for countless koṭis of niyutas of eons, practiced what is extremely difficult to practice, and out of great compassion made this aspiration:

'When I attain full awakening, may I attain perfect and complete buddhahood in a world system full of wrongdoing, in a place where there are mountains, ravines, gravel, pebbles, thorns and the like, where the beings too are full of afflictions and fully engage in the five acts of immediate retribution and the ten nonvirtues. There, for their benefit and happiness, may I cut off all suffering entirely, make them fully endowed with every happiness, perfect the Dharma body, and pass into nirvāṇa without remainder.' Such was the aspiration he made. Because that Buddha, the Blessed One, first made that aspiration, you should go to that world system and conduct yourselves just as the Buddha himself goes and just as the Buddha himself abides."

When he had spoken, all those bodhisattvas replied with one voice, "We shall do as the Blessed One commands." Then each of those bodhisattvas, surrounded by a retinue of some hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of bodhisattvas, set out for where the Sahā world system was. In whatever world systems they went to, those world systems quaked in six ways, and a great light shone everywhere. From the sky above, the gods rained down mandārava flowers and great mandārava flowers. Illuminated by the great light, world systems as numerous as the sands of the Ganges River also quaked through miraculous power. Besides this, countless hundreds of thousands of divine instruments, unplayed, sounded of their own accord in the sky above. Those bodhisattvas arrived at Vulture Peak Mountain, in the presence of the Tathāgata; they bowed their heads at his feet, circumambulated the Buddha three times, and sat down to one side.

At that time the light of the Tathāgata illuminated, in the southern direction, beyond eighty koṭis of niyutas of buddha fields, a world system called Light of Qualities. In that world system dwelt the Buddha called Attending to Signs, the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, endowed with knowledge and conduct, the Sugata, knower of the world, guide of beings to be tamed, unsurpassed, teacher, victor, the Buddha, the Blessed One, in the buddha field called Utterly Pure.

That buddha field was perfectly level. Its ground was made of beryl and marked out in a checkered pattern with lines of refined gold. Trees of the seven precious substances grew there, and those trees were as tall as the range of an arrow's flight and adorned with flowers, fruits, branches and leaves. When they were stirred by a gentle breeze, a sweet sound arose, and ordinary beings who heard those sounds could never have enough of them. Here and there were springs, ponds and bathing pools, filled to the brim with water endowed with the eight qualities, and the floors of the pools were strewn with golden sand. On the four sides of the pools, lotuses, puṇḍarīka and pāruṣaka flowers with a sublime fragrance, and other flowers, blue, yellow, red and white, each as large as a chariot wheel, covered the surface of the water entirely. In that water birds of many kinds also sang sweet and most delightful songs. In the pools there were boats made of the seven precious substances, in which many beings played and enjoyed themselves as they pleased.

Between those jewel trees, lion thrones one league high and wide were set up and adorned with the seven precious substances. On a throne layered with divine cloth, wafting the scent of divine incense and strewn with divine flowers, the Tathāgata Attending to Signs was seated in the cross-legged posture. Countless hundreds of thousands of koṭis of bodhisattvas arrayed themselves before him, and joining their palms and bowing, each of them addressed that Tathāgata with the same words:

"Blessed One, through what cause and what condition has this light of excellent color, blue, yellow, red and white, brilliantly clear and beyond compare, shone from the northern direction upon this great assembly, so that all whom the light touches have become happy in body and mind? Out of your loving concern, may the Blessed One please explain it."

Then that Blessed One spoke as follows: "Sons of good family, listen very well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain and set it out for you. Beyond eighty koṭis of niyutas of world systems from here in the northern direction, there is a buddha field called Sahā. There the Buddha called Śākyamuni, the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, endowed with knowledge and conduct, the Sugata, knower of the world, guide of beings to be tamed, the Buddha, the Blessed One, surrounded by a great retinue, is teaching the whole great assembly the sūtra on repaying kindness through great skillful means. This light has shone for the sake of the benefit and happiness of all sentient beings; in order to pull out by the root the thorn of wrong views of all sentient beings; in order to make the beginner bodhisattvas irreversible and steadfast on the path to awakening; in order to establish all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas on the path of the one vehicle that leads to the ultimate; in order that the great bodhisattvas swiftly attain full awakening and repay the kindness of the Buddha, and so that all sentient beings remember great kindness; in order to deliver all sentient beings from the ocean of suffering; and because he wishes ordinary people to serve their parents with filial devotion."

At that time, in that assembly, some ten thousand bodhisattvas, who had become leaders and teachers of great assemblies, rose from their seats, draped their upper robes over one shoulder, placed their right knees on the ground, and addressed that Blessed One as follows:

"Through the blessing of the Blessed One, please bless us so that we may go to that Sahā world system, make offerings to and honor the Tathāgata Śākyamuni, and listen to the sacred sūtra The Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha Repays Kindness."

Then that Buddha spoke to the bodhisattvas as follows: "Sons of noble family, go to that Sahā world system, make offerings to that Buddha, the Blessed One, and honor him, and give rise to the perception that he is extremely difficult to meet. Why is that? For countless eons, for koṭis of niyutas of eons, the Buddha Śākyamuni practiced what is extremely difficult to practice, and with great compassion he made this aspiration: 'When I attain full awakening, may I attain full awakening in a world system full of evil, a place with mountains, ravines, gullies, gravel, thorns, and the like, a place where sentient beings are full of afflictions and fully engage in the five acts of immediate retribution and the ten nonvirtues. For the sake of their benefit and happiness, may I cut off all their suffering and endow them with all happiness, perfect the Dharma body, and pass into nirvāṇa without remainder.' Thus he made his aspiration. Since that Buddha, the Blessed One, made such an aspiration at the very beginning, when you go to that world system, you should act just as you would if the Buddha himself had come and as if the Buddha himself were present."

When he had said this, all those bodhisattvas said with one voice, "We shall do as the Blessed One commands." Then each of those bodhisattvas, surrounded by a retinue of hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of bodhisattvas, went to where the Sahā world system was. In whatever world systems they passed through, those world systems shook in six ways and were completely illuminated by great light. From the sky above, the gods rained down mandārava flowers and great mandārava flowers. Illuminated by great light, world systems as numerous as the grains of sand of the river Ganges also shook through miraculous power. In addition to that, hundreds of thousands of countless divine instruments resounded of their own accord in the sky above without being played. Those bodhisattvas arrived in the presence of the Tathāgata on Vulture Peak Mountain, bowed their heads at his feet, circumambulated the Buddha three times, and sat down to one side.

At that time, the Tathāgata Śākyamuni, with light rays of five colors, illuminated a world system called Pure Abode, which lies to the west beyond hundreds of thousands of countless koṭis of niyutas of buddha fields. In that world system, a buddha named Sun, Moon, and Lamp Light, a tathāgata, arhat, perfectly and completely awakened buddha, perfect in knowledge and conduct, a sugata, knower of the world, unsurpassed guide of beings to be tamed, teacher, victor, buddha, blessed one, dwells in a buddha field called Sukhāvatī.

That buddha field is perfectly level. Its ground is made of beryl, laid out in a checkered pattern with lines of refined gold, and trees of the seven precious substances grow there. Those trees are about an arrow's flight in height, beautifully adorned with flowers, fruits, branches, and leaves. Stirred by a gentle breeze, they give forth a pleasing sound, and the individual beings who hear those sounds never tire of them. Here and there are springs, ponds, and bathing pools, filled to the brim with water possessing the eight good qualities. The beds of the pools are covered with golden sand. On the four sides of the pools, lotus flowers, white lotuses (puṇḍarīka), pāruṣaka flowers, and other flowers of blue, yellow, red, and white, endowed with sublime fragrance and each the size of a chariot wheel, completely cover the surface of the water. Within that water, too, many kinds of birds give forth exceedingly delightful, melodious sounds. In those pools are boats made of the seven precious substances, in which many sentient beings play and enjoy themselves as they please.

Between those jewel trees are set up lion thrones, each a full league in height, adorned with the seven precious substances. Divine cloths are spread in layers upon the thrones, the scent of divine incense wafts about them, and divine flowers are strewn over them. On such a throne the Tathāgata Sun, Moon, and Lamp Light sits with legs crossed, and before him are arrayed hundreds of thousands of countless koṭis of bodhisattvas. Joining their palms and bowing, each of them addressed that Tathāgata with one voice as follows:

"Blessed One, for what cause and for what condition has this excellent light, blue, yellow, red, and white, brilliantly clear and beyond compare, appeared from the east to these great assemblies, so that all whom the light has touched have become happy in body and mind? Out of your loving kindness, Blessed One, please explain it."

Then that Blessed One spoke as follows: "Son of noble family, listen well and keep it thoroughly in mind, and I shall explain it to you in detail."

"To the east of here, beyond hundreds of thousands of countless koṭis of niyutas of world systems, there is a buddha field called Sahā. There a buddha called Śākyamuni, a tathāgata, arhat, perfectly and completely awakened buddha, perfect in knowledge and conduct, a sugata, knower of the world, unsurpassed guide of beings to be tamed, teacher, victor, buddha, blessed one, surrounded by a great assembly, is teaching the sūtra The Great Skillful Means of Repaying Kindness to all of that great assembly. This light has appeared for the sake of the benefit and happiness of all sentient beings; in order to pull out by the root the thorn of wrong views of all sentient beings; in order that bodhisattvas who have newly given rise to the mind of awakening not turn back from the path of awakening but become steadfast; in order to establish all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas on the path of the one vehicle that leads to the ultimate; in order that the great bodhisattvas swiftly attain full awakening and repay the kindness of the Buddha; so that all sentient beings remember great kindness; in order to deliver all sentient beings from the ocean of suffering; and because he wishes ordinary people to serve their parents with filial devotion."

At that time, in that assembly, some ten thousand bodhisattvas, who had become leaders and teachers of great assemblies, rose from their seats, draped their upper robes over one shoulder, placed their right knees on the ground, and addressed that Blessed One as follows: "Through the blessing of the Blessed One, please bless us so that we may go to that Sahā world system, make offerings to and honor the Tathāgata Śākyamuni, and listen to the sacred sūtra The Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha Repays Kindness."

Then that Buddha spoke to those bodhisattvas as follows: "Sons of noble family, go to that Sahā world system, make offerings to that Buddha, the Blessed One, and honor him, and give rise to the perception that he is extremely difficult to meet. Why is that? For countless eons, for koṭis of niyutas of eons, the Buddha Śākyamuni practiced what is extremely difficult to practice, and with great compassion he made this aspiration: 'When I attain full awakening, may I attain full awakening in a world system full of evil, a place with mountains, ravines, gullies, gravel, thorns, and the like, a place where sentient beings are full of afflictions and fully engage in the five acts of immediate retribution and the ten nonvirtues. For the sake of their benefit and happiness, may I cut off all their suffering and endow them with all happiness, perfect the Dharma body, and pass into nirvāṇa without remainder.' Thus he made his aspiration. Since that Buddha, the Blessed One, made such an aspiration at the very beginning, when you go to that world system, you should conduct yourselves just as you would if the Buddha himself had come and as if the Buddha himself were present."

When he had said this, all those bodhisattvas said with one voice, "We shall do as the Blessed One commands." Then each of those bodhisattvas, surrounded by a retinue of hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of bodhisattvas, went to where the Sahā world system was. In whatever world systems they passed through, those world systems shook in six ways, and great light completely illuminated them. From the sky above, the gods rained down mandārava flowers and great mandārava flowers. Illuminated by great light, world systems as numerous as the grains of sand of the river Ganges also shook through miraculous power. In addition to that, hundreds of thousands of countless divine instruments resounded of their own accord in the sky above without being played. Those bodhisattvas, too, arrived in the presence of the Tathāgata on Vulture Peak Mountain, bowed their heads at his feet, circumambulated the Buddha three times, and sat down to one side.

At that time, the Tathāgata Śākyamuni, with light rays of five colors, illuminated a world system called King of Mighty Renown, which lies to the north beyond five hundred koṭis of niyutas of buddha fields. In that world system, a buddha called Crimson Lotus Light, a tathāgata, arhat, perfectly and completely awakened buddha, perfect in knowledge and conduct, a sugata, knower of the world, unsurpassed guide of beings to be tamed, teacher, victor, buddha, blessed one, dwells in a buddha field called Stainless.

That buddha field is perfectly level. Its ground is made of beryl, laid out in a checkered pattern with lines of refined gold, and trees of the seven precious substances grow there. Those trees are about an arrow's flight in height, beautifully adorned with flowers, fruits, branches, and leaves. Stirred by a gentle breeze, they give forth a pleasing sound, and when individual beings hear that sound, they never tire of it. Here and there are springs, ponds, and bathing pools, filled to the brim with water possessing the eight good qualities. The beds of the pools are covered with golden sand. On the four sides of the pools, lotus flowers, white lotuses (puṇḍarīka), pāruṣaka flowers, and other flowers of blue, yellow, red, and white, endowed with sublime fragrance and each the size of a chariot wheel, completely cover the surface of the water. Within that water, many kinds of birds give forth exceedingly delightful, melodious sounds. In those pools are boats made of the seven precious substances, in which many sentient beings play and enjoy themselves as they please.

Between those jewel trees are set up lion thrones, each a full league in height, adorned with the seven precious substances. Divine cloths are spread in layers upon the thrones, the scent of divine incense wafts about them, and divine flowers are strewn over them. Upon such a throne the Tathāgata Crimson Lotus Light sits with legs crossed, and before him are arrayed hundreds of thousands of countless koṭis of bodhisattvas. Joining their palms and bowing, each of them addressed that Tathāgata with one voice as follows:

"Blessed One, for what cause and for what condition has this excellent light, blue, yellow, red, and white, brilliantly clear and beyond compare, appeared from the south to these great assemblies, so that all whom the light touches become happy in body and mind? Out of your loving kindness, Blessed One, please explain it."

Then the Blessed One spoke as follows: "Sons of noble family, listen well and keep it thoroughly in mind, and I shall explain it to you in detail. To the south of here, beyond hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of world systems, there is a buddha field called Sahā. There a buddha called Śākyamuni, a tathāgata, arhat, perfectly and completely awakened buddha, perfect in knowledge and conduct, a sugata, knower of the world, unsurpassed guide of beings to be tamed, teacher, victor, buddha, blessed one, surrounded by a great assembly, is teaching the sūtra The Great Skillful Means of Repaying Kindness to all of that great assembly. This light has appeared for the sake of the benefit and happiness of all sentient beings; in order to pull out by the root the thorns of all sentient beings; in order that bodhisattvas who have newly given rise to the mind of awakening not turn back from awakening but become steadfast; in order to establish all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas in the one vehicle that leads to the ultimate; in order that the great bodhisattvas swiftly attain full awakening and repay the kindness of the Buddha; so that all sentient beings remember great kindness; in order to deliver all sentient beings from the ocean of suffering; and because he wishes ordinary people to serve their parents with filial devotion."

At that time, in that assembly, some ten thousand bodhisattvas, who had become leaders and teachers among the great assemblies, rose from their seats, draped their upper robes over one shoulder, placed their right knees on the ground, and addressed that Blessed One as follows: "Through the blessing of the Blessed One, please bless us so that we may go to that Sahā world system, make offerings to and honor the Tathāgata Śākyamuni, and listen to the sacred sūtra The Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha Repays Kindness."

Then that Buddha spoke to those bodhisattvas as follows: "Sons of noble family, go to that Sahā world system, make offerings to that Buddha, the Blessed One, and honor him, and give rise to the perception that he is extremely difficult to meet. Why is that? For countless eons, for koṭis of niyutas of eons, the Buddha Śākyamuni practiced what is extremely difficult to practice, and so with great compassion he made this aspiration: 'When I attain full awakening, may I attain full awakening in a world system full of evil, a place with mountains, ravines, gullies, gravel, thorns, and the like, a place where sentient beings are full of afflictions and fully engage in the five acts of immediate retribution and the ten nonvirtues. For the sake of their benefit and happiness, may I cut off all their suffering and endow them with all happiness, perfect the Dharma body, and pass into nirvāṇa without remainder.' Thus he made his aspiration. Since that Buddha, the Blessed One, made such an aspiration at the very beginning, when you go to that world system, you should regard it just as you would if the Buddha himself had come and as if the Buddha himself were present."

When he had said this, all those bodhisattvas said with one voice, "We shall do as the Blessed One commands." Then each of those bodhisattvas, surrounded by a retinue of hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of bodhisattvas, went to where the Sahā world system was. In whatever world systems they passed through, those world systems shook in six ways, and great light completely illuminated them. From the sky above, the gods rained down mandārava flowers and great mandārava flowers. Illuminated by great light, world systems as numerous as the grains of sand of the river Ganges also shook through miraculous power. In addition to that, hundreds of thousands of countless divine instruments resounded of their own accord in the sky above without being played. Those bodhisattvas arrived in the presence of the Tathāgata on Vulture Peak Mountain, bowed their heads at his feet, circumambulated the Buddha three times, and sat down to one side.

In the same way, all the great bodhisattvas who came from the four intermediate directions, from above and below, from the ten directions, each together with countless hundreds of thousands in their retinues, came to where the Blessed One was, made offerings and showed honor, and offered praise. Then all of them with a single voice extolled the Blessed One in some hundred thousand verses. Having thus praised the Buddha, they too sat down to one side.

At that time the Sahā world system also became completely pure. There were no rugged mountains, no great or small mountains, no rivers, streams, ponds, pools, lakes, ravines, or chasms. The sentient beings living there, illuminated by the light, came to where the Buddha was. With joyful and delighted minds they joined their palms, bowed, and touched their heads to the ground, and they sat gazing at him without blinking, with hearts full of devotion.

Then the Blessed One drew the light rays back in. Those light rays circled his body seven times and then vanished into the crown of his head. Ānanda saw this happen, and he understood in his mind that doubt had arisen in all of the great assembly. In order that the Tathāgata's deeds of skillful means be revealed, that the eye of wisdom arise in people of the future, that all ordinary people cross the river of craving, reach the far shore, and be established in happiness, and that ordinary people remember and recognize the great kindness of their parents, teachers, and gurus, he rose from his seat, arranged his robes properly, draped his upper robe over one shoulder, placed his right knee on the ground, joined his palms, bowed, and addressed the Blessed One as follows:

"Venerable Blessed One, I, Ānanda, attending upon the Buddha, have seen you smile. If there is surely some intention behind this, then in order to cut through the net of doubt of the great assembly, may the Blessed One please teach it. May the Buddha please explain it."

This concludes the first chapter, called "The Introduction," of The Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha Repays Kindness.

At that time, in that great assembly gathered there, a lotus made of the seven precious substances arose from the very ground. Its stem was made of silver, its petals of gold, and its base of precious crystal, and it was adorned all over with nets of pearls. Then the Blessed One Śākyamuni rose from where he was seated and sat on that lotus with his legs crossed. As soon as he had sat down on it, he manifested a completely pure body.

From that body he displayed multitudes of bodies of the five destinies of rebirth. In each multitude of bodies, too, he displayed different forms of some ten thousand hundreds of thousands of kinds. In each of those kinds of forms, too, he displayed some hundred thousand multitudes of bodies. In each of those multitudes of bodies, too, he displayed multitudes of bodies as numerous as the grains of sand of four Ganges rivers. In each of those multitudes of bodies as numerous as the grains of sand of four Ganges rivers, too, he displayed multitudes of bodies as numerous as the atoms of the four continents. In each of those multitudes of bodies as numerous as atoms, too, he displayed multitudes of bodies as numerous as the atoms of a great trichiliocosm, a world system of a billion worlds. In each of those multitudes of bodies as numerous as atoms, too, he displayed, in each of the ten directions, multitudes of bodies as numerous as the atoms of hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of buddha fields. Thus he manifested multitudes of bodies of sentient beings, filling the whole expanse of space, equal to the realm of phenomena, inconceivable in number.

At that time, having manifested so many multitudes of bodies, the Tathāgata spoke as follows to Ānanda, to the great bodhisattvas gathered and assembled from the ten directions, and to the great assemblies:

"Sons of noble family, today the Tathāgata will speak, through the wisdom of omniscience, words that are true. Although the Dharma cannot be expressed in words, the Tathāgata, by the power of skillful means, will teach with names and signs the Dharma that has neither signs nor names.

"It is like this. When the Tathāgata first took birth and circled in saṃsāra, he fully experienced all those different kinds of multitudes of bodies, inconceivable, as numerous as atoms. Since he has thus experienced all those multitudes of bodies, there is no sentient being that has not been the Tathāgata's father or mother, and there is no sentient being whose father or mother the Tathāgata has not been. For the sake of all who have been his parents, he constantly practiced what is extremely difficult to practice. Of what is extremely difficult to give, there was nothing he did not give: his head, the supreme limb, his eyes, his brain, lands, cities, wives, sons, daughters, elephants, horses, chariots of the seven precious substances, vehicles, clothing, food, drink, bedding, medicines for illness, and all the necessities he was asked for. With intensely strenuous diligence he guarded moral discipline, practiced generosity, gained great learning, cultivated meditative concentration, and practiced wisdom; he carried out every difficult practice. He strove in diligence without weariness or fatigue and without interruption, in order to serve his parents with filial devotion, to recognize their kindness, and to repay it; to establish all sentient beings swiftly in unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening; to bring the Tathāgata's former aspirations to complete fulfillment; to make it known that, this being so, all sentient beings have received the great kindness of the Buddha; so that the great compassion of the Tathāgata never abandons sentient beings; so that, constantly and with great skillful means, he might cultivate even and impartial loving-kindness, for inconceivable eons, toward all sentient beings dwelling in the twenty-five states of existence of the three realms, and act with skillful means endowed with equanimity; so that, realizing all sentient beings to be empty, the Dharma to be empty, and the five aggregates to be empty, without retreating, without sinking, and without agitation, he might meditate with skillful means on emptiness, the true characteristic; and so that, skilled in the means of training to master the three vehicles without abandoning any of them, he might be skilled in practicing the sublime and most profound domain of activity.

"He realized the characteristics of phenomena with perfect clarity, and he realized and understood that the teachings of the Buddha, from beginning to end, are not of only one kind. Yet ordinary people, intoxicated and corrupted, are tormented by the three torments and sink into the ocean of suffering. Obscured by the four inversions, they become inverted with regard to contaminated phenomena. Wholly conceiving what is untrue, they view what is without self as a self, what is impermanent as permanent, what is unsatisfactory as pleasant, and what is impure as pure. Afflicted by birth, aging, sickness, and death, and by momentary impermanence, they are entangled by the five obscurations and the ten entanglements. They circle in the three states of existence and experience birth and death without beginning or end."

"Therefore the Tathāgata, according to what suits each and every being, has taught the three baskets, distinguishing them into the nine divisions of the scriptures and up to the twelve divisions of the scriptures. In accordance with the wishes of each, he has spoken many sūtras. Some who are attached to the words hold, 'This alone is enough for me; I have attained nirvāṇa.'

"Moreover, through the force of the Tathāgata's former aspiration of great compassion, all those of concordant karma in the world systems of the ten directions have been gathered together. Intending to teach the sacred Dharma, the sūtra, in that maṇḍala of the great assembly where all those of concordant karma have thus gathered, he ensures that the semblance of the Dharma will never be cut off and that all ordinary people will attain great happiness.

"Therefore the Tathāgata knows how to tame and teach without missing the right time, and he passes into nirvāṇa without missing the right time. In some world systems, the Tathāgata is renowned as the one called Vairocana, the arhat, perfectly and completely awakened buddha, perfect in knowledge and conduct, sugata, knower of the world, unsurpassed guide of beings to be tamed, teacher, victor, buddha, blessed one. At some times he goes to the abode of Tuṣita and becomes the teacher of the gods. At other times he descends from the abode of Tuṣita and shows himself living in Jambudvīpa for a lifespan of eighty years.

"Therefore the Tathāgata is inconceivable. The world systems, too, are inconceivable. The maturation of karma, too, is inconceivable. The intentions of sentient beings, too, are inconceivable. The samādhis of meditative concentration, too, are inconceivable. The power of the nāgas, too, is inconceivable. This is the inconceivability of the Buddha: if all sentient beings wish to know what the Buddha has in mind, he lets everyone, down to the lowest beings of weak faculties, know it completely. Whoever among all sentient beings wishes to see the Buddha, he lets them see him at once. If he does not let them see him, then even though he is right before their eyes, they do not see him. Therefore, even though śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas have the higher knowledge of the divine eye, they are unable to see him. Beyond that, even though the Buddha radiates great light and illuminates everything from the Peak of Existence down to the hell of the beings who committed the acts of immediate retribution, those who have the good fortune to be tamed will see it, while those without such fortune will not see it even though it is right before their eyes. At some times the Tathāgata deliberately urges beings on; at other times he says nothing at all. Therefore the Buddha, the Blessed One, is inconceivable, extremely difficult to fathom, extremely difficult to know and realize. How, then, did you find the courage to ask the Tathāgata about something so profound and so difficult to practice? This question you have asked is truly an act of love and compassion toward all sentient beings: it blocks the path to the three lower realms and opens the path to gods and humans. Ānanda, I shall now teach you briefly about the difficult practice of serving one's parents with filial devotion. Listen well and keep it in mind."

Then the Blessed One spoke as follows to Ānanda, to those great bodhisattvas, and to the great assemblies:

"Long ago, countless, boundless, measureless eons in the past, in the land of Vārāṇasī there appeared in the world a buddha called Vipaśyin, a tathāgata, arhat, perfectly and completely awakened buddha, perfect in knowledge and conduct, sugata, knower of the world, unsurpassed guide of beings to be tamed, teacher, victor, lord of the world, teacher of gods and humans. The lifespan of that buddha lasted twenty intermediate eons. The sacred Dharma also remained for twenty intermediate eons. The semblance of the Dharma also remained for twenty intermediate eons.

"During the time when the semblance of the Dharma was being practiced, there appeared in the world a king of men called Rāja, who ruled the land of Vārāṇasī. That king had twenty thousand queens, some four thousand ministers, and five hundred elephants. He ruled over sixty minor kingdoms and had some eight hundred cities. That king had three sons, and each of the three ruled a minor kingdom.

"At that time, the king of Vārāṇasī was learned and endowed with wisdom. Since he always governed the land with just Dharma, no one could be said to go without. Through the power of the king's merit and good qualities, the rains never failed to come in their season, the harvests were always good, and so the people who lived there were happy and content.

"At that time the king of Vārāṇasī had an evil minister called Rāhu. The minister Rāhu rebelled against the king and drew up the four divisions of an army: a division of elephants, a division of chariots, a division of cavalry, and a division of foot soldiers. Having drawn them up, he attacked the king of Vārāṇasī and took the king's life. Having thus killed the king, he deployed the four divisions of the army and killed the eldest prince as well, who ruled a minor kingdom on the border. Then he overthrew the middle prince too, and drew up his army to march upon the minor kingdom on the border ruled by the youngest prince.

"That youngest prince was wonderfully handsome in form and features, nobly beautiful, virtuous by nature, and gentle. He smiled as he spoke, and whatever he said, he said for the sake of benefit, never causing harm. He always governed his land with just Dharma and did not live by wrong means, and so the people of his land were happy, content, and prosperous, rich in possessions, wealth, and precious things, their storehouses full. All praised and extolled the good qualities of that king, and all the gods of the sky and the gods of the world rejoiced in him and honored him.

"At that time that king had a single son, a prince called Sujāti. He was very gentle and bright, possessed a mind of loving-kindness, and delighted in giving. His body was golden in color, his seven places were full and even, and he possessed in full the marks of a great being. He was seven years old, and his father the king loved him dearly and held him most precious.

"At that time a god who guarded the palace spoke to that king as follows:

"Does the king know? The minister Rāhu has rebelled and seized the kingdom, and he has killed the king your father. He has also sent out his fourfold army, and he has deprived your two elder brothers, the kings, of their life force. His troops will arrive here before long, so why does the king not save his life and go away?"

When the king heard these words, all the hairs on his body stood on end. His body trembled, he was dejected, and his mind was tormented with grief. He rolled on the ground and fainted. After a long while he came round a little, and slowly he spoke to the god in the sky: "Who are you, whose voice speaks like this but whose form cannot be seen? Are these words you have spoken really true?"

The god said, "I am the god who guards the king's palace. King, because you are wise and endowed with merit and good qualities, and rule the kingdom by the Dharma of uprightness, I spoke to you just now. You should flee quickly, before you are afflicted by suffering."

Then the king went into the palace and thought, "Now I must go to another country." And he thought further, "There are two roads that lead to the neighboring country. If I take the near road, I will reach that country in seven days; if I take the long road, I will arrive in fourteen days. I will certainly go by the near road." Having thought this, he provided himself with provisions for seven days and went outside the palace. Then he turned back into the palace, took Prince Sujāti on his lap, and gazed at him without blinking; and he stood up as if startled, and sat down again.

At that time the queen, seeing him so ill at ease and seemingly afraid, said to the king, "Great king, why do you show a look of fear and alarm? Your body seems covered in dust, your hair is tangled, and your breathing is uneasy, as if you had come to the loss of your kingdom, or had been parted from those you love, or had met with a hated enemy; and there are inauspicious signs. Please tell me what this is."

The king said, "You do not know what has gone wrong for me, so do not speak."

The queen said, "The king and I have two bodies but one mind, like the two wings of a bird, the two legs of a body, or the two eyes in a head. Since it is so, why do you keep this secret and not tell me?"

The king said to the queen, "Do you not know? The minister Rāhu has conceived an evil intent and rebelled against the king. He has killed the king my father and cut short the lives of my two elder brothers, the princes, and now he is coming with an army to attack me. So I am going to flee to another country." Having said this, he took Prince Sujāti on his lap and set out on the road, and the queen too followed after the king.

At that time the king, overcome by grief and with his mind confused, took the fourteen-day road and so lost his way. That road was hard to travel and the grass was coarse, and there was not even a little grass or water. After many days had passed, their provisions ran out. At the start he had kept it secret, meaning to go alone, and had taken no more than seven days' provisions; but he had lost his way, three people had been eating, and he had taken the fourteen-day road, so there was still a long way to go when the provisions ran out.

Then the king and the queen cried aloud and wept: "Alas! Alas! How wretched! From the time we were born until now we have never experienced anything like this, and now we are experiencing it. Now we are miserable and destitute!" So saying, they beat their heads with their hands. They rolled on the ground, their bodies covered with dust, and lamented in these words: "What evil, unwholesome karma did we do in a former life? Did we kill a father, a mother, or an arhat? Did we disparage the sacred Dharma? Did we cause a schism in a harmonious Saṅgha? Were we hunters or fishermen? Did we falsify scales and measures and plunder others? Did we make use of the Saṅgha's property? What did we do, that we should experience suffering like this?"

So they lamented. If they rested even a little, then when the enemy arrived they would surely lose their lives; and if they went on along the road, they were tormented by hunger and thirst and were on the point of death. The king and queen, tormented by such suffering, cried aloud and wept, fell to the ground, and fainted.

After a long while they came round, and the king thought, "All three of us together will die right here. I will kill the queen and so save my own life and my son's." Having thought this, he drew his sword and made ready to kill the queen. But Prince Sujāti caught the king by the hand and said, "What are you thinking of doing now?"

His father the king, his eyes brimming with tears, said to the prince, "I mean to kill your mother, the noble queen, and so keep you alive. Even if she is not killed, she will die anyway. Whether I die or live, I mean to kill your mother the queen in order to keep you, my son, alive."

Then Prince Sujāti said to his father the king, "If the queen is killed, I will not eat her flesh, for nowhere is it possible for a son to eat his mother's flesh. Since I too will then die, why does my father the king not kill me instead and so save the lives of my father and mother?"

When his father the king heard him say this, he at once staggered and fell, rolling on the ground, and said to the prince, "You are like my own eyes. Who would gouge out his own eyes and eat them? I would rather die than kill my son. I cannot kill my son and eat his flesh."

Then Prince Sujāti spoke again to his father the king: "If my father and mother kill me, my flesh and blood will rot and spoil before long and will be of no use. If you will not grant me one request, you are not loving parents; so please do not go against my wishes."

Then his father the king told his son the prince, "I will not go against your wishes, so tell me quickly whatever you ask." And Prince Sujāti said to his father the king:

"Out of love for me, my father and mother, cut three measures of flesh from my body each day. Offer two portions to my father and mother, and if I myself eat one portion, it will serve to sustain body and life."

Then the king granted what the prince had asked. Each day he cut three measures of flesh and divided it into three portions: the king and queen ate two portions, and the prince himself ate one. Sustaining body and life in this way, they set out on the road and went on and on. When they were still two days short of the neighboring country, the flesh of the prince's body was used up. Only his joints and sinews held together, and with only a remnant of life left in him, he fell to the ground.

Then the king and queen took the prince on their laps, cried out loudly, wailed greatly, and said, "In our ignorance we two have eaten your flesh and made you suffer the pain of such sickness. The road is still long, and the flesh of your body is used up. Now let us lay down our corpses together in this place."

Then Prince Sujāti said to his father the king in a faint voice, "From here to the other side there remains no more than a day's journey. I cannot stir or move, so I will give up my life here. King and queen, do not say, like base and lowly people, 'Let us lay down our corpses together in this place.' If you think of me with love and affection, please do not go against what I have in mind. Please strip off whatever flesh there still is between my bones, leaving none, and go on to the neighboring country."

Then the king did as the prince had asked. He stripped off all the flesh there was between the joints and the bones and divided it into three portions. The king and queen ate two portions, and the prince himself ate one. Then the king and queen set out on the road and went.

Prince Sujāti got up and, standing there, watched the king and queen go. The king and queen went off crying aloud and weeping, and Prince Sujāti, with a mind of attachment to them, gazed and gazed at them without blinking until they were out of sight. After a long while he fell back to the ground. Drawn by the smell of flesh and blood, many flies and gadflies gathered from the ten directions until they covered the prince like a blanket, eating and stinging his body, and he was tormented by suffering.

At that time, while the last remnant of his life had not yet been cut off, the prince cried out and made this aspiration: "May my past unwholesome karma be purified and exhausted by this. From this day on I will not commit it. Since I have offered my body to my father and mother as an act of giving, may my father and mother have eleven merits greater than before: may they sleep in happiness, wake in happiness, have no bad dreams, be protected by the gods, and be honored by people; may they be free of disputes, thieves, enemies and robbery, and may they be fortunate at all times. Since I have given the flesh and blood remaining on my body to the flies, may they all be filled and satisfied, and in a future time may I attain buddhahood. When I have attained buddhahood and gained the Dharma body, may that Dharma body dispel for all beings the famine of hunger and thirst and the grave disease of saṃsāra, of birth and death."

When the prince made this aspiration, heaven and earth quaked in six ways. The sun lost its brightness. The wild beasts and game animals took fright and ran in all directions. The ocean was violently churned. Mount Meru shook and swayed, and everything up to the sky of the Thirty-Three quaked violently.

At that time Śakra (Indra), lord of the gods, came down to Jambudvīpa with the gods of the desire realm. In order to frighten Prince Sujāti, they emanated bodies of lions, tigers, wolves and the like. Glaring with their eyes, their jaws gaping and their fur bristling, they let out great roars, and as if leaping upon the prince, they menaced him.

At that time Prince Sujāti, seeing those ferocious beasts, spoke in a low voice and told them, "If you want to eat me, eat as you please. What need is there to frighten me with such a show of power?"

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, said, "We are not lions or wild beasts. I am Śakra, lord of the gods, and I have come to test you and to examine you."

At that time, when the prince saw Śakra, lord of the gods, he was overjoyed and delighted. Then Śakra said to the prince, "You have given what is extremely hard to give, offering your body's flesh and blood to your father and mother. Through the merit of this, do you wish to become a king of māras? Or do you wish to become a king of Brahmās? A lord of the gods, a king of men, or a wheel-turning king?"

The prince said, "I wish to be none of these: neither a king of māras, nor Brahmā, nor a king of the gods, nor a king of men, nor a wheel-turning king. I wish for unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening in order to liberate all sentient beings."

Śakra said, "You seem to be utterly deluded. Unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening is achieved only after practicing extremely difficult austerities for a very long time. Can you bear such intense suffering?"

The prince said, "Even if a blazing iron wheel were to spin continually on the crown of my head, such suffering would not turn me back from unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening."

Śakra said, "Who would believe these empty words of yours?"

Then the prince made a vow and spoke this aspiration: "If the words I have spoken are false, and I have deceived the lord of the gods, may the wounds on my body never heal. If the words I have spoken are true, may my body be restored and my blood turn to milk."

No sooner had he said this than the wounds on his body were restored as they had been before, and his blood turned to milk. The prince's form became even more fine, lovely and beautiful than before. He rose and bowed his head at the feet of the lord of the gods.

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, said: "Prince, excellent! Most excellent!

"Through supremely heroic diligence you will without doubt attain unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. When before long you attain unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, may you act first for my benefit." Having said this, Śakra, lord of the gods, vanished into the sky right where he was.

At that time the king and queen reached the king of that neighboring country, and that king came out from afar to meet them and provided them with whatever necessities they wished. Then the king and queen told that king in detail how the prince had offered his body's flesh and blood to his father and mother. When that king heard all that had happened, he repaid the kindness of the prince, who had shown such great devotion by offering his body's flesh and blood to his father and mother: he arrayed a fourfold army and led it, together with that king, against the land of Vārāṇasī.

At that time that king, equipped with the fourfold army, set out on the road. When he reached the place where he had parted from the prince, he thought, "My son must surely have died right here. I will gather up at least my son's bones and carry them back to my own country." With this thought he cried aloud and wept, and went on to gather his son's bones. But from afar his own son came up to him, the wounds on his body healed, finer and more beautiful even than before.

Then the king took him on his lap and said, "Are you still alive?" And the prince told his father the king and the queen what had happened. The king and the queen rejoiced greatly. They all mounted elephants and went back to their own country, and through the power of the prince's merit he regained his own country. He empowered Prince Sujāti as king and set him on the throne.

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "If you think that the one who was the father king at that time, on that occasion, was someone else, do not regard it so. Why is that? The father king at that time, on that occasion, has now become my father, called Śuddhodana. If you think that the one who was the mother at that time, on that occasion, was someone else, do not regard it so: she is now my mother, called Māyā. If you think that the one who was called Prince Sujāti at that time, on that occasion, was someone else, do not regard it so: that prince at that time, on that occasion, is now I, called Śākyamuni. The one who was Śakra, lord of the gods, at that time, on that occasion, is now the one called Ājñāta Kauṇḍinya."

When the Blessed One taught this chapter on devotion to one's parents, in that assembly some two hundred thousand bodhisattvas attained eloquence of speech skilled in what benefits and brings happiness to all beings. A hundred and twenty thousand bodhisattvas attained acceptance of the Dharma that phenomena are unborn. Moreover, śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas gathered from the ten directions, as numerous as the finest atoms, abandoned the mind of the two vehicles and reached the final goal through the one vehicle. Laymen and laywomen as numerous as the finest atoms attained fruits ranging from the first fruit up to the fruit of the two vehicles. Some hundred thousand gods and humans generated the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. Among the many other hosts of gods, nāgas, pretas, asuras, gandharvas, garuḍas, kinnaras, mahoragas, humans, nonhumans and so on, some generated the mind set on unsurpassed awakening, and others generated the mind set on the awakening of śrāvakas or of pratyekabuddhas.

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "This is how bodhisattvas, for the sake of all sentient beings and in order to show devotion to their parents, offer even the flesh and blood of their bodies to their parents and practice what is extremely hard to practice."

When the whole great assembly had heard what the Buddha said, each of them gained what brought them benefit and happiness. Delighted and overjoyed, they bowed their heads, circumambulated him to the right, and departed.

This concludes the second chapter, called Devotion, from The Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha Repays Kindness.

The Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha Repays Kindness, second fascicle.

At that time, on that occasion, the Blessed One sat amid the great assembly, blazing and radiant with light: like the disk of the sun outshining the many stars; or like a rainbow coiled like a nāga, glittering and bright, so that one can never gaze at it enough; so dazzling that the mind reels merely to think of it. Nothing can serve as an example for it. The light of the firefly vanishes the moment the sun rises. The sun and moon have hundreds of thousands of rays, yet beside Śakra they are like a heap of charcoal. Śakra has a clear radiance, yet beside the light of the Great Brahmā he is like a potsherd or a shard of stone, or like night compared with the light of a precious jewel. And all the light of the great king Brahmā, with his hundreds of thousands of supreme rays, became like a heap of charcoal beside the light of the Tathāgata.

Why was this? Because the color of the Tathāgata's light illuminated all the world systems of the ten directions. When that light touched the beings dwelling in them, the blind saw forms with their eyes, the hunchbacked became straight in body, those with impaired faculties had their faculties made whole and possessed all their major and minor limbs, and the intoxicated regained their mindfulness. In short, they were freed from everything unpleasant and obtained whatever they wished.

At that time, in that very assembly, some seventy bodhisattva great beings rose from their seats, bowed their heads at the Buddha's feet, circumambulated him a hundred thousand times, and sat down to one side.

All of them, with one voice, praised the Tathāgata in a hundred thousand verses. They were the bodhisattva Inconceivable, the bodhisattva Melody Free of Conceptions, the bodhisattva Mind of Pure Bliss, the bodhisattva Vimalakīrti, the bodhisattva Boundless Melody, the bodhisattva Great Fame, the bodhisattva Crown Light, the bodhisattva Steadfast Lion, the bodhisattva Sole Delight, the bodhisattva Abandoning Conceptions, the bodhisattva Heaped Wisdom, the bodhisattva Intelligence Abiding in Virtue, the bodhisattva Utterly Signless, the bodhisattva Radiant Intelligence, the bodhisattva Intelligence That Calms Anger, the bodhisattva Well Guarded, the bodhisattva Mighty Faith, the bodhisattva Exalted Flower, the bodhisattva All-Blissful, the bodhisattva Work of Noble Wisdom, the bodhisattva Ready Exposition, the bodhisattva Unthinkable, the bodhisattva Brahmadatta, the bodhisattva Jewel Work, the bodhisattva Abode of Divine Flowers, the bodhisattva Excellent Mind, the bodhisattva Immeasurable Dharma, the bodhisattva Mind of Fame, the bodhisattva Heaped Eloquence, the bodhisattva Gate of Sovereignty, the bodhisattva Ten Powers, the bodhisattva Holder of the Ten Powers, the bodhisattva Guarded by the Great Noble One, the bodhisattva Unsurpassed, the bodhisattva Delighting in Peace, the bodhisattva Abiding There, the bodhisattva Countless Gods, the bodhisattva Meru Light, the bodhisattva Trunk of Great Weight, the bodhisattva Exalted Above Causes, the bodhisattva Going Alone, the bodhisattva Exalted Splendor, the bodhisattva Skilled with the Great Host, the bodhisattva Protector of Lineages, the bodhisattva Upholder of the Three Worlds, the bodhisattva Possessed of Readiness, the bodhisattva Proclaimed Fame, the bodhisattva Clear Sunlight, the bodhisattva Mighty Lion, the bodhisattva King of Seasons, the bodhisattva Lion Essence, the bodhisattva Revealer of Existence, the bodhisattva Far-Shining Light, the bodhisattva Lion Mountain, the bodhisattva Giver of Rebirth, the bodhisattva Jinadatta, the bodhisattva Supreme Banner, the bodhisattva Delighted, the bodhisattva Steadfast Diligence, the bodhisattva Undiminished, the bodhisattva Renowned, the bodhisattva Fearless, the bodhisattva Unattached God, the bodhisattva Great Lamp, the bodhisattva Light of the World, the bodhisattva Sublime Melody, the bodhisattva Power of Great Means, the bodhisattva Dispeller of Darkness, the bodhisattva Peerless Quarter, and others.

All these bodhisattvas made this vow in the presence of the Buddha: "After the Blessed One has passed into nirvāṇa, we will protect the Buddha's Dharma, spread it throughout all the world systems of the ten directions, and keep it from being cut off. Why is that? Because, having seen the inconceivable, sublime light of the Tathāgata, from that light we have also come to hear the inconceivable Dharma of the Buddha. Having heard that Dharma, we are freed from the obscurations of the mind, and all afflictions are purified; our minds have become utterly pure, like divine precious gold, and so everything appears clearly.

"Recalling such qualities of benefit and happiness, we have come to regard the Tathāgata as the great Teacher. Regarding him as loving parents, we always remember the Buddha's kindness and acknowledge the Buddha's kindness. Why is that? Because by hearing the sublime Dharma, before long one will sit at the seat of awakening, turn the wheel of the sublime Dharma, and liberate all sentient beings; and they too, by hearing the Dharma, will attain unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening."

Then the Tathāgata said to the great assembly: "These seventy bodhisattva great beings, long ago, as many countless hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of eons in the past as there are finest atoms, attended upon buddhas, blessed ones, as numerous as the finest atoms in world systems as many as hundreds of thousands of times the sands of the river Ganges. They always practiced pure conduct under those buddhas and pleased those victors with untiring minds. Guarding their own bodies with loving-kindness, they uphold the Buddha's Dharma, and with great compassion they continually work for the benefit and happiness of all in the ten directions.

"Whatever sentient being, at the very moment of death, hears the name of one of these bodhisattvas, or of two, or three, or four, up to all seventy, and goes for refuge to them with his whole life, will, as soon as he passes from that life, be born miraculously from a lotus in a world system where a buddha dwells. He will be entirely free of desire, will not be born from a womb, and will be free of filth and impurity. His body will be completely pure and fragrant, and he will be pleasing to all and honored by them. Because he is pleasing to all and honored by them, his mind will be filled with joy. Because his mind is filled with joy, he will generate the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

"By generating the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, he will generate a mind of loving-kindness and great compassion toward all sentient beings. By generating such a mind of loving-kindness and compassion, there will arise in him a mind that does not abandon any being to indifference. Moreover, there will arise in him the mind to bring benefit and happiness to all sentient beings; the mind to accomplish the aims of self and others; the mind to remove what becomes an obscuration or an obstacle; the mind to pacify afflictions; the mind to attend upon spiritual friends and always honor them; the mind to listen to the Dharma with eager interest; the mind to keep it in attention without forgetting and to retain its excellent meaning; the mind that, hearing little, understands much meaning; and the mind that does not want to hear much without understanding the meaning.

"Then there will arise in him a mind of clear faith in reality itself, and a mind of faith in the true meaning of suchness. Having generated a mind of faith in the true meaning of reality, there will arise in him the mind to practice yoga just as it has been taught. Having generated the mind to practice yoga just as it has been taught, there will arise in him a mind that does not turn back. Having generated such a mind that does not turn back, there will arise in him the mind of equality toward all those sentient beings, which is the antidote.

"Just as I do not delight in being born in the twenty-five states of existence of the three realms, so it is too for all living creatures, those with form and those without form, the four-footed and the many-footed, down to ants and beetles. Therefore a bodhisattva, even at the cost of his own body and life, does not do the deed of taking the life of another.

"Just as I guard and take care of my possessions, my wealth and goods, grain, clothing, food, drink, elephants, carriages and vehicles, lands, towns, wife and children, down to the limbs of my body, and do not like others to carry them off and steal them, so it is too for all sentient beings. Therefore a bodhisattva, even at the cost of his own body and life, does not give rise to the thought of stealing the wealth, clothing, food, drink and possessions of sentient beings.

"Just as I do not want others to behave improperly toward my beautiful sister, wife or daughter-in-law, so it is too for all sentient beings. Therefore a bodhisattva, even at the cost of his own body and life, does not give rise to a wrong mind with a base notion toward another's beautiful woman, let alone commit an improper, base act.

"Just as I do not like slander and harsh words that lead to strife, so it is too for sentient beings. Therefore a bodhisattva, even if his own body and life should perish, does not tell lies or speak words that set people at strife with one another.

"Just as I do not like being struck with stones and sticks, beaten with whips, or treated with force, so it is too for all sentient beings. Therefore a bodhisattva, even if his own body and life should perish, does not harm sentient beings with stones and sticks, whips, or acts of punishment.

"Just as I do not like the harms of being fettered with iron chains, stocks and weapons, of being seized, tied and bound with bonds, so it is too for all sentient beings. Therefore a bodhisattva, even if his own body and life should perish, does not inflict the harms of iron chains, stocks, seizing, binding and the like.

"Just as I do not like others to despise me, to lord it over me and treat me violently, or to presume on their power, act tyrannically, look down on me and treat me badly, so it is too for all sentient beings. Therefore a bodhisattva, even if his one body and life should perish, does not harm sentient beings by improper, unfitting means.

"Just as I delight in happiness when others honor me, make offerings to me, or praise me with pleasing words, so I too give to sentient beings clothing, food, drink, bedding, medicine for illness, and all the requisites of comfort.

"Suppose I am carrying out a great undertaking, whether for the sake of the Buddha, for the sake of the Dharma, or for the sake of the Saṅgha, and my intelligence is not equal to accomplishing the aim, so that it is not accomplished, and I am unhappy and tormented with grief. If a wise and intelligent person sees that I am unhappy and grieving because the aim I was pursuing has not been accomplished, and says to me, 'Son of good family, do not be unhappy. I will be your companion in accomplishing your aim, and we will accomplish it as you wish,' then a mind of great joy arises in me toward him. Therefore I shall be a companion in the work that is done for the benefit and happiness of sentient beings.

"Suppose I am harmed by the king, by thieves, by water or by fire, or am seized, tied or bound by ministers and the like, and am tormented by unhappiness and grief. If a wise and intelligent person sees me thus tormented by grief and says to me with gentle words, 'Do not grieve. For your sake I will petition the king or the chief minister, and if need be I will give them a bribe, or by all sorts of means I will somehow free you from the suffering of your distress,' then merely hearing those words, my mind becomes joyful. Therefore a bodhisattva trains with diligence to master everything that is to be known, even the arts and crafts.

That is, he trains to become skilled in what is to be known: the fields of learning, the fields of craft, music, sports, numbers, calculation, the spells of illusion, medicine, elephants, horses, riding, the ways of dealing with enemies, the leading of armies, investigation, and so on. Having thus trained to be skilled in every art, he does not act against the wishes and intentions of the many beings, of kings, great ministers and the rest.

Apart from that, he may also have the conditions for enjoyment: clothing, wealth, food, things to eat, things to drink, jewels, bracelets, ornaments for the body, gold, silver, beryl, coral, crystal, agate, pearls, amragarbha stones, precious gems, elephants, horses, carriages, servants, laborers, wives and maidservants as well; springs, bathing pools, fine jeweled pavilions, and all the various kinds of articles of enjoyment. Yet though he has them, that bodhisattva has no conceit or pride about them.

Thus he is skilled in every art, he possesses hundreds of thousands of treasuries of jewels, he has elephants, horses and carriages, he has countless lovely young women, he has fine pavilions, and he has whatever pleasures of the senses there are, springs, bathing pools and the like. Yet he is not attached to them. He always delights in having few desires and in contentment, and he longs to dwell peacefully in meditative concentration in remote wildernesses, on mountains, in groves, and at the foot of trees. Though he talks and converses with many people, his mind always enters the door of the antidotes. Though he behaves in keeping with the conduct of ordinary people, he gives away precious jewels for the benefit of sentient beings, and he does nothing that harms sentient beings or does them no good. When those who are poor, destitute and wretched come to the bodhisattva to beg, the bodhisattva gives them whatever they wish.

Some sentient beings, inclined toward the Dharma of the buddhas and having faith in it, serve and wait upon the bodhisattva in order to honor him and make offerings to him. They do the work of washing his feet, or of washing and massaging his body; or they sprinkle water and sweep; or they provide tooth sticks and bathing water; or they do the work of spreading his bedding and shaking out his bedclothes; or they light the lamps at dusk and at dawn; or in the early morning and the forenoon they offer foods, pana, pushena and khadane; and they prepare drinks such as yulishi, a drink made from the fruit of the bulashaka, grape juice, and black sugar water. If, with such provisions, they make offerings to the bodhisattva for seven days and up to ninety days because they long for the Dharma, at that time the bodhisattva has no feeling of delight at that offering of provisions. Why is that? Because for a long time, for countless eons, the bodhisattva has sought the Dharma of the buddhas; because he has no partiality, favoring some and slighting others, toward any sentient being; because he possesses a mind of loving-kindness and compassion; and because he abides in a mind of equanimity.

Therefore at certain times he becomes a wheel-turning king, and he always works for the benefit of sentient beings through the Dharma of the ten virtues. Those sentient beings, for their part, rejoice and act in accordance with the king's wishes, and so when they die they are endowed with the supreme and excellent enjoyment of the five sense pleasures in the worlds of gods and humans, and they are born into great and high families. Just as they wish, they lie down, rise and go about without harm. Wherever they go they have horses to ride and carriages, and in pleasure groves they play at games and music and enjoy themselves. Though they enjoy excellent food and drink and every delight, in the end they meet with impermanence. Afflicted by old age and sickness, they die, and the family of the household, tormented by grief, cry aloud and weep. Some beat their heads with their palms; some tear out their own hair, shed tears and roll on the ground. Then they lay the dead on a litter or a cart and carry them to the charnel ground. And having carried them there, the family come back home tormented by grief. Some faint and fall to the ground; some fall ill because of it; some lose their wits and become deranged; some die before their time. Yet even though they do all this, it does no good to the living and greatly harms the dead.

At that time, on that occasion, the wheel-turning king was traveling through the land with his retinue, and when he saw sentient beings afflicted by grief, a feeling of tender compassion arose in him and he said this: "One who is called a king rules over many beings by his power over the land.
