Even if, having thus tamed beings by the path of the ten virtues, they have obtained the result, the supreme and excellent five sense pleasures, still, if they are not freed from the terror of birth, old age, sickness, death and impermanence, then although I rule the realm by the upright Dharma, it is of no benefit. If it is thus of no benefit to beings, how can one be called a great wheel-turning king? How can one be named a father of great loving-kindness, or a great king of physicians, or a great guide and teacher? One called a great guide and teacher leads onto the right path and shows the path to nirvāṇa, bringing about the attainment of the supreme bliss of the unconditioned. Our name and our conduct do not match.

"It is like this: a man tormented by thirst and on the verge of losing his life runs here and there in search of water. He comes upon a dry well, great joy arises in him, and he thinks, 'Now this body of mine has come back from death. Why? Had I not found water, this life of mine would not have been far from death. But now that I have found a good well, I shall surely find a pool of cool, clean water, quench my thirst and be satisfied, and my life will be saved.' Thinking this, he hurried as fast as he could to the well. When he reached the well, he took off the clothes he was wearing, hid them to one side, and, wanting water, climbed down into the well.

When he reached the bottom of the well, there was no water. All he saw were venomous snakes, centipedes, scorpions, lizards, frogs, potsherds and the like, gravel, sticks and matted grass. Then the man, worn out by thirst, found no water as he had first hoped, and he was bitten by many venomous creatures. He meant to climb back out of the well, but the well was old and ruined, and as deep as an arrow's flight. Inside it there was no ladder, no rope or cord, not even a tree to climb. He tried to leap up from below, but he was so worn out and weak that he could not jump high enough. He fell back to the bottom, and the venomous snakes and the rest bit him and sucked at him. While his life was not quite cut off, he said this: "Had I known before that there was no water in this well, I would not have dared even to look at it, let alone climb down into it. Now, because of this well, I am undergoing the suffering of suffering." So he lamented.

In the same way, the wheel-turning king saw that when one ordinary person died, the family and relatives of the household were tormented by the suffering of separation from someone dear, and he said this: "This body of mine is like that dry well. It has only the name of a well, but there is no water in it. So although there is a going in, there is no gaining anything, and one undergoes the suffering of losing body and life. Now I wield the power of a wheel-turning king and possess the seven precious things. I tame beings through the actions of the ten virtues and rule the realm by the upright Dharma. I have established many people in the higher realms of gods and humans and let them enjoy the supreme and excellent qualities of the five sense pleasures. Yet they cannot be freed from the suffering of birth, old age, sickness and death, of separation from those dear to them, and of meeting with hated enemies. That they weep and wail over one another is my fault; it is not the fault of sentient beings. Why is that? Because I have no Dharma that transcends the world, which would bring benefit to many beings. Though they seek from me the Dharma of virtue and long for perfect happiness, they cannot cross the ocean of suffering."

Then the wheel-turning king also thought, "Now this body of mine is like an ignorant, deluded donor, for example."

At that time, on another occasion, no rain fell for seven years, and a great drought came about. The trees and plants withered, and grain became very scarce. A great famine arose, and people seized and ate one another. They ate one another's flesh and blood. Some parents ate the flesh and blood of their children; some children ate the flesh of their parents. Parents, brothers and sisters, husbands and wives, sons and daughters all ate one another's flesh.

At that time a great donor was walking about and strolling here and there, and he saw many sentient beings starving, their strength gone, emaciated and dried up, feeble and of poor color, their hair matted, their bodies blackened and gaunt, carrying human corpses on their shoulders as loads. Some carried human heads, hands, feet, limb joints, forearms, shoulders, joints, backs, thighs and calves, every major and minor limb. He saw others carrying entrails: livers, spleens, lungs, intestines and rectums. When the donor saw such things, he asked them, "What are these things you carry as loads?"

They said, "These things we carry as loads are human corpses: human heads, hands and feet, thighs and calves, every major and minor limb."

He asked, "Why do you need these human heads and limbs, major and minor, that you are carrying? What will you do with them?"

They said, "Don't you know? A great famine has come and grain has become very scarce. Everyone is starving, and now people eat one another. So these loads we carry are our food."

When the great donor heard them say this, he was terrified. His hair stood on end, he trembled and fell to the ground. Cold water was sprinkled on his face, and after a long while he came back to his senses and asked, "Well then, if this is your food, whose flesh is it?"

They cried out loudly and wept, and said, "It cannot be told to the donor. Alas, how wretched we are! Alas, how wretched we are! Alas, alas, yet it is not right not to speak to you plainly. Of these things we carry, some is the flesh of a father, some the flesh of a mother, some the flesh of a child, some the flesh of a brother or sister, and some the flesh of relatives and friends." So each of those starving people spoke plainly to the householder: "Because we are so terribly starved, we have come to eat the flesh of one another's kin."

Then the donor, drawing a deep breath, said this to the starving people: "From this day on, do not eat the flesh of one another's kin. Whatever requisites you need, clothing to wear, food, drink, medicine for sickness, whatever requisites you need, I will give you all of it. In seven days, come to my door, and I will satisfy you as you wish with clothing, food, medicine for sickness, whatever you desire." When he had told them this, they were overjoyed and praised him, saying, "Excellent, excellent! How wonderful!"

Then the donor went back home and gathered his wife, his children, his servants and his laborers. With a joyful countenance, and with gentle, pleasing and sweet words, he instructed his family, servants and laborers, saying, "Understand this, and listen well to what I say. Do you know that because of the great drought a famine has arisen, everyone is starving, and countless people have died? In my house all the storehouses are full to overflowing, and there is countless grain. So let us make merit without letting the time slip by."

When the whole family heard him instruct them in this way, they said, "Excellent, excellent! Even giving away our lives and bodies we would not begrudge, so what need is there to speak of storehouses, wealth and food?"

Then the donor too was overjoyed and said, "How good it is that you are truly my companions for attaining unsurpassed awakening! Excellent!"

"Each of you, too, whatever has to be done, whatever has to be prepared, do at once what is needed for the cooking, and quickly accomplish what needs to be accomplished, so that in seven days from now it will certainly all be ready."

Then the donor, having given each of them their instructions, went outside the house. He looked for a place where the ground was level, spacious and pleasant, and at once he had the ground cleaned, cleared of litter, earth and stones, and swept, and he had seats arranged. Having thus prepared a place where many could gather, a ground for giving gifts, he loaded food onto five hundred elephants and had it brought to the place of giving. The food was heaped up like mountains, and curd was gathered like lakes. Butter, oil, flatbreads and all sorts of foods with a hundred flavors were prepared in great abundance. Besides these, he also prepared clothing, bracelets, garlands, all kinds of ornaments for the body, elephants, horses, the seven precious things and more, everything in perfect abundance.

When seven days had passed, at daybreak on the seventh day, bells and drums were struck and conches blown, and it was proclaimed in a loud voice, "Everyone, gather and come to this place of giving!"

At that time, when everyone heard the call, a feeling of great joy arose in them, as though by the blessing of a noble one, and all came to the place where the gifts were being given. They took, just as they wished, clothing, bedding, food and drink, all kinds of ornaments for the body, many medicines for sickness, elephants, horses, the seven precious things and whatever else they desired, and between them they carried off every last thing there was to give.

Then the donor, delighted, came back home and, together with his wife and children, enjoyed the five sense pleasures as they pleased.

About seven days later, people said, "Those who ate the food when that donor gave away clothing, food, drink and so on have been poisoned, and all of them have died." Because they had died in this way, everyone said, "How astonishing, how astonishing!" So it was proclaimed that although the great donor, out of compassion for all, had satisfied them with food and the rest just as they wished and had kept their bodies and lives alive, afterward, before many days had passed, they had been poisoned and all had died.

At that time, when the great donor heard what had happened, he became dejected and unhappy, and he asked his wife, his children and the others, "How did you prepare your food, that deadly poison got into it?"

His wife, children, servants and laborers said, "There cannot have been any poison. Look carefully into where this poison came from."

Then the donor, looking after the household, went round from place to place and saw a well whose top had been covered over and concealed. He asked, "Why has this well been covered?"

He was told, "This well, householder, was dug when you were a small child and were raising three venomous snakes, and the three venomous snakes were put into it. This well is the well where the venomous snakes live. Its water is poisonous too, so whoever drinks this water falls sick and dies."

When the donor heard these words, he asked the laborers, "When you were cooking the food the other day, did you not draw water from this well and cook the food with it?"

The laborers said, "Because we were cooking so much food we were in a hurry, so we drew this water as well and used it to cook the food."

The householder was unhappy and said, "How astonishing! I am like someone deluded and without wisdom. Why did I raise venomous snakes and dig a poisonous well?" Filled with remorse, he instructed his wife and children, "Fill in the well of the three venomous snakes at once! And I myself will burn the three venomous snakes." When he had given this instruction, the laborers immediately filled in that well and did away with it.

Then, having done away with the well of the venomous snakes, the donor went out for a walk, and with one voice all said of those who had died from the donor's poisoned food, "This donor gave us poisoned food, and so we died before our time. Had we known this, it would have been better not to go there." So remorse arose in them.

In the same way, that wheel-turning king tamed sentient beings by means of the ten virtues, and those beings were born in the higher realms of gods and humans and enjoyed the five sense pleasures to their hearts' content; yet that they were not freed from birth, old age, sickness and death is just like that.

Then the wheel-turning king at once made a vow: "I shall accomplish the unsurpassed Dharma of the buddhas, the Dharma that transcends the world, and sentient beings too shall read it, recite it, inquire into it and make themselves familiar with it, so that they completely abandon the cycle of birth and death, saṃsāra, and attain nirvāṇa."

At that time the wheel-turning king, in order to seek the Dharma of the buddhas, had it proclaimed everywhere throughout Jambudvīpa, "Who knows the Dharma of the buddhas?" But although the wheel-turning king, longing for the Dharma, had it proclaimed everywhere, no one who knew it appeared. When the proclamation reached a small border land, it was said that there was a brahmin there who knew the Dharma of the buddhas.

So he sent a messenger to that brahmin. The messenger went to where the brahmin was and asked, "Venerable sir, do you know the Dharma of the buddhas?" and he said, "I know it." Then the messenger bowed his head at the brahmin's feet and said, "Venerable sir, the wheel-turning king has sent me to invite you. Please come to where the wheel-turning king is."

Then the brahmin came there, and the wheel-turning king went out a long way to meet him. He bowed his head at his feet and greeted him, asking, "Did you travel well? Were you not troubled on the road? Are you not weary?" Then he led him into the palace and seated him on the royal throne in the middle of the palace. The brahmin sat down on the seat with his legs crossed. When the brahmin was comfortably seated, the king offered him all the requisites he needed as gifts, and then, joining his palms, he asked the brahmin, "Great teacher, do you know the Dharma of the buddhas?" The brahmin said, "I know the Dharma of the buddhas."

Then the king said to the brahmin, "Great teacher, please teach it to me and explain it."

The brahmin said, "King, you are very foolish. When I studied the Dharma of the buddhas, I mastered it only after long undergoing suffering and hardship. And now does the king expect to hear it for nothing?"

The king said, "Great teacher, what then do you want?"

The brahmin said, "Make offerings to me."

The king said, "Great teacher, what offerings do you want? Do you want clothing, or bedding, or food, or drink? Or do you want gold, or silver, or jewels?"

The brahmin said, "I do not want offerings like those."

The king said, "If you do not want those offerings, then do you want elephants, or horses, or carriages? Or do you want a country, or a city, or wives, or children? Do you want musicians, or singers, or dancers?"

The brahmin said, "I want none of these. If you cut a thousand wounds in your body, pour butter into the wounds, set wicks in them, and make offerings to me with a thousand burning lamps, then I will teach and explain the Dharma of the buddhas. If you cannot do that, I shall get up and go." Having said this, before the king had answered for even a moment, he got down from the throne.

Then the king caught him in his arms and said, "Great teacher, please wait a little. Because my understanding is weak and my merit small, let me think a little, and I will make the offering."

At that time the wheel-turning king thought, "Since beginningless time this body of mine has undergone death countless times, yet it has never died for the sake of the Dharma. And now this body of mine too will in the end perish and rot, so it is of no use. Now the time has come." Having thought this, he said to the brahmin teacher, "Great teacher, I will make the offering as you wish."

Then the king went into the palace and said to the queens, "Now we are to be parted."

When the queens heard him say this, they were frightened and trembled, and not knowing what was wrong, they said, "King, what are you now thinking of doing?"

The king said, "I am thinking of setting a thousand lamps in this body of mine and making an offering to the brahmin teacher."

When the queens heard him say this, they rolled on the ground, wailed and fainted. After a long while they came back to their senses. "Great king, in the world nothing is dearer than one's own body. Fearing that harm may come to it, one takes great care to nourish it. Why then would you injure and harm your own body? The king is endowed with a wise mind; has he now become deranged? Or has he been possessed by a hungry ghost? What is wrong?"

The king said, "It is none of these."

"Then why would you do such harm and think of making offerings to the brahmin?"

The king said, "I long for the Dharma of the buddhas, and I mean to work for the benefit of all sentient beings."

"If you are thinking of all sentient beings, then why are you casting us aside?"

The king said, "In the world, all who are dear and beloved are in the end parted. Therefore I shall make the offering of my body, intending to light the lamp of great wisdom in the dark house of yourselves and all sentient beings, to illuminate the darkness of ignorance that circles through birth and death, to cut the knots of the afflictions that bind on every side, to free you from the faults of saṃsāra, and to establish you in the state of nirvāṇa. So do not hinder me or put obstacles in my way."

When the queens heard him instruct them in this way, they were struck speechless as though choked, then cried out aloud, wept, and tore their own hair. "We have no merit," they said. "Because we are ill-starred, we have lost our lord while we are still alive."

That king had five hundred princes, all handsome and lovely, sincere and endowed with wise minds, perfectly possessed of the marks of a man. The king cherished and loved them as his own eyes.

At that time the king said to the princes, "Now that I mean to perform an act of offering, I fear my body and life will be put at risk, and I think I shall be parted from you. Take charge of the government of the realm and the welfare of the subjects in order of seniority." So he instructed them.

When the princes heard him instruct them in this way, they were tormented by suffering as if the limbs and veins of their bodies had been cut, and like a man choking who can neither cough up nor swallow, they said to the king, "Why do you abandon us and leave us without a refuge?" Some embraced the king around his neck; some held his hands and feet, crying aloud and weeping. "Alas, alas! Why must we be left without protector or refuge?" they said.

Then, to give the princes heart, the king told them, "In the world, all who are dear and beloved are in the end parted."

Though they heard him say this, their hearts clung to him, and unable to bear the parting, they said, "Please grant us one request. Let us offer our bodies and lives to the king, and with them, please make the offering to the brahmin teacher."

The king said, "My sons, you are still young and your strength has not yet developed, so you could not endure such an offering. As for me, I sought out the teacher from far away, and it would not be right to go against the intention with which I promised to make the offering. A dutiful son does not go against his father's wishes; will you go against mine?"

When the princes heard him say this, they cried out and wailed, so that even the gods of the land were frightened, and like mountains toppling, they all threw themselves down upon the ground.

At that time all the lesser kings of the border lands too paid homage to the king. Then he went to where the great teacher was within the palace. When he had come before him, he took off all the ornaments of his body, removed his clothes as well, laid them to one side, and sat with his body held upright. As soon as he had sat down, he gave instructions to the lesser kings, the ministers, the five hundred princes and the twenty thousand queens.

He said, "Which of you is able to cut a thousand wounds in my body?"

The queens, the princes, the ministers and the people of the lesser kings said with one voice, "Even if we were to take a knife to our own bodies and gouge out our eyes, we would not dare to make a wound in the body of our sovereign king."

Then the king became unhappy and said, "Now I am left all alone. Among all this great retinue, is there not a single person who will be my companion?"

At that time the king had an executioner of low caste, by nature wrathful and fearsome to all, and he summoned him. The executioner came. When the executioner had come, he said to the princes, "Do not grieve. I have a way to make sure the great king's aim does not succeed. And if it does not succeed, you will simply keep the king's rule as before." When the princes heard him say these words, a feeling of great joy arose in them.

Then the executioner came before the king and said, "Great king, what are you now thinking of doing?"

The king said, "I mean to set a thousand lamps in my body and make an offering to the great teacher, the brahmin."

The executioner said, "In that case, I can cut your body."

When the king heard him say this, he was filled with great joy and said to the executioner, "You are truly my companion in accomplishing unsurpassed awakening."

Then the executioner, standing before the king, said to him in a loud, sobbing voice, "Does the king know? By the law for putting people to death, first the head must be cut off: the neck is chopped through, the hands and feet are severed, and the sinews and tendons are pulled out as well. Great king, can you bear the suffering of such pain?"

When the king heard him say this, he was filled with great joy. Then the executioner took up a knife shaped like an ox's tongue and, without delay, cut a full thousand wounds in the king's body. The executioner had thought that the king's resolve would waver, but since his resolve did not waver, he threw the knife on the ground and fled.

Then into the thousand wounds cut in the great king's body melted butter was poured, wicks of fine cotton were set, and a thousand lamps were placed.

At that time, when the brahmin, the great teacher, saw this done, he thought, "If I do not explain the Dharma quickly now, there is no telling whether his body and life will survive or not. If his life is not spared, who will hear the Dharma?" Having thought this, he said to the king, "Because, longing to hear the Dharma of the buddhas, you have performed this difficult deed with heroic diligence, listen well and keep it firmly in mind. Now I shall teach the king the Dharma of the buddhas."

When the king heard him say this, he was filled with great joy. Just as a dutiful son is deeply unhappy when his parent dies, and tormented by suffering, and then, when the parent comes back to life, that son is overjoyed, so too was the king overjoyed.

Then the brahmin said to the king:

"Where there is birth, there will be death.
This cessation is happiness."

So he taught this half verse.

When the king heard the sublime Dharma taught, he was filled with great joy, and he instructed the princes and ministers: "If you have any love and affection for me, keep this Dharma in mind without forgetting it, and in every region, wherever there are places where many gather, in villages, towns and marketplaces, proclaim this to the many beings: 'The great wheel-turning king, in order to draw out the many sentient beings who are sunk in the ocean of suffering, and having given rise to great compassion for all sentient beings, set a thousand lamps in his body and heard this half verse. So that this great king's loving-kindness and compassion may be fulfilled, all should write it down, recite it, learn it by heart, inquire into it and make themselves familiar with it, reflect on its meaning, and diligently practice it as it teaches.'"

At that time, when many people heard this proclamation, they were overjoyed and delighted, and all with one voice praised the king in great joy: "The great king is truly our father of great compassion! For the sake of all sentient beings he has done what is extremely hard to do." So all extolled him. All of them wrote it down on paper, silk, bark, wood and stone, and learned it by heart. They also wrote it up in places where many people gather, so that all might see it, and they inspired them to give rise to the mind set on unsurpassed awakening.

At that time the light from the great king's setting a thousand lamps in his body and making an offering to the brahmin, the great teacher, illuminated the world systems of the buddha fields in the ten directions. From the light of those lamps, too, the sound of the Dharma of the half verse resounded, and all gave rise to the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

The light of those lamps illuminated even the dwellings of the gods of the Thirty-Three in the heavens, and the light of the lamps outshone all the light of the gods. Because the gods of the Thirty-Three were completely bathed in the light of those lamps, they thought, "Through what cause, through what condition, has such a light appeared?" Looking with their divine eyes, they saw that in the world a great wheel-turning king had given rise to compassion and, for the sake of all beings, had set a thousand lamps in his body and made an offering to a brahmin, a great teacher.

A feeling of great joy arose in those gods. They descended to the world, took on the bodies of ordinary men, went to where the king was, and said to the great king, "Great king, what is the use of setting a thousand lamps in your body, doing something so extremely hard to do, just to hear half a verse?"

The king said, "Son of good family, it is in order to lead all sentient beings to give rise to the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening."

Then that emanated man changed back into the body of Śakra, lit up everything with the light of his body, and said this to the great king: "Great king, by making such an offering as this, do you wish to be king of the gods? Do you wish to be king of the māras? Do you wish to be king of the Brahmās?"

The universal monarch said to Śakra, "Lord of the gods, I do not wish for any of the enjoyments of gods or humans. I wish for unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. I wish to establish all ordinary beings who are without happiness in a state of happiness, to free those who are not free, to bring across those who have not crossed over, and to bring those who have not attained awakening to attain it."

Śakra said, "Great king, you are an utter fool. Unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening is attained only by practicing what is extremely hard to practice for a very long time. How then would you attain unsurpassed awakening?"

The king said to Śakra, "Even if a blazing iron wheel were to spin ceaselessly on the crown of my head, I would not turn back from unsurpassed awakening because of that much suffering."

Śakra said, "Who would believe you when you say such words?"

Then the universal monarch said to Śakra, "If I do not wish for unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, and I have deceived you, lord of the gods, then may these wounds on my body never heal. If that is not so, may my blood turn into milk, and may my wounds be healed." The moment he made this vow, his wounds were healed.

Śakra, lord of the gods, praised the king, saying, "Excellent, excellent! Great king, you are truly endowed with great compassion; this is the conduct of great compassion. Since by practicing such hard practices you will before long attain unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, when you attain unsurpassed awakening, may you first act for my benefit." Having said this, Śakra, lord of the gods, lit up the whole of the king's body with the light rays of his own body, and together with a retinue of a hundred thousand gods he gave rise to the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

When the five hundred princes saw that the wounds on the body of their father the king had thus healed and that he was as he had been before, they were overjoyed. They bowed their heads at the king's feet, sat down to one side, bowed with joined palms, and said in unison, "Such a deed is a great wonder. Our father the king truly has great compassion for all beings." So they praised him.

The king said, "Sons, if you truly honor me, all of you give rise to the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening." When he had commanded them thus, the princes gave rise to the mind set on perfect and complete awakening. Likewise his twenty thousand queens and some hundred thousand palace attendants also gave rise to the mind set on awakening.

At that time, beings dwelling in that assembly as numerous as the sands of seventeen Ganges rivers gave rise to the mind of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. Countless gods, nāgas, yakṣas, gandharvas, asuras, garuḍas, kinnaras, mahoragas, humans, nonhumans and others, having seen and heard all this, every one of them gave rise to the mind set on awakening, and departed overjoyed.

This concludes the third chapter, called "The Antidote," from The Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha Repays Kindness.

At that time, in the circle of that assembly, the bodhisattva great being King of Joy rose from his seat, arranged his upper robe over one shoulder, placed his right knee on the ground, bowed with joined palms toward the Blessed One, and said to the Blessed One, "How does a bodhisattva know kindness? How does he repay kindness?"

When he had asked this, the Blessed One said to the bodhisattva King of Joy, "Son of good family, listen well. For a bodhisattva great being, to know kindness is to give rise to the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. To repay kindness is to lead all other beings to give rise to the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening."

He asked, "As for what is called giving rise to the mind set on awakening, how does one give rise to that mind? Through the condition of what thing does a bodhisattva come to give rise to the mind set on awakening?"

"Son of good family, when a bodhisattva great being first gives rise to the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, he makes this great pledge: 'When I attain unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, I vow that, for the benefit and happiness of all beings, I will surely establish all beings in great nirvāṇa.' Further, he should vow to bring all beings to complete the Perfection of Wisdom.

"That is called accomplishing one's own benefit and the benefit of others. Therefore, having first given rise to the mind set on awakening, he is called one who possesses the cause of awakening. He is called one who possesses the cause of beings. He is called one who possesses the cause of the true goal. He is called one who possesses the thirty-seven factors conducive to awakening. He is called the basis that gathers all virtuous qualities. Therefore a bodhisattva is called virtuous. He is called greatly virtuous, and he is also called the root of virtue of all beings. He is called the one who purifies the actions of body, speech and mind of all beings. Because he has purified the three evil kinds of action, no aspiration in the world surpasses giving rise to the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening: it is the supreme, holy, unsurpassed aspiration.

"When a bodhisattva great being first gives rise to the mind set on awakening, he should be endowed with five things. What are the five? Nature, conduct, sphere of activity, qualities, and generation."

"When a bodhisattva gives rise to the mind set on awakening in this way, he is called a bodhisattva great being, and he will without doubt attain unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening and come to practice the Great Vehicle. Therefore, by first giving rise to the mind set on awakening, he gathers all virtuous qualities. Having given rise to the mind set on awakening, a bodhisattva great being attains unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening in accordance with how he practices. If he does not give rise to the mind set on awakening, he will never attain awakening. Therefore, when he gives rise to the mind set on awakening, he obtains the foundation of unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

"When a bodhisattva great being sees beings tormented by suffering, a mind of love and compassion arises in him. Therefore a bodhisattva, with the mind of love and compassion as the cause, gives rise to the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. By the cause of giving rise to the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, he accomplishes the thirty-seven factors conducive to awakening. By the cause of accomplishing the thirty-seven factors conducive to awakening, he attains unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. Therefore giving rise to the mind set on awakening is called the foundation, or the root.

"By giving rise to the mind set on awakening he comes to practice the moral discipline of a bodhisattva; therefore giving rise to the mind set on awakening is called the root. It is also called the limb, the gathering, the sequence, and the seed.

"The bodhisattva's giving rise to the mind set on awakening may also be carried through to the end or not carried through to the end. When he brings it all the way to the attainment of unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, it is carried through to the end, since he never turns back or slackens. What is not carried through to the end turns back and declines. Turning back is of two kinds: turning back in the end, and not turning back in the end. Turning back in the end is being unable to accomplish or to practice earnestly whatever qualities keep one from turning back from the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. Not turning back in the end is, having given rise to the mind set on awakening, accomplishing and earnestly practicing whatever qualities there are.

"The conduct of a bodhisattva is of four kinds. When any son or daughter of good family hears of or sees the inconceivable things that buddhas or bodhisattvas do for others, at that time a mind of faith and aspiration arises, and he thinks, 'The things the buddhas and bodhisattvas do for others are inconceivable. Since those inconceivable things that the buddhas and bodhisattvas do for others can be attained, I shall attain unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.' Therefore he keeps awakening in mind single-pointedly and gives rise to the mind set on awakening.

"Some, though they have not seen the inconceivable things that buddhas and bodhisattvas do for others, merely by hearing the essence of what the buddhas and bodhisattvas have taught with intent, give rise to a mind of faith and aspiration; and with faith arisen, they give rise to the mind set on awakening in order to attain unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening and omniscience.

"Some, though they have neither seen the inconceivable things that buddhas and bodhisattvas do for others nor heard the Dharma they have taught with intent, see that the holy Dharma is about to perish. They think, 'This unsurpassed Dharma of the Buddha, which pacifies the countless afflictions of beings and serves their happiness and benefit, is kept abiding long and from declining only by the buddhas and bodhisattvas. Now I too shall give rise to the mind set on awakening, and in order to remove completely the afflictions of all beings, even if this body of mine undergoes great suffering, I shall make the Buddha's Dharma abide long in the world.' Thinking this, they give rise to the mind set on awakening.

"Some, though they do not see the Buddha's Dharma perishing, see that the beings of this evil age have extremely heavy afflictions: desire, aversion and delusion, shamelessness and lack of conscience, avarice, envy, anger, quarrelsomeness, lack of faith, doubt, laziness and other such afflictive states. They think, 'Because the age is so very evil, these beings do not practice virtue. If, the age being so evil, they cannot even give rise to the mind of the two vehicles, what need is there to speak of giving rise to the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening?' Thinking this, they give rise to the mind set on awakening. Having given rise to the mind set on awakening in this way, they strive to bring all beings too to give rise to the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening."

Then the bodhisattva King of Joy said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, if it is said that for a bodhisattva knowing kindness is giving rise oneself to the mind set on awakening, and that for a bodhisattva repaying kindness is leading other beings to give rise to the mind set on awakening, then when the Tathāgata was formerly wandering in saṃsāra, by what cause did he first give rise to the mind set on awakening?"

The Blessed One said, "Son of good family, in the past, for countless eons, while wandering in saṃsāra through birth and death, because of great afflicted actions of body, speech and mind I experienced the eight great hells, namely: the Ahaha hell, the Alala hell, the Achacha hell, the Copper Cauldron hell, the Great Copper Cauldron hell, the Black Stone hell, the Great Black Stone hell, and on to the Fire Chariot hell.

"At that time I had fallen into the Fire Chariot hell, and we were made to pull a chariot of fire, one or two of us together. The hell guardian Abang sat upon the chariot, eyes glaring and teeth bared, breathing out fire. Flames blazed from his mouth, his eyes, his ears and his nose, and smoke billowed forth. That guardian's body was enormous and black. Sitting cross-legged on the chariot, he held an iron club in his hand and beat me with it. Then, unable to bear the pain, I pulled the chariot with all the strength I had. One of my companions was weak and could not pull, so the hell guardian Abang took up an iron trident and drove it into his chest, and beat him with an iron hammer, so that blood fell like rain. Then that man, unable to bear the pain, let out great cries of agony; at times he called out, 'Father! Mother!' and at times he called, 'Wife! Child!' But it was of no use.

"At that time, seeing him undergo such great suffering, a mind of compassion arose in me. Through the condition of that great mind of compassion arising, I came to give rise to the mind set on awakening. Then, for the sake of the one undergoing that suffering, I pleaded with the hell guardian Abang, 'Have a little compassion for this man who is suffering!' The hell guardian flew into a rage and, with a mind of aversion arisen, took up the iron trident and drove it into my neck. I died there, and so I was freed from the Fire Chariot hell, and the unwholesome evil of some hundred eons was purified. In this way, because I gave rise to the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, I was freed from the Fire Chariot hell."

The Blessed One said to the King of Joy, "The one who pulled the fire chariot at that time is I myself now. Because I gave rise to the mind set on awakening, I swiftly attained buddhahood. Therefore, the grounds on which all sentient beings give rise to the mind set on awakening are not just one. For some it arises through the condition of compassion. For some it arises through the condition of aversion. For some it arises through the condition of generosity. For some it arises through the condition of avarice. For some it arises through the condition of joy. For some it arises through the condition of sorrow. For some it arises through the condition of being parted from what they desire. For some it arises through the condition of meeting a hated enemy. For some it arises through the condition of relying on a spiritual friend. For some it arises through the condition of an unwholesome friend. For some it arises through the condition of seeing a buddha. For some it arises through the condition of hearing the Dharma. Thus the ways in which sentient beings give rise to the mind set on awakening differ from one another in this way. King of Joy, the matter of how bodhisattva great beings know kindness and repay kindness should be understood in this way."

When this Dharma was taught, some hundreds of thousands of myriads of people gave rise to the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. Among that great assembly, some attained the fruit of stream entry, and so on up to the fruit of arhatship. Gods, nāgas, hungry ghosts, humans, nonhumans and all the rest set their minds on becoming śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. Greatly rejoicing at having heard the Dharma, they bowed their heads, circumambulated keeping him to their right, and departed.

This concludes the fourth chapter, called Giving Rise to the Mind Set on Awakening, from The Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha Repays Kindness.

The Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha Repays Kindness, fascicle three.

At that time, for the sake of his mother Māyā and the gods, the Tathāgata had gone to teach the Dharma to the gods for ninety days, and for those ninety days the people of Jambudvīpa did not know where the Tathāgata was staying. Even the great Maudgalyāyana, who was supreme in miraculous powers, searched in the ten directions, exerting his miraculous might, yet he did not find out. Even one such as the noble Near Protector, with his supreme divine eye, looked throughout the worlds of the great trichiliocosm in all ten directions, yet did not see him. Likewise, all the noble ones, down to the retinue of five hundred, searched, yet did not see him. Not seeing the Tathāgata in this way, they were unhappy, and their minds were tormented with sorrow.

At that time King Udayana, not seeing the Tathāgata, grew unhappy, and in order to remember the Tathāgata he made an image of the Tathāgata from oxhead sandalwood. He bowed before it and made offerings to it, and he made those offerings imagining that the Tathāgata was present before him in person.

At that time the great king summoned the six teachers of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) and asked, "Where is the Tathāgata staying now?" The six teachers said this to the king: "Great king, do you not know? The śramaṇa Gautama is a conjurer; that is what he is like. The illusions of a conjurer have no true reality. Our treatises explain, 'In a thousand or two thousand years an illusory conjurer will appear,' and so the śramaṇa Gautama is just that illusion."

Then the noble Near Protector went to where the great king was, and said this to the king: "Great king, do you know? Seven days from now the Tathāgata will come from the heaven of the Thirty-Three to Jambudvīpa."

When the king heard him say this, his mind was filled with great joy. He gathered the people of the land, and they sprinkled water and swept. They laid out incense and flowers, hung silk streamers, banners and parasols, and set out and arranged many kinds of food, music and every sort of offering article. The six teachers of the non-Buddhists came there and asked, "These many kinds of offering articles that you have set out and arranged like this, are they to invite a king? Or are they to invite a prince?" When the people said, "It is not that," they asked, "Then are you summoning a great minister? Are you summoning a brahmin or a householder? If not that, are you holding a feast for your relatives and friends?" The people said, "It is not that. We are inviting the Buddha, the Blessed One, to come."

The six teachers asked, "Who is this one called the Buddha?"

They answered, "He is the Omniscient One."

"And who is this one called the Omniscient One?"

They answered, "He is the father of loving-kindness and great compassion. Do you not know? The lineage of King Śuddhodana is high, noble and supreme. From the very beginning, from the first eon, a single son of the chief queen has carried on the line and become a universal monarch. In between, for two generations, they did not become universal monarchs, yet they were kings of Jambudvīpa. That king had three sons. The eldest was called King Śuddhodana; the middle one was called Droṇodana; and the youngest was called Amṛtodana. King Śuddhodana had two sons: the elder was called Siddhārtha and the younger son was called Nanda. King Droṇodana had two sons: the elder was called Devadatta and the younger son was Ānanda. King Amṛtodana had one daughter, a girl called Taste of Nectar.

"At that time the eldest of the brothers, Prince Siddhārtha, went out beyond the palace to stroll and to see the sights, and there he saw old age, sickness and death. He became unhappy and would not even eat his food. Moved by a mind of compassion, he fell to reflecting, and thought, 'Among those born as human beings there is no high or low: all who have taken on a body have such sickness and faults as these, and not a single one escapes them.' At night he fled the palace, and for six years he practiced austerities at the foot of the tree of awakening. He became the one called the Omniscient One: he awakened fully as one possessed of the wisdom of omniscience. Endowed with the ten powers, the four fearlessnesses and the eighteen unshared qualities, he became the one called the Omniscient One. Seven days after he was born, his mother Māyā passed away and was reborn among the gods of the heaven of the Thirty-Three. For ninety days he has been teaching the Dharma to his mother, and seven days from now he will come to Jambudvīpa."

When the six teachers of the non-Buddhists heard this said, jealousy arose in them, and tormented by a bitter, joyless sorrow, the six teachers and their followers sat down together and deliberated as follows: "If the śramaṇa Gautama comes here to Jambudvīpa, all the people will abandon us and make offerings to the śramaṇa Gautama instead, and we shall be ruined and destitute, and our very lives will be at stake."

When they had deliberated in this way, the six teachers of the non-Buddhists further thought, "Let us go quickly to the place where many people gather, and proclaim this among the crowd."

So they proclaimed: "Let everyone know this! The śramaṇa Gautama in truth knows nothing at all. As a little boy he left the palace of the Śākyas all alone, and sitting and sitting at the foot of the tree of awakening, he declares, 'I have attained the wisdom of omniscience.' Those words are false, not true. Why is that? King Agnidatta invited him to receive alms, and then gave him nothing but horse fodder and treated him badly; yet he did not know this beforehand and went there, so he is not omniscient. Moreover, when he asked Ānanda, 'How many days from now until the observance begins?' Ānanda said, 'Seven days remain.' And again, when he asked Ānanda, 'Why are so many birds chattering in the park?' Ānanda said, 'Because the many birds are looking for food.' Seven days after he was born his mother also died, and putting all this together, he is not omniscient.

"He is inauspicious and exceedingly sinful. How is that evident? As soon as he was born, his mother died. Again, a son ought to serve his father, yet instead of serving him he went off to the mountains, so he does not know kindness. His father the king took the queen Prajāpatī for him, yet he did not treat her as a wife, and made the queen Prajāpatī undergo great suffering, so he neither knows nor remembers kindness.

"The following of the śramaṇa Gautama makes no distinction between good and bad. Each of his five hundred disciples says, 'I am the foremost.' Since the preceptor himself has no proper system of Dharma, the disciples too have no system of Dharma or proper conduct. Even the lowliest, such as the monk Dravya, a man who lays out seats, says, 'I am the foremost of all.' Someone such as Śāriputra, upright of mind, great in wisdom and foremost in sharpness of intellect, is there; and the monk Panthaka, dull of wisdom and without understanding, is also admitted. The lowly nun Yaśodharā, of little desire, is also admitted. In Śrāvastī, the wicked courtesan Utpalavarṇā is also admitted. The lowly Cunda, a feeble and ignorant child, is also admitted. Among them, a decrepit old man called Subhadra, a hundred and twenty years old, is also admitted. The Śākyas, of great and noble lineage, are also admitted. And in Śrāvastī, a man of low caste, a sweeper who carries away refuse, is also admitted. Therefore the Dharma of the śramaṇa Gautama gathers a crowd with no distinction between good and bad, and it is neither worthy of offerings nor a proper object of honor and respect. Just as, when a great wind rises, all the leaves and petals of the trees are blown away together, so too is the Dharma of the Buddha. Just as many birds gather around food or scraps thrown out by people, so too is the following of the śramaṇa Gautama: they take whatever they get and eat it. So why do you all intend to make offerings to the śramaṇa Gautama?"

At that time, although the many people heard them say this, their minds, devoted and bowing to the Tathāgata as one tormented by thirst longs for water, did not waver, just as the great earth cannot be shaken.

The minds of the great crowd did not waver at the words spoken by the six teachers of the non-Buddhists; they became as firm as a vajra, neither increasing nor diminishing, and with devotion and faith, like one tormented by thirst, toward the Tathāgata, they waited until seven days had passed. Then the Tathāgata came back from the heaven of the Thirty-Three to Jambudvīpa. He was surrounded by countless hundreds of thousands of gods in his retinue, and illuminated everything with the light rays of the Tathāgata, and was honored with hundreds of immeasurable divine musical offerings. Gods, nāgas, yakṣas, gandharvas, asuras, garuḍas, kinnaras, mahoragas, and so on down to humans and nonhumans, all gathered, bowed to him and made offerings. At that time King Udayana too, together with his retinue, went out from afar to meet the Tathāgata, bowed his head at the feet of the Tathāgata, and sat down to one side.

Then the six teachers of the non-Buddhists also gathered to one side, and this thought came to them: "The one we call our bane has come here. Although we proclaimed what we did among the crowd, it did nothing to sway their belief. Now, even if we proclaim it amid the assembly of gods and humans, it will make no difference." Having thought this, the six teachers of the non-Buddhists, leading some hundred thousand followers, came into the midst of the great assembly and sat down to one side.

At that time a gandharva youth called Garland of Dharma, carrying a lute made of the seven precious substances, came to where the Tathāgata was, bowed his head at his feet, and sat down to one side. Seated there to one side, he played music accompanied by song, and the exceedingly sweet sound of that music delighted the minds of the whole assembly. Even śrāvakas such as Śāriputra, without realizing it, began to sway and move their bodies as if dancing, and even Mount Meru, king of mountains, trembled and shook.

At that time the Tathāgata entered the samādhi called Possessing Signs, and through the power of that samādhi the sound of the lute resounded throughout the worlds of the great trichiliocosm. From the sound of the lute came forth the sounds of suffering, emptiness, impermanence, impurity and no-self. And from that music all sentient beings heard resound the sounds of recognizing the kindness of the Tathāgata, of repaying that kindness, and of serving one's father and mother through immeasurable, countless eons. All the beings who heard that sound came to Jambudvīpa, to where the Buddha was, bowed their heads at his feet, and sat down to one side.

At that time, while the Tathāgata remained in samādhi, the great assembly sat gazing at the Tathāgata without blinking, and without saying a word. Then, in the very midst of the circle of the assembly, a stūpa made of the seven precious substances, adorned with countless victory banners and pennants, hung with hundreds of thousands of precious bells that rang out most sweetly when stirred by the wind, emerged from within the earth, rose into the sky, and remained there.

At that time the whole great assembly was filled with doubt about the precious stūpa that had come forth from the earth. They wondered, "Through what cause and what condition has a precious stūpa such as this come forth from the earth?" Śāriputra and the others searched with all the power of their understanding, yet did not comprehend it. Even the bodhisattva great beings dwelling in the Sahā world itself, up to Maitreya, did not comprehend it.

Then the six teachers of the non-Buddhists thought, "Through what cause and what condition has a precious stūpa such as this appeared? If someone else should ask us, and we do not know, how could we be called all-seeing and all-knowing? Now, what harm would there be if the śramaṇa Gautama quickly showed and explained it to the great assembly?"

Then the Blessed One arose from that samādhi, and Śakra, lord of the gods, together with the gods of the heaven of the Thirty-Three, set out a lion throne and spread it with divine cloth. Then the Tathāgata sat cross-legged on the lion throne, like Mount Meru, king of mountains, standing in the midst of the ocean.

At that time the bodhisattva Maitreya knew in his mind that the great assembly had fallen into doubt, and he too was in doubt. Not comprehending it, he rose from his seat, went over to where the Buddha was, bowed his head at his feet, and with palms joined in reverence he asked, "Blessed One, through what cause and what condition has a precious stūpa such as this appeared?"

The Blessed One said this to the bodhisattva Maitreya: "In the past, countless inconceivable eons ago and beyond, there appeared in the world a buddha called Vipaśyin, a tathāgata, an arhat, a perfectly complete buddha, perfect in knowledge and conduct, a sugata, knower of the world, unsurpassed guide of beings to be tamed, the Teacher and King, and he established countless hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of ordinary beings in unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

"After that buddha had passed into nirvāṇa, during the time when the semblance of the Dharma was being practiced, there was a land called Vārāṇasī. The great king of the land called Vārāṇasī was endowed with a wise intellect and always governed the land according to the righteous Dharma, so that the people too were happy and content. That great king held sway over sixty vassal kingdoms, and although he had eight hundred cities, the king had no son. Because he had no son, for twelve years, without weariness or discouragement, he worshipped and served the gods of the world and all the gods, and so the chief queen conceived a son. When ten months were complete, she gave birth to a son. That son was lovely and handsome and perfectly endowed with the marks. As soon as he was born, the great ministers and the kings and vassal kings of the borderlands all assembled and examined the prince's marks, and they found that the prince by nature did not know anger and was virtuous; so they named him Prince Forbearance. When Prince Forbearance grew up, he delighted in giving gifts and was endowed with a wise intellect, and great compassion arose in him for the many sentient beings."

"At that time that great king had six great ministers who were angry by nature, who lived by deceit and guile, and who imposed punishments on the innocent; no one wanted them, and no one held them dear. Those six great ministers, knowing that they themselves were at fault, were constantly jealous, and they disliked the prince.

"At that time the great king was struck by a grave illness and tormented by it. He grew weak and emaciated and came close to death. Prince Forbearance asked the ministers, 'Is my father the king gravely ill? Will his life not be cut short?' The ministers, angered, said to the prince, 'The king's life will not last long. Why is that? Because good medicine cannot be found, and therefore his life will not last long.' When the prince heard them say this, his mind became deeply unhappy; he fainted and fell to the ground.

"The six great ministers went to a secluded place and deliberated as follows. They thought and deliberated, 'Unless we do away with this Prince Forbearance, we shall never be at ease.' One minister said, 'If this prince has no fault at all, how can we do away with him?' Another minister said, 'I have a means of doing away with the prince.' Having said this, he went to where the prince was and said this to the prince: 'We have searched for medicine in the lands of the sixty vassal kingdoms of the borderlands and in the eight hundred cities, but we have not found it.'

"The prince said, 'What is needed for the king's medicine, that it cannot be found?'

"The minister said, 'Does the prince know? What is precious as medicine for the king is the eyeballs and the marrow of a person who, from the time of first being born until the last, has never known anger. If such medicine is found, the king will live; if it is not found, he will not live. But such a thing has not been found anywhere.'

"When the prince heard him say this, he was unhappy, and sorrow arose in his mind, and he said to the ministers, 'I myself am such a one now. Why is that? From the time I was first born until now, I have never experienced anger.'

"The minister said, 'Even if the prince is such a one, this is an extremely difficult thing to do. Why is that? Because in the world there is nothing one holds dearer than one's own body.'

"The prince said, 'Minister, it is not as you say. If my father the king could live and recover from his illness, I could give up even a hundred thousand bodies; it would not be hard. What need is there then to speak of giving up this impure body of mine?'

"The minister said, 'Such matters are for the prince to do as he sees fit.'

"Then Prince Forbearance was overjoyed, and joy arose in his mind. Thinking, 'If this medicine will cure my father the king's illness, the time has come to act quickly,' he went into the palace, came before his mother, bowed his head at his mother's feet, and with palms joined in reverence he said this to his mother: 'Now I am to divide up this body of mine as medicine for my father the king. Since I fear I may not survive, I have come to bow to you, mother, in farewell. Mother, please do not be unhappy.'

"When his mother heard him say this, she fainted, and lost all sense of direction. Terrified, like a choking person who can neither swallow nor vomit, she took the prince onto her lap and fell to the ground. Then cold water was sprinkled on her face, and after a long while she came to again. The prince said this to his mother once more: 'My father the king's life will not last long. I shall quickly accomplish this aim of mine and keep the king alive.'

"Then Prince Forbearance said to the ministers, the vassal kings and the many in the retinue, 'I am going; I bow to you in farewell.'

"At that time the great minister summoned a low-caste butcher, and he broke the prince's bones, extracted the marrow, and gouged out the eyeballs of both his eyes as well. Then those ministers pounded and compounded those medicines and gave them to the king. The great king ate the medicine, and his illness was cured.

"When the king's illness was cured, he said to the ministers, 'Where did you find such good medicine, which cured my illness and saved my life?'

"The ministers replied, 'This medicine was provided by Prince Forbearance. It was not we who found and prepared it.'

"When the king heard them say this, he was shocked and collapsed, and the hair on his body stood on end. Softly he asked the ministers, 'Where is Prince Forbearance now?'

"The minister replied, 'Prince Forbearance is now outside, having his wounds tended, and we fear he will not survive.'

"When the king heard these words, he let out a great cry, saying, 'Alas! Alas!' He flung himself to the ground, so that his body was covered with earth and dust, and said, 'I am truly shameless! Why did I eat my son's flesh as medicine?' Having said this, he set out to where his son was, but before he arrived the prince had passed away.

"The king, the queen, the ministers and the whole great retinue gathered around him, and his mother flung herself upon the prince's corpse and lamented, 'What evil did I do in the past, that this son of mine should undergo such suffering? It would have been easier had this body of mine been ground to dust. Why has this son of mine died?'

"Then the father, the king, and the vassal kings heaped up fragrant oxhead sandalwood and cremated the prince's corpse. They gathered the prince's bones and built a stūpa of the seven precious substances."

At that time the Blessed One said this to the bodhisattva Maitreya: "Sons of good family, understand this. If you think that the great king of Vārāṇasī at that time, on that occasion, was someone else, do not regard it so. The king at that time, on that occasion, is now my father, King Śuddhodana. If you think that the queen at that time, on that occasion, was someone else, do not regard it so: the queen at that time, on that occasion, is now my mother Māyā. Prince Forbearance at that time, on that occasion, is now I myself. For immeasurable, countless eons, in order to serve his father and mother, the bodhisattva gives clothing, food, drink, dwellings and bedding, and even the flesh, blood and marrow of his own body; and through that cause and that condition, he goes on until he attains full awakening."

"This precious stūpa that has now come forth from the earth is the one that, long ago, when I had given up my body, its marrow and even my life for the sake of my father and mother, was built in this very place and honored with offerings. Now that I have attained full awakening, it has emerged and become visible."

At that time, when the great assembly of countless gods, nāgas, yakṣas, humans and others heard him say this, joy and sorrow arose in them mingled together. All of them shed tears, and with one voice they praised and extolled the hundreds of thousands of qualities of the Tathāgata, and at one and the same time they gave rise to the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. In addition, some immeasurable hundreds of thousands of sentient beings set their minds on becoming śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. Besides these, immeasurable numbers of other people attained the fruit of stream entry, and so on up to the fruit of arhatship. Moreover, some immeasurable hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of bodhisattva great beings, before long, fully awakened in unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. Therefore the Tathāgata truly serves his father and mother.

Moreover, the bodhisattva knows the good qualities of his father and mother, and knows that it was through her former aspiration that his mother gave birth to the body of the Tathāgata. Thus, in order to fulfill the Tathāgata's aspiration, and because he did not wish his mother to bow down to him, she passed away.

At that time the whole great assembly said with one accord to his mother Māyā, "Excellent! Mother Māyā, it is most excellent that you gave birth to the Tathāgata, the teacher of gods and humans, who has no equal in the world."

Then the gandharva youth Garland of Dharma rose from his seat, draped his upper robe over one shoulder, placed his right knee on the ground, bowed with palms joined toward the Buddha, and said this to the Blessed One: "What merit did this mother Māyā make? Through what cause and what condition did she come to give birth to the Tathāgata?"

The Blessed One said, "Listen well, and bear it carefully in mind, and I shall explain it to you. In the past, countless inconceivable eons ago and beyond, there appeared in the world a buddha called Vipaśyin, a tathāgata, an arhat, a perfectly complete buddha, perfect in knowledge and conduct, a sugata, knower of the world, unsurpassed guide of beings to be tamed, the Teacher, the Victor.

"After the true Dharma of that buddha had declined, and even the period of practicing the semblance of the Dharma had declined, there was at that time, in the land called Vārāṇasī, a mountain called Abode of Noble Ones. On that mountain dwelt some hundred thousand of those called pratyekabuddhas. Countless sages endowed with the five superknowledges also dwelt on that mountain. Because so many noble ones and sages dwelt there, that mountain was named Abode of Noble Ones.

"On that mountain one sage dwelt on the southern side of the mountain, and one sage dwelt on the northern side. Between the dwellings of those two sages there was a spring, and beside that spring there was a fine flat boulder. Then, on one occasion, the sage who dwelt on the southern slope of the mountain washed his clothes and his feet on that boulder and went back to his dwelling. Not long afterward, a doe came to the spring to drink. After drinking from the spring, she drank the water in which the sage had washed his clothes on the boulder. After drinking the water from the washing of the clothes, she turned around, looked behind her, and licked her genitals.

"Then the doe conceived a child in her womb, and when the months were complete, since by the way of wild animals they give birth in the very place where they first conceived, she came to the edge of the spring, and there on the boulder, crying out and rolling about, she gave birth to a girl. At that time the sage of the southern slope heard the doe crying, and a mind of compassion arose in him. He went over toward the doe and saw that a girl had been born to the doe, and the doe was licking her. When the sage arrived, the doe abandoned the child and went off.

"Then the sage, seeing that the girl born of the doe was beautiful and lovely and endowed with the marks, wrapped her in a garment of grass out of compassion, took her up in his arms, and came back to his dwelling. He gathered fruits and berries and fed and raised her, and gradually she grew up, until she reached fourteen years of age. The sage, too, loved the girl dearly, and had her tend the fire constantly, without ever letting it go out. But one day she was lazy and the fire went out. The sage scolded her in words: 'From the time I first raised you until now, the fire has never gone out. Why did you let the fire die?' And he sent her to the sage dwelling on the northern slope of the mountain to fetch fire.

"Then the girl, as her father had instructed, went toward the northern slope of the mountain, and at every single step a lotus sprang up from beneath her feet. Following her footsteps, lotuses sprang up in an even row, and so she went to the northern slope of the mountain to fetch fire. At that time, when that sage saw that the girl was greatly endowed with merit and that lotuses sprang up from beneath her feet, he said this to the girl: 'If you want fire, circle this hut of mine seven times, with your steps even and at a proper distance, so that lotuses spring up beneath your steps.' When she had circled it seven times in this way, he instructed the girl again: 'If you want fire, circle once more, keeping it to your right, and then go, and I shall give you fire.' At that time, because the girl wanted the fire, she did as the sage instructed, took the fire, and went back.

"Not long after the girl had gone back, the king of the land of Vārāṇasī, with his great ministers and a retinue of some hundred thousand, came to that mountain to stroll and to hunt wild animals. Then the king of Vārāṇasī, riding his royal elephant, came to where the sage of the northern slope of the mountain was. When he arrived there and saw the sage's hut ringed all around with lotuses, a mind of great delight arose in him, and he praised the great sage, saying, 'Excellent, most excellent! Virtuous indeed is the sage endowed with great qualities, endowed with the qualities of teaching and guiding!'"

Then the sage said to the king, "Great king, you should know that these lotuses are not my own power." The king said, "Then who made them?" The sage replied, "Great king, the sage who dwells on the southern slope of the mountain has a daughter, born of a deer, fair of face and lovely, endowed with the marks of a human being, rare in the world, and wherever she walks lotuses spring up beneath her feet."

When the king heard him say this, his mind was filled with delight, and he went over to where the sage of the southern slope of the mountain was. When he arrived, he bowed his head at that sage's feet. The sage came out to the door, welcomed him, and asked, "Great king, having come from so far away, are you not very weary?" Then the great king spoke to the sage: "Venerable one, you have a daughter, and I wish to take her in marriage." The sage said, "Great king, I am poor and have only one daughter. She is young and knows nothing at all. Since she has been raised on the mountain from childhood, she understands nothing of the ways of human beings; she eats nothing but the roots of grasses and fruits. How could she be fit to be offered to the king? Moreover, this girl was born of a deer and is not fit to be offered." And so he went on, telling the king in detail the whole story of how it had come about.

The king said, "Even so, that is no matter. Show me where she is." The sage said, "She is in the hut right now." Then the great king went into the hut and looked at the girl, and a mind of great delight arose in him. He had the deer girl bathed in scented water, dressed her in priceless garments, adorned her with all kinds of jeweled ornaments, and set her upon the royal elephant. Then, surrounded by a retinue of some hundred thousand, with music playing, he returned to his own land.

At that time the deer girl, who since her birth had never seen so many people, was alarmed and afraid. Then the sage climbed to the top of a high mountain and gazed at the girl without blinking, thinking, "Now my daughter is going farther and farther away; once she is out of sight, I shall go back home." With tears streaming from his eyes he thought, "This daughter whom I raised has left me, knowing nothing at all." Then again he thought, "I shall not move from this place yet. Why is that? If my daughter should turn back to look at me and not see me, she would be unhappy and tormented by suffering. For now I shall stay right here." So he stayed on for a long while, watching until she was out of sight, but the girl never looked back.

Because the girl did not look back, anger arose in the sage, and he said, "Born of an animal after all, she did not even look back at me! I raised her and raised her from when she was little, and now that she has become a human being the king has carried her off, and I am left forlorn." Then he went into his hut and worked a spell, laying this curse on her by the mantra: "If the king casts her off, then let that be the end of it. But if he cherishes her and does not cast her off, may things not turn out as he wishes!"

At that time the great king of Vārāṇasī, having reached his palace, cherished her and gave her the name Queen Deer Lady. The lesser kings of the borderlands and the ministers gathered together and praised the king with pleasing words, and the king was filled with delight. Not long afterward she conceived a child in her womb, and the king himself waited upon the queen, providing her with bedding, food, drink and the like, all fine and soft. Then, when the ten months were complete, though he had hoped she would bear a son to carry on the king's lineage, when the months were complete she gave birth to a lotus. Through the power of the sage's spell the king became angry and said, "So it is true that she was born of an animal!" He removed her from the rank of queen and had the lotus thrown away.

Then, after a long time had passed, the king went with the ministers of Vārāṇasī to the pleasure garden to watch the spectacles and amuse themselves. Playing and making music, they set horses and elephants to fight, and had the wrestlers contend in strength. Among the wrestlers was one of the very greatest strength, and when he stamped his foot on the ground, the earth shook, and a lotus pond close by shook as well. Near that lotus pond there was a boulder, and beneath it lay a lotus, which tumbled into the pond. The lotus that fell into the pond was of the finest color, its hue clear and vivid, glowing with a shining light. When the king saw it, his mind was filled with delight, and he said to the ministers, "Never before have I seen a lotus like this!"

Then he gave the order, "Take that lotus out of the water," and sent someone to fetch it. That lotus had five hundred petals, and beneath each and every petal was seen a young boy, fair of face, beautiful, and surpassing ordinary people. The man who went to fetch the lotus, seeing such great wonders in it, said, "Great king, that lotus has five hundred petals, and beneath each petal is a son of the gods." When the king heard these words he was so startled that every hair of his body stood on end, and he said to the man, "Is this really true?" Wondering, "Is this the very lotus that Queen Deer Lady bore?" he asked the queen, "Where did you throw the lotus that you bore?" The queen answered, "Great king, I hid that lotus beneath a boulder on the bank of the pond." Then, convinced that it was indeed the one Queen Deer Lady had borne, he came back into the palace.

Having returned to the palace, he said to Queen Deer Lady, "I am deluded and without understanding; I could not tell the wise from the foolish. Thinking that what was said of the queen must be true, I went against the queen's heart, and I confess it." And he installed her once again as chief queen. Then the king, overjoyed, gathered together all the ministers, the brahmins, the lesser kings and the readers of marks, took the five hundred princes upon his lap, and had the readers of marks examine their marks, good and bad.

The readers of marks said, "These princes have come about through the king's merit and power. If they remain in the household life, the four borderlands will submit of their own accord, and even the gods will follow their lead. If they go forth, they will without doubt cut the root of birth and death, be freed from the river of desire, and cross to the far shore of the ocean of saṃsāra; they will be endowed with the three knowledges and the six superknowledges, and will attain the four fruits of the ascetic life."

When the king heard these words he was all the more delighted, and he gathered five hundred nurses to rear the five hundred princes. Then Queen Deer Lady said to the king, "Great king, there is no need to gather nurses from all over the realm. At present there are five hundred queens in the king's palace, and because I have sons, those queens are all jealous of me. Now if the king has each queen rear one prince, the princes will be reared."

The king said to the queen, "Since the five hundred queens are jealous of you, my queen, I would do as you wish even to the point of rebuking them, beating them, rejecting them, banishing them, or putting them to death. Why then would you take on something so very hard to bear toward those who have become your enemies, and think of giving my sons, more precious than anything between heaven and earth, to those queens?" At that time the five hundred queens heard her speak as she had, and they were overjoyed and said, "Queen Deer Lady has always granted us ease and happiness, and now she has given us her sons as well; we are overjoyed." At that time a retinue of some hundred thousand also heard these words, and a mind of great joy arose in them, and they generated the mind set on awakening.

Then the great king said to the queen, "This is a great wonder. I could not do as much as you." The queen said, "Through the force of jealousy, anger arises. As the antidote to wrongdoing one should cultivate patience, and as the antidote to wrath one must generate a mind of loving kindness. From the very beginning I have never quarreled with anyone over anything; the queens have only been harming and injuring themselves. It is like a man walking along at night who sees a log and takes it to be an enemy or a hungry ghost, and at once, terrified, he flees. Fleeing into the darkness, some fall from a high cliff into a chasm; some fall into water, or fire, or a heap of thorns, or a thicket, and their bodies are wounded and broken. So it is that, through the condition of a mistaken perception, they meet with such harm. In the same way, all sentient beings are born by themselves and die by themselves, like a silkworm, or a moth that flies into a flame; no one else brought it upon them. All faults arise from perceiving what is untrue. The queens too are like that, and so I take no notice of it, and have no quarrel at all with anyone."

The five hundred queens bowed to Queen Deer Lady, confessed their own wrongs, and served and attended upon Queen Deer Lady, regarding her as a mother or as a daughter. And they reared and cared for the princes as if they were their own sons.

Then the five hundred princes gradually grew up, and each prince had the might and strength of a thousand men. If any lesser kings of the borderlands rebelled and would not obey the king's command, there was no need for the fourfold army: the princes themselves would go out to attack and subdue them. At that time the land was peaceful and happy, and the guardian gods were pleased, so that wind and rain rose and fell in their proper seasons, and the people lived at leisure in comfort and happiness. The five hundred princes rode on fine elephants and played and amused themselves in the pleasure garden as they pleased, and their father and mother loved them, cherishing them like the apples of their eyes.

At that time the five hundred princes had grown up and come into their full strength. Later, on another occasion, they all gathered and sat on the bank of that lotus pond, and as they sat there, each one's reflection appeared in the water of the pond, and they said to one another, "It is like this: all phenomena are like an illusion, or a magical emanation, or a dream, or a reflection in water; there is nothing true or real in them. We too are now just like that. Though we are of great and noble lineage, live within the palace, and enjoy the five sense pleasures heedlessly, the years of our prime and our good looks cannot be relied upon. Whatever has come into being will perish; whatever is born will die. Youth and the prime of life do not last long, but turn to old age and decline. If one does not keep to moderation in food, one falls sick. Even if one lives a hundred years, in the end one must die." So the princes were overcome by a joyless sorrow.

Wanting neither food nor drink, they went back to the palace and said to their father the king, "The world is suffering; there is no happiness in it. Therefore, father, king, we ask to go forth." The king said to the princes, "Birth, aging, sickness and death are the same for everyone, so why should you alone grieve over them?" The princes replied, "We cannot bear the affliction of taking birth again and again after death, and of going on and on among the five destinies." Then the king, not opposing the princes' wishes, gave them leave to go forth.

The queen said to the princes, "When you have gone forth, do not abandon me and go far away. Behind the palace there is a pleasure garden that is very secluded, and its trees and groves are secluded too. Stay there, and I shall offer you the four requisites so that you will not be troubled by want." The princes went forth and, as the queen had asked, stayed in the pleasure garden behind the palace, and there every one of those princes, one after another, attained the awakening of a pratyekabuddha.
