In this way four hundred and ninety-nine princes in turn went forth and attained the fruit of solitary awakening. Then they came to the king's palace and said to their father the king and to their mother, "We have attained the aim for which we went forth." Then those monks rose up into the sky above. When they vanished in the east, they appeared in the west; when they vanished in the west, they appeared in the east; when they vanished in the south, they appeared in the north; when they vanished in the north, they appeared in the south. At times, with an immense body, they filled the whole expanse of space; at times, from one body they emanated countless bodies; at times, water streamed from the upper part of the body while fire blazed from the lower part. Having displayed many kinds of miracles such as these, they set their bodies ablaze and passed into nirvāṇa. Then Queen Deer Lady gathered their relics, built four hundred and ninety-nine stūpas in the pleasure garden behind the palace, and made offerings to them.

The youngest of the princes, too, attained the awakening of a pratyekabuddha after ninety days had passed, and he too displayed great miracles to his father and mother. Having displayed great miracles, he passed into nirvāṇa. Then Queen Deer Lady gathered his relics, built a stūpa, and made offerings to it.

At that time Queen Deer Lady made offerings every day to the stūpas of the five hundred pratyekabuddhas with the finest incense, the finest flowers, and all kinds of music, and before those stūpas she grieved, unhappy, and said, "I bore five hundred sons, and they went forth, yet not even one of them generated the mind set on unsurpassed awakening." And so she made this aspiration: "Whatever merit there is in my having made offerings to the five hundred pratyekabuddhas, building five hundred stūpas and making offerings to their relics, I dedicate to all sentient beings. In time to come, I do not wish to bear many sons who do not generate the mind set on unsurpassed awakening. Rather, may I bear a single son who generates the mind set on unsurpassed awakening, goes forth in that very life, and comes to possess the wisdom of omniscience!" Such was the aspiration she made.

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "If you should think that Queen Deer Lady at that time, on that occasion, was someone else, do not see it that way. Queen Deer Lady at that time, on that occasion, is none other than my present mother, Māyā. Because Queen Māyā made offerings in this way to the five hundred pratyekabuddhas and generated countless roots of virtue, she has now given birth to the body of the Tathāgata."

When this Dharma of the Buddha was taught, countless gods and humans attained the fruits, from the first fruit up to the fourth, and immeasurable numbers of sentient beings generated the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

Then Ānanda said to the Blessed One, "What deed did Queen Māyā do in the past, that she was born among animals, of an animal, and became the daughter of a deer?" The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Listen well. I shall unravel and explain to you the causes and conditions of the deeds Queen Māyā did in times past.

"Long ago in the past, countless, immeasurable eons ago, at that time, on that occasion, there appeared in the world a buddha, a blessed one called Vipaśyin, a tathāgata, an arhat, a perfectly and completely awakened buddha, perfect in knowledge and conduct, the Sugata, knower of the world, unsurpassed guide of persons to be tamed, the Teacher, the Victor. After he had worked for the benefit of sentient beings and passed into nirvāṇa, while the Dharma was being practiced in his semblance period, at that time, on that occasion, in the land called Vārāṇasī, a certain brahmin had an only daughter, and then that brahmin died. The brahmin's wife raised the girl, and the girl gradually grew up.

"Mother and daughter had an orchard of fruit trees, and the mother had the girl guard the orchard. She herself went in search of food, and whatever she found she brought back and ate together with her daughter. While she was always seeking food in this way, one day she was late and the usual hour passed, and the girl, tormented by hunger and thirst, became angry. 'Whatever has happened to my mother,' she raged, 'that she has not given me food until so late, and has left me hungry and thirsty like this? My mother is not even as good as an animal! Even animals, beasts of prey, deer and birds, remember that their young are hungry and thirsty and come to feed them at the proper time.' So she spoke.

"Not long after she had said this, her mother came bringing food. While they were eating, the girl saw a pratyekabuddha in the guise of an ascetic passing through the sky from the south toward the north. A joyful mind toward that monk arose in her; she rose from where she sat, joined her palms, bowed her head, and invited him down. She spread out a clean seat, honored him with fine flowers, and, sharing out some of that food, offered it to the monk. When the monk had finished his meal, he taught the sublime Dharma and filled her with delight and joy. At that time the girl made this aspiration: 'In time to come, may I meet with noble ones, and serve them, make offerings to them, and pay them homage! May I be fair of face and beautiful, great and powerful, and wherever I walk may lotuses spring up beneath my steps to bear up my feet!' Such was the aspiration she made."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "The girl of that time, of that occasion, was Queen Deer Lady. Because she offered a single meal of alms to a pratyekabuddha in this way and honored him with fine flowers, for five hundred lifetimes she was great and powerful, had clothing and food as she wished, and lotuses sprang up beneath her feet. Through the power of her aspiration she has now also met with five hundred pratyekabuddhas, and made offerings to them, served them, and paid them homage. But because at that time, ungrateful for her mother's kindness, she spoke one harsh word, saying her mother was like an animal, through the condition of that evil verbal deed she was born for five hundred lifetimes from the womb of a deer."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Every fault of whoever is born in the world comes from their own mouth; therefore one should guard one's verbal actions. Verbal action is worse than being burned by a blazing fire. Being burned by a blazing fire lasts for only one life, but the blazing fire of nonvirtuous verbal action burns for countless births. A blazing fire burns only worldly wealth, but the blazing fire of nonvirtuous speech burns up the seven noble riches. Therefore, Ānanda, the faults of all sentient beings come from the mouth; the mouth and tongue are called the weapons that cut down the body."

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, about a thousand laymen and laywomen, by guarding their actions, attained the first fruit. Countless gods and humans generated the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, and others generated the resolve for everything down to the awakening of a pratyekabuddha. The whole great assembly rejoiced at what the Blessed One had said, bowed to him, and departed.

At that time the Blessed One and Ānanda, having received alms in Rājagṛha, were on their way back, and outside the city there was a deep ravine. The people of the city of Rājagṛha carried all their excrement, urine and filth to that ravine, and when it rained, the rainwater all drained into it as well. At that time, in the foul water of that ravine, a creature shaped like a human being but with many hands and feet saw the Tathāgata coming from afar. It raised its head out of the water, gazed at the Tathāgata, and tears flowed from its eyes. When the Blessed One saw it, he was moved with compassion and saddened, and he went back to Vulture Peak. Then Ānanda spread out the Tathāgata's seat, and he sat down with his legs crossed.

Then Ānanda looked over the whole assembly, and knowing in his mind what the assembly was thinking, he said to the Blessed One, "Blessed One, that creature in the foul water just now: what did it do in the past that it was born in that foul water? How long has it been since it was born there? And when will it ever be freed from it?" The Blessed One said to Ānanda and the great assembly, "Listen well. I shall unravel it well and explain it to you.

"Ānanda, long ago in the past, a thousand countless eons ago, at that time, on that occasion, a buddha appeared in the world, worked for the benefit of sentient beings, and passed completely into nirvāṇa. After he had passed into nirvāṇa, while the Dharma was being practiced in its semblance period, a certain brahmin built a dwelling for the Saṅgha and offered it to the Saṅgha. At that time a certain patron offered butter and sesame oil to the Saṅgha, and some visiting monks also arrived there. At that time a certain person who served as steward saw the visiting monks arrive and, being stingy, hid the butter and sesame oil away and mixed them up, and did not give them to the visiting monks. The visiting monks asked the steward, 'The butter, sesame oil and honey that came in, will you not give them to us?' The steward said, 'You are visitors; we are the residents.' The visiting monks said, 'Was it not offered by the patron to the Saṅgha present here?' At that the one serving as steward became angry and fearsome, and spoke harsh words to the visiting monks: 'Instead of eating excrement and urine, you want butter and sesame oil from me?'

"Through the deed of speaking those harsh words, for ninety koṭis of eons he has been born continually in this foul water. The one serving as steward at that time, on that occasion, is that creature living in the foul water just now. Thus because he once spoke harsh words to the Saṅgha in time past, he has been born in that place for a hundred thousand lifetimes counted one by one."

The Blessed One said to his disciples, "Restrain and guard your verbal actions. The faults of speech are worse than a blazing fire. Since parents and the Saṅgha are worthy of praise and acclaim, speak gently to them and always be mindful of their kindness. What is called the Saṅgha is the field of merit that brings one beyond the three realms. Parents are the supreme, sublime field of merit within the three realms. Why is that? Within the Saṅgha are the four pairs and eight kinds of persons and the twelve noble ones; if one serves them one gains merit, and if one practices, awakening is accomplished. Parents keep a child in the womb for ten months, and once it is born, they leave it the dry and take the wet for themselves, and raise it with milk from the breast. After it is grown, they have it learn to be skilled in the sciences, and attend to it without ever missing the proper time. If the child goes forth, then by making merit it crosses the ocean of saṃsāra, of birth and death, and accomplishes its own aim and the benefit and happiness of all sentient beings. Therefore, Ānanda, parents and the Saṅgha are called the two fields of merit for all sentient beings. Through them as conditions, gods and humans alike come to attain the sublime fruit of liberation, passing beyond sorrow, and even accomplish buddhahood."

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, hundreds of thousands of immeasurable sentient beings, human and nonhuman, attained the fruits from the first up to the fourth. Some generated the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, and some set their minds on becoming śrāvakas or pratyekabuddhas. Each of them joined their palms, bowed to the Buddha, circumambulated him, and departed full of joy.

Furthermore, in the land of Vārāṇasī there was a brahmin minister who was very wealthy, with many riches and precious things: gold, silver, beryl, crystal, coral and agate, and many elephants, horses, cattle and sheep, fields and pastures, servants and workers. When he was eighty years old a son was born to him. The boy was fair of face, lovely and beautiful, endowed with all the marks of a fine human being, so his father and mother were overjoyed, and they called a reader of marks to examine his marks.

When the reader of marks had examined his marks, they named the boy Cunda and raised him. When he had reached seven years of age, his father and mother, out of their great love, gave him leave to go forth. So that he could go forth, they took him to the mountain called Īśvara, where the Tathāgata was staying. At that time the Tathāgata, with the fourfold assembly, the gods, nāgas, yakṣas and others at their head, was teaching the great assembly both the worldly sciences and treatises and the Dharma that transcends the world.

Then that brahmin said to the Blessed One, "Blessed One, I am old and decrepit, and I have a son whom I offer to the Blessed One. I beg you, in your great compassion, to protect him and accept this son of mine as your disciple." Then the Blessed One said, "Welcome!" and the boy's hair fell away of its own accord and the Dharma robes were on his body of themselves. He was encouraged and uplifted, a mind of delight and joy arose in him, and he attained the fruit of the path, coming to possess the three knowledges, the six superknowledges, and the eight liberations.

At that time Ānanda looked over the great assembly, and in order to resolve everyone's doubts he rose from his seat, set his robes in order, placed his upper robe over one shoulder, joined his palms, bowed, and said to the Blessed One, "What merit did the novice Cunda make in times past, that he has met the Blessed One here and so swiftly attained the fruit?" The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "It is not only in this present time. In times past too, the novice Cunda made supreme merit toward his parents and the Saṅgha, and so he met with a spiritual friend and attained the fruit of the path." Ānanda said, "Blessed One, please teach us that story." The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Listen well.

"Long ago in the past, a thousand immeasurable ages ago, a buddha, a blessed one called Vipaśyin, appeared in the world. Having worked for the benefit and happiness of gods and humans, he passed completely into nirvāṇa. After he had passed into nirvāṇa, there was a young monk who practiced the sublime Dharma. He was highly learned in the Mātṛkā, the Sūtras and the Vinaya, handsome and fine of form, endowed with the marks of a fine human being, and confident in teaching the Dharma. Because of the voice and the sweet words with which he spoke the Dharma, the kṣatriyas and brahmins were his friends, and they served him and made offerings to him.

"At that time there was also an old monk who was very ugly of face and not endowed with the marks of a fine human being, and whose voice was not pleasant, but who always praised the Three Jewels with words and chant. At that time the young monk, master of the three baskets, said to that monk with the unpleasant voice, 'Making such an unpleasant noise is like the howling of a wounded dog.' Then the old monk said to the young monk, 'What, do you not know me, that you speak harsh words to me?' The monk who was master of the three baskets said, 'I know you. You are the old monk called Mahala, who went forth in the sublime Dharma of the Buddha Vipaśyin. Why would I not know you?' The monk Mahala said, 'Now what was to be done has been done. The holy life has been lived. I shall not take up any further existence.'

"When the young monk, master of the three baskets, heard these words, his flesh crept and he was struck with alarm. Then the young monk lifted up the monk Mahala with his right hand, and from that hand Mahala sent forth a great light that illuminated the ten directions. Then the young monk, master of the three baskets, bowed his head at the old monk's feet, repented, and confessed and acknowledged his fault, saying, 'Veiled by the delusion of not knowing, I did not know that you were a noble one. May this evil deed done in ignorance not become a sin! In time to come, may I meet with a spiritual friend, meet with you, noble teacher, and bring my defilements to exhaustion!'"

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Because at that time, on that occasion, that young monk, master of the three baskets, spoke one coarse word to the old monk, for five hundred lifetimes he experienced the body of a dog."

When the Blessed One had said this, the great assembly exclaimed, "How wonderful, how wonderful! In this world there is no supreme venom that exceeds verbal action!" And the whole great assembly made this vow in verse:

Though a burning wheel of iron
Should whirl unceasing on my crown,
I would not count it suffering;
I shall never speak a harsh word.
Though a burning wheel of iron
Should whirl unceasing on my head,
I would not count it suffering;
I shall speak no harsh word to the noble.

The Blessed One said, "Because Śāriputra has become the spiritual friend of many sentient beings, six times through the day and night he surveys and watches over the beings of the five destinies with his divine eye, and when the time for taming someone has come, he acts for that one's benefit.

"At that time five hundred merchants were constantly traveling back and forth between the land of Magadha and this land. Since the road they traveled back and forth was very dangerous, one merchant kept a white dog. At that time a companion of that merchant cooked some meat in the evening and set it aside, and in the latter part of the night the dog stole it and ate it. The next day at dawn, when the merchant's companion went to eat the meat and could not find it, he was vexed and angry. He took up a weapon, cut off the dog's four legs, threw it into a pit, and went on his way.

"In the first part of the night, Śāriputra, surveying everything with his divine eye, saw from afar the dog suffering great pain in the pit. Then the next day at dawn he put on his Dharma robes, took up his alms bowl, received alms in the city, and went to the pit on the dangerous road, where he fed the dog with the alms he had begged. Then he taught it a Dharma that brought delight and joy. When the dog heard that Dharma, it felt shame and remorse. Then, after seven days had passed, it cast off its evil karma, was freed, and was born as a human being."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "The white dog of that time, on that occasion, is now this śramaṇa Cunda. In a past age, because he had spoken harsh words to a noble one, he was born in an unfortunate realm. Immediately he felt remorse, confessed, and a sense of shame arose in him, and he made the aspiration, 'In a future time, may I meet a spiritual friend!' Because he made that aspiration, he met a spiritual friend, his evil karma was purified, he was born as a human being, and by meeting a buddha he exhausted the defilements."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Keep in mind the kindness of your parents and of spiritual friends. Then, recognizing their kindness in this way, you should repay that kindness. What is called a spiritual friend is a cause that brings about benefit."

When he had said this, countless hundreds of thousands of beings generated the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, and others generated the mind of śrāvakas and of pratyekabuddhas. The great assembly rejoiced greatly at what the Buddha had said, paid homage, and departed.

This concludes the fifth chapter, called "The Treatise," of The Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha Repays Kindness.

The Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha Repays Kindness, fourth section.

At that time the fourfold assembly, placing the Blessed One before them, made offerings to him, served him, honored him, and praised him in words. Then the Tathāgata smiled, and from his mouth he sent forth rays of light, blue, yellow, red and white, called "great compassion." They illuminated the world systems of the ten directions, from the Peak of Existence down to the beings of the eighteen great hells. The light also illuminated the body of Devadatta, and Devadatta, who had been undergoing suffering, was made comfortable.

Then all the many members of the assembly praised the Tathāgata with one voice, saying, "Excellent, excellent! The Blessed One truly possesses great loving-kindness and great compassion; his mind is impartial toward enemy and friend alike. Although someone like Devadatta constantly harms the Blessed One and acts as his enemy, the Blessed One thinks with tenderness even of the one who harms him, and sending forth the light of great compassion he touched the body of Devadatta and freed him from suffering."

Then the Blessed One said to the great assembly, "Not only in this life has Devadatta harmed me. In past ages too he constantly did me harm, yet through the power of loving-kindness and compassion I constantly worked for his benefit and happiness."

Then Ānanda, knowing in his mind the thoughts of the great assembly, in order to cut through everyone's doubts, rose from his seat, arranged his upper robe over one shoulder, set his right knee on the ground, joined his palms, bowed, and said to the Blessed One, "Blessed One, please explain what harms Devadatta did to the Blessed One in past ages."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Listen well, and I shall explain it to you in detail."

The Blessed One said, "Long ago, countless thousands of years in the past, in the land called Vārāṇasī, there appeared in the world a buddha, a blessed one, a tathāgata, an arhat, a perfectly complete buddha, perfect in knowledge and conduct, the Sugata, knower of the world, unsurpassed guide of beings to be tamed, teacher, victor. Having worked for the benefit of beings for ten thousand years, he passed into nirvāṇa. After he passed into nirvāṇa, the true Dharma remained for two hundred thousand years. When the semblance Dharma too had declined and disappeared, there was in the land called Vārāṇasī a king called Mahārāja, virtuous by nature and endowed with a wise intellect. He governed the realm by the true Dharma, and the people were happy and content.

"That king ruled over sixty minor kingdoms on the borders and eight hundred great cities, and he had five hundred Nepalese elephants as well. Though he had twenty thousand queens, he had no son. So the king continually made offerings to and prayed to the worldly gods and the gods of the mountains, of the rivers, and of the trees, and when twelve years had passed, the chief queen conceived a son, and the middle queen also conceived a son. The king was overjoyed, and he himself attended to them, providing service, honor, bedding, food, drink, and every other necessity, all soft and excellent. When he had served them in this way for ten months, the chief queen bore a son, handsome and beautiful in appearance, adorned with excellent marks, and perfectly endowed with the marks of a man. The middle queen also bore a son. The king, delighted, gathered his ministers, the soothsayers, and the brahmins, and showing them his sons he had their marks read.

"When the soothsayers had examined the marks, in order to name the prince they asked, 'What omens occurred when this son was conceived?'

"The king said, 'Formerly the chief queen, the mother of the elder prince, was by nature irascible, very jealous and proud. But once the son was conceived in her womb, she became by nature gentle and good, always smiling, loving all beings as if each were her only child, smiling with a mind of tenderness, and speaking honestly.'

"Then the soothsayers named the prince Good Purpose.

"When they asked, 'What omens occurred when the middle queen conceived her son?' the king said, 'The mother of this son was formerly virtuous and gentle by nature, smiling, speaking honestly, and pleasing everyone's heart. But once the son was conceived in her womb, she became by nature irascible and full of hatred, jealous and proud.'

"The soothsayers said, 'This too is the residue of the son's past karma,' and they named the son Evil Purpose.

"Then the two princes were raised, and when they reached fourteen years of age, Prince Good Purpose was by nature endowed with a wise intellect and a loving mind, and he delighted in giving, so that his parents especially cherished that son as dearly as their own eyes.

"Prince Evil Purpose, however, was irascible by nature, so his parents did not delight in him. Out of hostility, Prince Evil Purpose was constantly jealous and intent on harming his elder brother.

"Later, on another occasion, Prince Good Purpose went out with his retinue, playing music, to stroll beyond the palace and see the sights. There he saw plowmen plowing a field, and as worms came up from the plowing, many birds gathered and ate them. When Prince Good Purpose saw this, a compassionate mind arose in him. 'Since I was born deep within the palace, I have never seen or heard of such a thing,' he thought, and he asked his retinue, 'Why do these creatures harm and eat one another?'

"The retinue said to the prince, 'This realm the king now rules depends on its subjects. The subjects in turn depend on food, and food comes from plowing the fields, from which the five kinds of grain grow and sustain the lives of beings.'

"The prince said, 'Alas, alas!' Then, going on further, he saw men and women doing the work of spinning thread and weaving, toiling to and fro in hardship. Having seen them, he asked the servants on his right and left, 'What are these people doing?'

"They said, 'These people do the work of spinning and weaving, and with it they make clothes to dress and cover our bodies. They too, alas, do work full of hardship.'

"Then, going on further still, he saw many people killing cattle, camels, horses, pigs and sheep and flaying their hides, and he asked, 'What are these people doing?'

"They said, 'These people do the work of killing. By selling the meat they earn clothing and food, and by this they make their living.'

"When the prince heard this, his hair stood on end and all the flesh of his body trembled. 'Alas, alas! My heart cannot bear this killing! One eats another's flesh in revenge, and for countless eons they will go on killing one another.'

"Then, going on further still, he saw some people carrying fishing nets and bird nets, killing birds and fish, so that one eats the flesh of another in revenge. Then he asked, 'What are these people doing?'

"They replied, 'These are fowlers and fishermen. They do this work of killing and so seek clothing and food.'

"When Prince Good Purpose heard those words, his eyes filled with tears. Thinking, 'These beings of the world do deeds that become evil and nonvirtuous, and they will never be freed from suffering,' he was unhappy, overwhelmed with sorrow, and he returned to the palace.

"When his father the king saw the prince return unhappy and sorrowful, he asked the prince, 'What has made you unhappy?' The prince described to his father the king in detail all the things he had seen.

"The king said to the prince, 'Such things exist everywhere and are just the way of existence, so what is there to grieve about?'

"The prince said, 'If I ask one wish of the king, will the king grant it?'

"The king said, 'You are my only son, and since I love and cherish you, I will not go against your wishes.'

"The prince said, 'I wish to give all the wealth, jewels, food and drink there are in my father the king's treasury to all beings.' And the king declared, 'I will not go against your wishes, so give as you please.'

"Then Prince Good Purpose summoned the ministers, opened the king's treasury, loaded wealth and jewels onto five hundred Nepalese elephants, and heaped them up at the four gates of the city. In the four directions he proclaimed, 'All you many beings! Do you want clothing? Do you want food and drink? Take whatever you wish, as you please, and carry it away!' As the fame of Prince Good Purpose spread in every direction in this way, everyone gathered and carried away whatever they wished.

"Before long a third of the king's treasury was exhausted, and the ministers said to the king, 'A third of the treasury has been spent. The prince is making it disappear; may the king consider this.' The king declared, 'I cannot go against the prince's wishes.'

"Then they said, 'Now two thirds of the king's treasury have been spent; may the king consider this.' The king declared, 'I cannot go against the prince's wishes. Use whatever means is best to restrain him a little, without going against his wishes.'

"Then, when Prince Good Purpose went again to open the treasury, the minister in charge of the treasury had gone off on business and did not come for a long time, so there was a delay. Prince Good Purpose said, 'A junior minister guarding the treasury would not dare to go against my wishes; this must surely be the command of the king my father. It is not right for a son who is called devoted to his parents to empty his father's treasury, so now I shall seek wealth and jewels myself and satisfy all beings. If I cannot give all beings clothing, food and drink to their full satisfaction, as they wish, how could I be called the son of a great king?'

"Having said this, he gathered everyone from the great ministers down to the minor ministers and consulted with them, asking, 'By what means could one best acquire wealth and profit?'

"One great minister said, 'If one seeks profit, nothing surpasses plowing the fields: one becomes ten thousand.'

"Another great minister said, 'Of all the ways of making profit in the world, if one establishes herds of livestock and keeps them on good pasture, they multiply greatly, and there is no profit greater than that.'

"Another minister said, 'If one seeks profit in the world, nothing surpasses going to sea and searching for precious jewels. If one finds a precious wish-fulfilling jewel, one will be able to give to all beings as they wish, to their full satisfaction.'

"Good Purpose said, 'This suits my mind; this will do.' He went to the palace and said to his father the king, 'I wish to go out on the great ocean to fetch a precious jewel.'

"When the king heard these words, he became distressed, like a person choking on something who can neither cough it up nor swallow it down, and he said to the prince,

"This realm is your realm too. The treasury, all the wealth, and whatever jewels there are, are yours to command and to use as you wish, so why must you go out on the ocean yourself? You are the son of a king, born deep within the palace. When you sleep you are surrounded by curtained canopies, and you have whatever food you desire. If you now set out on the road and travel, who knows how you will be afflicted, tormented by hunger, thirst, cold and heat? And besides, the hazards in the ocean are not just one but many: savage pretas, venomous nāgas, swirling waves, violent winds that toss and drive one off course, the color of the waters, mountains, sea monsters, great fish and the like. Because the hazards are so many, even if a hundred thousand set out, no more than one or two ever return. So why do you think of going out on the great ocean? I will not consent to it.'

"Then Prince Good Purpose threw himself face down on the ground, stretched out all four limbs, and said to his father the king and his mother, 'If you do not allow me to go to the ocean, I shall give up my life right here and never rise from this spot.'

"Then, when the great king and the queens saw him like this, they gazed at him without blinking, and they tried in every way to cheer him, saying, 'Prince, get up, eat some food and drink something.' But Prince Good Purpose would not listen, and said, 'If you do not allow me to go to the great ocean, I shall never take food or drink.'

"The king and the queens, tormented by unhappiness and grief, wept beside him, lamented, and rolled on the ground. In this way he went without eating or drinking for one day, then two days, three days, up to six days, and his father and mother were tormented by grief all the more. Fearing he would not survive, when the seventh day came his father and mother took his hands and feet into their laps, kissed his hands and feet, and tried to encourage him with sweet, gentle words: 'Now get up, eat some food and drink something. This body, built of food, stays alive through the condition of depending on food. If you do not eat or drink, you will not survive.' But the prince would not listen, and said, 'If my father and mother do not allow me to go, I shall certainly never rise from this very spot.'

"Then the chief queen said to the king, 'The resolve the prince has made is very hard to carry out. Could you bear for the prince to die right here? It is not right to go against his wishes. Out of compassion, it is proper to let him go to the great ocean. If you let him go, there is a chance he may return, one in a hundred; but if you do not let him go now, he will certainly die.'

"The king, unable to bear it, gave permission for him to go. When permission had been given, Prince Good Purpose rose from where he lay, utterly delighted, and bowed his head at the feet of his father the king.

"The king's retinue, the queens, the servants, and many hundreds of thousands of people had been saying to one another, 'Is Prince Good Purpose now dead or alive?' Now they said, 'Prince Good Purpose has risen, overjoyed, and is even eating and drinking.'

"Then the king said to the prince, 'What is your purpose in asking so insistently to go to the ocean?'

"The prince said, 'Great king, I wish to obtain a great precious wish-fulfilling jewel and satisfy the needs of all beings.'

"Then the great king issued a proclamation to everyone: 'Who wishes to go to the ocean? If you return from the ocean, you will be given clothing, food, wealth and jewels without interruption for seven generations, and everything you need for going out on the ocean will be given and provided.' When they heard that Prince Good Purpose was going out on the ocean to fetch a precious jewel, everyone gathered, overjoyed. Among them five hundred men pledged themselves, saying, 'We too ask to go as the prince's attendants.'

"At that time in the land of Vārāṇasī there was an ocean guide who had gone to the ocean many times before. He was very skilled in knowledge of the routes, both those that lead to safety and those that do not; he was eighty years old, and both his eyes were blind. Then the great king himself went to the guide and said to him, 'I have only one son. He has never even gone out of the door, yet he wishes to go to the ocean, so you must go as his attendant.'

"When the guide heard the king's words, he cried out loudly and wept, saying, 'Great king, going out on the great ocean has many hazards and obstacles, and its afflictions are not just one. Even if countless hundreds of thousands set out, no more than one or two ever return. Great king, why are you sending such a prince on such a long and dangerous road?'

"The king said, 'Because of my love and tenderness for the prince, I could not refuse him, and so I have sent him.'

"The guide said, 'In that case, I too will go.'

"Then Prince Good Purpose prepared provisions and equipment for the five hundred men and escorted them to the shore of the ocean.

"At that time his younger brother Evil Purpose thought, 'Our father and mother keep loving Prince Good Purpose more and more. Now he is going to the ocean to fetch a precious jewel, and if he comes back, Good Purpose will be loved more and more, and I will be all the more disliked and despised.' Having thought this, he said, 'I too will go after Prince Good Purpose to fetch jewels.' His father and mother allowed him to go, saying, 'If he goes too, then on dangerous or difficult roads the two brothers, traveling together, will surely help each other,' and so they sent Prince Evil Purpose as well.

"Then Prince Good Purpose boarded the ship, had seven iron mooring chains fastened to it, and stayed there for seven days. On the morning after he first boarded the ship, when the sun rose, Prince Good Purpose beat a drum and called out in a loud voice,

"'Whoever among you wishes to go to the ocean, say nothing at all. Those who are attached to parents, brothers and sisters, wives and children, and who have faith in the pleasures and comforts of Jambudvīpa, turn back from here! Do not come to regret it on my account. Why is that? Because in the ocean the hazards are exceedingly many: countless set out, yet no more than one or two return.' Though he called this out in a loud voice, the many in his company said nothing at all, so he cut one mooring chain and drew it up onto the ship. In the same way he called out once each day for seven days, but all remained silent and said nothing at all, so he cut all seven mooring chains and drew them up onto the ship. Then they raised the sail to the wind and set off.

"Through the force of the prince's compassionate mind and merit, they reached the ocean island of precious jewels without mishap. When they had reached the jewels, Prince Good Purpose beat the drum again and instructed the many people, 'Since the road is long and dangerous, gather jewels here, rest for seven days, and then go back.' He also said, 'These jewels are marvelously precious in Jambudvīpa, but do not make your loads too heavy, for if they are heavy the ship will sink. If they are too light, there is no point in the hardship of the long road, so take a moderate amount and go.' And he instructed those people, 'May you reach your land safely! I am going on to seek the precious wish-fulfilling jewel.' Having instructed them so, he took his leave and they went their separate ways.

"Then Prince Good Purpose set out together with the guide, and for seven days they went through water no deeper than the knees. Then for seven days they went through water reaching up to the neck. Then for seven days they swam, and reached an ocean island on the far side. When they reached that ocean island, a land strewn with silver sand, the guide asked, 'What is this ground?' The prince said, 'This ground is nothing but silver sand.'

"The guide said, 'Look in the four directions: do you see something like a white mountain?'

"The prince said, 'In the southeast a white mountain is visible.'

"The guide said, 'The road leading onward lies at the foot of that mountain. Just beyond its edge there should be a land strewn with golden sand.' Having said this, worn out and exhausted, he collapsed on the ground and said to the prince, 'I am exhausted and have no strength left; I shall die here. Prince, from here go east for seven days and you should come to a golden mountain. Then go on for seven days and you should reach a land covered entirely with blue lotuses. Then go on another seven days and you should come to a land covered entirely with red lotuses. Beyond those lotuses there is a castle made of the seven precious substances. Its walls are made of gold, its turrets of silver, and its parapets of red kekeru gems; it is adorned with various jewels such as crystal and agate, covered above with a net of red pearls, and surrounded on its outer edge by seven successive moats of vaidūrya and sapphire. Within it is the palace of the nāga king, and in the ear of that nāga king is a precious wish-fulfilling jewel. Ask him for it. If you obtain it, it will rain down the seven precious substances on Jambudvīpa, and it will rain down clothing, food, medicines for the sick, music, and every necessity, just as one wishes. That is why it is called the precious wish-fulfilling jewel. If the prince obtains it, all his aspirations will surely be fulfilled.'

"Having said this, he died. Then Prince Good Purpose took the guide into his lap, cried out and wept: 'Alas, alas! Why have I lost my dear guardian?' He buried the guide's bones in the golden sand, circumambulated them three times to the right, paid homage, and went on.

"Going on and on, he reached the foot of the golden mountain. When he had passed beyond the foot of the golden mountain, he found the ground covered with blue lotuses, and saw that beneath those lotuses it was full of great venomous snakes. Those venomous snakes had three kinds of venom: the venom of the bite, the venom of touch, and the venom of breath. The venomous snakes coiled around the lotus stalks, their mouths gaping and their eyes staring, looking at the prince. Then Prince Good Purpose entered the samādhi of a loving mind, and by the power of the samādhi set out from there. Though he went on, treading upon those lotuses with his feet, the venomous snakes did him no harm.

"Going on and on in this way through the force of the samādhi of a loving mind, he reached the nāga palace. The outside of the nāga king's palace was surrounded by seven successive moats, and in those moats venomous nāgas, coiled around one another with their heads raised, guarded the castle gate. Then Prince Good Purpose came to the castle gate. When he saw the venomous nāgas, with a loving mind he brought to mind all the beings of Jambudvīpa and thought, 'I have come all this way for the sake of beings. If the venomous nāgas harm me, there will be no benefit and happiness for them.' Having thought this, Prince Good Purpose raised his right hand and commanded the venomous nāgas, 'Know this! I wish to meet the nāga king for the sake of all beings.'

"Then the venomous nāgas opened a way, and passing through all seven successive moats, he reached the castle gate. At that castle gate he saw two crystal maidens sitting and spinning crystal thread. When he asked, 'Who are you?' they said, 'We are servant girls who guard the outer gate of the nāga king.'

"Then, entering further, he went on and reached the middle gate. At the middle gate he saw four crystal maidens sitting and spinning silver thread. When he asked those maidens, 'Are you the queens of the nāga king?' they said, 'No, we are servant girls who guard the middle gate of the nāga king.'

"Then the prince entered that gate and went on, and at the inner gate he saw eight crystal maidens sitting and spinning golden thread. When he asked, 'Who are you?' they said, 'We are servant girls who guard the inner gate of the nāga king's palace.'

"Then Prince Good Purpose instructed those maidens, 'I am the son of the king of Vārāṇasī in Jambudvīpa, called Good Purpose. Take this message: he wishes to meet the king and is now waiting at the gate.' The gatekeeper reported this to the nāga king, and the nāga king was amazed. He thought, 'Unless this is someone whose merit and roots of virtue are very pure, no other ordinary person could travel such a road and come here.' Having thought this, he called him in, and the nāga king himself came to greet him and led him into the palace.

"Inside the nāga king's palace the floor was made of vaidūrya and the throne of the seven precious substances. Lights of every kind blazed so brightly the eye could hardly bear them. Then the nāga king seated Prince Good Purpose on a seat that had been spread for him and conversed with him. Then Prince Good Purpose took up the conversation and spoke at length to the nāga king, teaching him the Dharma with many kinds of inspiring, uplifting and delightful talk: talk on giving, talk on moral discipline, talk on gods and humans, and so on.

"Then the nāga king was overjoyed and delighted, and asked, 'What do you wish for, you who have traveled such a hard road and come from so far?'

"Prince Good Purpose said to the nāga king, 'The beings of Jambudvīpa undergo endless great suffering for the sake of clothing, wealth, food and drink, so I wish for the precious jewel in the king's left ear.'

"The nāga king said, 'If you will consent to accept the offerings I prepare for just seven days, I will give you the jewel.'

"Then Prince Good Purpose, as the nāga king had invited him, stayed in the nāga palace for seven days. Taking the precious wish-fulfilling jewel, he set out to return to Jambudvīpa. Then the great nāga king, by the power of the nāgas, flew through the sky and escorted him to the near shore of the ocean. When he arrived there, he met his younger brother Evil Purpose. The prince asked his younger brother Evil Purpose, 'Where are all your companions? Are you here all alone?'

"Prince Evil Purpose said, 'The ship sank and everyone died. Only I, clinging to a corpse, did not die and came through; all the others died at once.'

"Prince Good Purpose said, 'In the world, no jewel surpasses one's own body.'

"His younger brother said, 'That is not so. It is easier to die rich than to live poor. How do I know this? When I went and stayed in a charnel ground, I heard the corpses in that charnel ground saying so.'

"Prince Good Purpose, being honest by nature, said to his younger brother, 'Even if you have no jewels, it does not matter, for I have the nāga king's precious wish-fulfilling jewel.'

"When his younger brother asked, 'Where is the jewel?' the elder brother said, 'The precious wish-fulfilling jewel is in my topknot.'

"When the younger brother heard him say this, he was all the more agitated by jealousy, and hatred arose in him. He thought, 'On top of my father the king and my mother disliking me so much, now my elder brother has found the precious wish-fulfilling jewel. From now on they will dislike me even more and despise me like dirt and stones.' Having thought this, he said to his elder brother Good Purpose, 'Excellent indeed! It is excellent that you have obtained the precious jewel, but now in this fearful place it must be guarded well.'

"Then Prince Good Purpose untied his topknot, gave the jewel into the hands of his younger brother Evil Purpose, and said, 'When you are tired and worn out and lie down to sleep, I will watch over it and guard it. When I am tired and lie down to sleep, you watch over it and guard it.'

"Then, when it was Evil Purpose's turn to guard the jewel and his elder brother Good Purpose had fallen asleep, Evil Purpose stuck two splinters of dry bamboo into both of Good Purpose's eyes, took the precious jewel, and fled.

"Then Prince Good Purpose called out, 'Younger brother Evil Purpose! A thief has stuck splinters of wood into both my eyes; take the precious jewel and flee!' He called out, but there was no answer. 'Now my younger brother Evil Purpose must have been killed by an enemy, for there is no answer,' he said, and though he called out in a voice so loud that even the gods heard it, for a long time there was no answer at all.

"Then a tree deity spoke thus: 'Your younger brother Evil Purpose is himself the enemy. He put out both your eyes, took the precious jewel, and has fled back home. What use is calling out "Evil Purpose"?' When Prince Good Purpose heard those words, he was tormented by grief and became despondent.

"Then Prince Evil Purpose took the precious jewel back to his own land, met his father and mother, and said to his father the king and his mother, 'Because I am endowed with merit, I have returned without mishap. Prince Good Purpose and the other companions had little merit, so they perished in the water and all died.'

"When his father the king and his mother heard those words, they cried out loudly and wept, fainted, and fell to the ground. Then, after cold water was sprinkled on them, after a long time they came to again and said to Prince Evil Purpose, 'What face do you have, coming back to meet us like this?' Prince Evil Purpose, displeased and angry, dug into the ground in a secluded place and hid the precious jewel.

"At that time, since there was no one to pull out the dry bamboo splinters stuck in both his eyes, Prince Good Purpose, tormented by pain, rolled on the ground. He did not know where to go, and was tormented by hunger and thirst as well. Neither able to live nor able to die, he went groping his way blindly until he reached the border region of the land of King Lishibar.

"That king of Lishibar had a daughter who had been promised in marriage to Prince Good Purpose, son of the king of Vārāṇasī. King Lishibar had a cowherd who tended five hundred cattle, going about grazing them wherever there was grass and water.

At that time Prince Good Purpose was lying on a road, and the cowherd came that way driving the greater part of his herd. The prince was all but trampled by the cattle, but among them was a king of bulls, which stood over the prince. When all the other cattle had passed, it lowered itself down, rolled on the ground, looked at the prince, and stood up again. Then with its tongue it licked the prince's two eyes and drew out the bamboo splinters.

Then the cowherd, coming along behind, arrived there and asked, "Who are you?"

Good Purpose thought, "If I tell in detail my family and lineage and what has happened to me, my younger brother may come to great suffering." Having thought this, he said, "I am a blind beggar."

Then the cowherd looked at him, and inferring from his other marks, saw that he was no ordinary person. He said, "My house is not far from here. I will provide for your livelihood; let us go to my house." And he led Prince Good Purpose to his home.

When the cowherd had brought him home, he provided him with food, drink and everything he needed, and he instructed the sons and daughters of the household and everyone in the house, "All of you must serve this blind man as you would serve me."

So for more than a month they looked after him in the house, but they grew weary of it and said, "Now our provisions are running low. Who could support this blind man forever?"

When Good Purpose heard these words, his heart sank. When that night had passed, the next day he said to the householder, "Now I shall go."

The householder said, "What has gone wrong, that you are thinking of leaving us?"

Good Purpose said, "By nature, host and guest do not stay together for long." And Good Purpose said further, "If you are so kind to me, make me a sweet-sounding lute, and take me to some place where many people gather, or to a great city."

Then the householder provided what was needed, just as he wished, brought him into the midst of the crowds in the city of King Lishibar, and returned home in peace.

Then, because Prince Good Purpose was exceedingly skilled at the lute, and the sweet sound of his lute was so delightful, everyone gave him the necessities of life, and he never lacked for food or drink. At that time there were five hundred beggars in the land of Lishibar, and he satisfied all of them as well with food and drink.

Now that king had an orchard, whose fruit grew so abundantly that the birds could never be kept away. The overseer who guarded the orchard then said to Prince Good Purpose, "Could you not stay and guard my fruit orchard? I will provide you with the necessities you need."

Good Purpose said, "I have no eyes and cannot see. How could I guard your orchard and drive away the birds?"

The overseer of the orchard said, "I have a method. I will stretch ropes across the tops of my trees, tie bells to the ropes, and place the rope in your hand. Whenever you hear the cries of birds, it will be enough to pull on the rope."

Good Purpose said, "If it is done that way, I can do it. Lead me to the trees." So saying, he went there. And Prince Good Purpose stayed in that orchard, guarding the fruit, playing music on his lute and amusing himself.

At that time the king's daughter, with her attendants, went to stroll in the orchard and enjoy the sights. Seeing the blind man, she went up to him and asked, "Whose man are you?"

He said, "I am a blind beggar."

When the king's daughter saw the blind man, she felt love for him and could not bear to leave him. When the king sent for his daughter, she said, "I will not go. Bring food and drink here; I will eat together with this blind man." When she had eaten the meal together with the blind man, she said to the king, "Please give me to this blind man. If you give me to him, my heart will be content."

The king said, "Daughter, have you been seized by a spirit or a graha? Or are you mad? Is your heart bewitched? Why would you stay with that blind man? Do you not know? Earlier the king of Vārāṇasī asked for you as a wife for his son Good Purpose, and you were promised to him. The prince has now gone to the ocean, and he has not yet returned. Why then would you think of becoming the wife of a beggar?"

The daughter said, "That is so, yet even if I lose my life, I will not leave the blind man."

When the king heard these words, he could not refuse his daughter. He summoned the blind man and lodged him in a secluded part of the palace.

Then the king's daughter went to the blind man and said, "Do you know? Now I will join with you as husband and wife."

Good Purpose said, "Whose daughter are you, that you think of joining with me as husband and wife?"

She said, "I am the daughter of King Lishibar."

Good Purpose said, "You are a king's daughter, and I am a beggar. How could we show each other due respect?"

The wife said, "I will serve you with all my heart, and I will never act against your wishes."

So they lived together for ninety days. Then one day the wife went outside without speaking to her husband, and after a long while she came back.

Good Purpose said to his wife, "Where did you go in secret? Where did you go without even telling me, and where have you come back from?"

The wife said, "I did not go anywhere in secret."

Good Purpose said, "Who can know whether you went in secret or not?"

The wife was unhappy and dejected, and with her eyes full of tears she spoke these words and swore an oath: "If I went off on some secret business behind your back, may your two eyes never be healed. But if that is not so, may one of your eyes see as it did before."

The moment she had spoken the words of her oath, one of her husband's eyes could see as before and became clear, shining like a star, and his wife saw this plainly. The wife said, "Now do you believe me?"

Good Purpose smiled and said nothing at all.

The wife said, "You are shameless and ungrateful. I am the daughter of a great king, and you are an ordinary man, and yet, though I have served you with all my heart, you still do not believe me."

Her husband said, "Do you know who I am?"

She said, "I do not know. You are a beggar."

Her husband said, "No, I am not. I am the one called Prince Good Purpose, son of the king of the land of Vārāṇasī."

The wife said, "You are a great fool. Why do you say such a thing? The son of the king of Vārāṇasī set out on the ocean and went to fetch jewels, and he has not yet returned. So why do you claim to be him? You are telling lies, and I do not believe you."

Prince Good Purpose said, "From the time I was first born until now, I have never told a lie."

The wife said, "Who could be sure whether what you say is true or false?"

Her husband said, "If these words I have spoken are false and I have deceived you, may my one eye never see again. But if my words are true, may my other eye also see as it did before." The moment he had said this, the eyeball of that other eye too became clear and could see as before.

When the wife saw that Prince Good Purpose's two eyes were clear and beautiful as before, and that he bore the marks of a great man, was delightful, and was handsome beyond compare in the world, a mind of intense joy arose in her. She was as elated as if blessed by a noble one, and she gazed at him almost without blinking.

Then she went to the palace and said to her father the king, "That husband of mine is Prince Good Purpose."

The king said, "Has this foolish girl gone mad? Has she been deceived by a hungry ghost? What is wrong with her, that she says such things? It is said that Prince Good Purpose set out on the ocean and has still not returned. Why do you call a beggar Prince Good Purpose?"

The daughter said, "It is not so. If the king does not believe me, would you please look with your own eyes?"

The moment the king looked, he recognized that it was Prince Good Purpose. Having recognized him, he was alarmed and said, "If the king of Vārāṇasī hears of this, we will be blamed." So saying, he said, "Prince Good Purpose, I did not know before that you were the prince. I confess my offense."

The prince said, "There is no offense in that. But, king, give a gift to that cowherd."

Then King Lishibar gave that cowherd gold, silver, jewels, clothing, food and drink, and also gave him five hundred cattle outright. Then the cowherd, overjoyed, proclaimed loudly among a great crowd of people, "What kindness of help did I do for Prince Good Purpose, that I should be given so much wealth and so many jewels? Even when giving is done in secret, its kindness returns plainly before one's eyes. So vast is the karmic maturation of the fruit of giving!"

At that, the whole great assembly rejoiced greatly, and a mind delighting in giving arose in them. The gods too praised and extolled that man, saying, "Those who wish for buddhahood should give to all," and so it came to pass.

Now in the past, before Prince Good Purpose set out on the ocean, while he was living in the palace, he had raised a white goose, which was his constant companion. When eating, drinking, going about, staying, sitting or lying down, they were always together.

Then his mother the queen went to the goose and said to it, "While the prince was here, you were always his companion. Now he has gone to the ocean, and there is no knowing whether he is dead or alive; I do not know. Do you not remember the prince?"

When the goose heard these words, it cried out and rolled on the ground, and with its eyes full of tears it said to the queen, "Queen, if you command me to search for the prince, I shall go."

When it had said this, the queen wrote a letter, tied it to the goose's neck, and sent it to search for the prince. Then the goose asked in which direction the prince had gone, flew up into the sky, and went toward the ocean.

When the queen saw the goose leave like this, she thought, "Now, without a doubt, I will soon surely hear news of whether my son is alive or dead."

Then the goose soared back and forth in the sky above the ocean, examining everywhere and searching, but it did not see the prince. Gradually it reached the land of King Lishibar, and from afar it saw Prince Good Purpose staying in the king's palace. Then the goose came down right there, came to where the prince was, gave a piteous cry, and gazed at the prince with great joy.

Then the prince took the letter from the goose's neck and respectfully bowed his head to it. Then he opened the letter and read it. When he had read it, he understood that his parents, the king and queen, remembering the prince day and night, had wept constantly until both their eyes had gone blind.

Then Prince Good Purpose wrote down in detail everything that had happened, tied it once more to the goose's neck, and sent it back. Then the goose, overjoyed, came back to the land of Vārāṇasī, to his father the king and his mother the queen.

When the king saw that a letter had come, he was overjoyed and delighted, and his elation knew no bounds. He also learned that Prince Good Purpose had been robbed by Evil Purpose, that his jewels had been stolen, and that he had suffered greatly.

Then his father the king at once seized Evil Purpose, had his hands and feet bound up, fastened an iron chain around his neck, and threw him into prison.

Then he sent a messenger to King Lishibar with the message, "Why have you detained the prince and caused us to be tormented by grief?"

Then King Lishibar, afraid, adorned Prince Good Purpose with clothes and ornaments and stationed him at the border. He did as the message required, and arranged it so that it was as though a messenger had come, saying, "Prince Good Purpose has returned from the ocean and is now coming here."

Then King Lishibar had the whole region through which the prince was to come swept and sprinkled with water. Victory banners, pennants and parasols were hung, bells were rung and drums were beaten, and all kinds of music were played. The king himself, with his retinue, went out from the palace to welcome the prince from afar. He gave him his daughter as his wife and sent him off to be escorted to the land of Vārāṇasī.

When the king and queen of Vārāṇasī heard that the prince was coming, they were overjoyed and delighted. Mounted on the finest elephant of Nepal, they went out to welcome the prince. The place of welcome was swept and sprinkled, incense, pennants, victory banners, parasols and the like were set out and hung up, and all kinds of music were played as well. All the people dwelling in that land, men and women alike, heard that Prince Good Purpose had returned safely from the ocean, and they all rejoiced greatly, and everyone went out to welcome the prince.

Then Prince Good Purpose bowed his head at the feet of his father the king and his mother the queen. The king and queen were blind and could not see the prince, so they stroked him with their hands and said, "Are you our son Good Purpose? Because we remembered you constantly and were tormented by grief, we have become like this."

When the prince had paid homage to his father the king and to the queen, he raised his hands to the vassal kings, the ministers and the multitude of people, and with a loud voice he lifted their spirits, saying, "Although I met with hardship, through your merit I have returned."

Then Prince Good Purpose said to his father the king, "Where is my younger brother Evil Purpose now? Where is he?"

His father the king said, "Do not ask about him. That evildoer is now in prison, and there is no question of releasing him."

Prince Good Purpose said, "Please let me meet Evil Purpose." He pleaded in this way, weeping, two and three times, and the king could not refuse his wish, so Evil Purpose was brought out of prison. The prince asked that the iron chains and shackles be taken off Evil Purpose's hands and feet.

Then, when he had been brought out of the prison, the prince embraced Evil Purpose in his lap and lifted his spirits with gentle words, saying, "Have you suffered greatly?" After they had talked, he asked two and three times, "Where is the precious jewel of mine that you took?" Only then did Evil Purpose say, "It is hidden under the ground."

Then Prince Good Purpose dug the jewel out of that place and took it. Then he came before his father the king and his mother the queen. He perfumed the precious jewel with fine incense, knelt down, and made this aspiration: "If you are truly a precious wish-fulfilling jewel, may the eyes of my father the king and my mother the queen see once more as they did before." The moment he had said this, the eyes of both his father the king and his mother saw clearly as before, and they were overjoyed and delighted.

Then Prince Good Purpose, on the fifteenth day, at the time of the full moon, bathed, put on new, clean clothes, and placed the precious jewel on top of a banner pole. Holding a censer in his hand, he bowed his head to the precious wish-fulfilling jewel and made this aspiration: "I have undergone great suffering for the sake of all the sentient beings of Jambudvīpa, and I have obtained this precious jewel."

The moment he had said this, a great wind arose from the east and blew away the clouds and mist, making the whole sky completely clear. The wind blew away all the filthy mire, excrement and urine, refuse, and everything unpleasant in Jambudvīpa, making it completely clean. Then, through the power of the precious jewel, self-grown śāli rice, needing no ploughing, rained down upon Jambudvīpa. That śāli rice was soft and full of color and flavor, and it fell until every hollow and ravine was filled about knee-deep.

After that, fine clothes to wear, ornaments for the body such as bracelets, jewels and armlets, rained down. After that, gold, silver, the seven kinds of precious substances and the like, and all kinds of musical instruments, in short, every necessity that any sentient being could need or wish for, rained down, and all were satisfied and contented.

Such is the meaning of a bodhisattva practicing the perfection of generosity through the force of great compassion, and giving all sentient beings the necessities they need until they are satisfied.

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "If you think that the great king of the land of Vārāṇasī at that time, on that occasion, was someone else, do not regard it so. The great king at that time, on that occasion, is now my father, King Śuddhodana. If you think that the queen at that time, on that occasion, was someone else, do not regard it so. She who was the queen at that time, on that occasion, is now my mother Māyā. Prince Evil Purpose at that time, on that occasion, is now Devadatta himself. Prince Good Purpose at that time, on that occasion, is now I myself.

"Even in times long past, this Devadatta constantly harmed me with malicious intent, yet I always accepted it with patience, always showed him kindness, and continually brought him happiness. How much more so now that I have attained complete awakening!"

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, hundreds of thousands of gods and humans attained results ranging from the fruit of stream-entry up to the fruit of arhatship. Furthermore, countless hundreds of thousands of gods and humans generated the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, and countless hundreds of thousands of sentient beings generated the resolve for the levels of the śrāvakas and the pratyekabuddhas.

Ānanda said to the Buddha, "Blessed One, what is the name of this sūtra? How should it be kept in mind?"

The Blessed One said, "The name of this sūtra is Skill in Means: Repaying the Kindness of the Buddha, Giving to All Sentient Beings Until They Are Satisfied."

The great assembly rejoiced greatly at the teaching of this sūtra, paid homage, and departed.

Furthermore, although Devadatta had gone forth into homelessness following the teaching of the Buddha, he was constantly jealous for the sake of honor and gain. Though Devadatta knew how to recite from memory as many sūtras as would make sixty thousand loads for a mighty elephant, he was not freed from the hell of the beings who commit the deeds of immediate retribution.

At that time Devadatta became close friends with King Ajātaśatru. They were fond of each other and felt close, and each trusted the other's word.

Then Devadatta said to King Ajātaśatru, "You become the new king, and I will become the new Buddha."

King Ajātaśatru said, "My father the king is still alive now, so that would not be proper."

Devadatta said, "He can be done away with. And I shall do away with the Buddha. After that, would it not be pleasant for a new king and a new Buddha to work for the welfare of beings?"

Then King Ajātaśatru, just as Devadatta had said, killed his father the king and seized Rājagṛha, the land of Vārāṇasī.

Devadatta said to King Ajātaśatru, "Now I too shall do away with the Tathāgata."

Ajātaśatru said, "The Tathāgata possesses the power of great miracles, and he knows the thoughts that others think. How could you do away with him? Besides, the Tathāgata has great close disciples, Śāriputra, Maudgalyāyana, Mist, Aniruddha and others, so you will not be able to."

Devadatta said to King Ajātaśatru, "King, be my ally."

The king said, "What do you intend to do?"

Devadatta said, "Let the great king issue a decree throughout the land: no one is to give the monks clothing, food, drink or alms."

Then King Ajātaśatru issued a decree: "If anyone gives clothing, food or alms to the monks, his hands and feet will be cut off."

At that time the Blessed One, together with his retinue, the great Saṅgha of monks, went out from Vulture Peak to beg for alms, but they obtained nothing at all. In the same way, from the first day through the seventh day they obtained nothing at all, and so the great disciples, Śāriputra and the others, went by the power of their miracles to other lands to beg for clothing, food and alms.

At that time Devadatta said to King Ajātaśatru, "Now not one of the Buddha's great disciples is left, and the Tathāgata is staying alone. Let the king send to invite the Tathāgata. When he comes into the palace, we will give liquor to five hundred savage black elephants of Nepal and make them thoroughly drunk. Then, once the Buddha has been invited and has come into the palace, those raging, drunken elephants will trample him and kill him."

Then King Ajātaśatru sent someone to invite the Tathāgata, and the Buddha and five hundred arhats accepted the king's invitation and went to Rājagṛha.

Then King Ajātaśatru let loose the five hundred raging, drunken elephants. The raging elephants broke down trees, tore through fences and knocked down walls, and, trumpeting loudly with fury, they charged toward the Tathāgata and the five hundred arhats.

Then the five hundred arhats, terrified, rose up into the sky above and remained in the sky above the Buddha. At that time Ānanda alone stayed at the Tathāgata's side.

At that time the Tathāgata, through the force of his love and compassion, raised his right hand, and from his five fingers he emanated lions with gaping jaws, roaring loudly. The five hundred raging elephants were terrified and fell to the ground.

Then the Tathāgata, surrounded by a great retinue, went to the king's palace. King Ajātaśatru came outside, welcomed the Tathāgata, and invited him to be seated. When he was seated, the king confessed and repented, and said to the Buddha, "Blessed One, it was not I who did this. Devadatta made me do it."

The Blessed One said, "I know that. This Devadatta has always done me harm, and not only now. In times past too, though he always did me harm, I, through the force of great compassion, always did him good."

At that time King Ajātaśatru joined his palms and said to the Blessed One, "What were the harms that this Devadatta did to the Tathāgata in times past?"

The Blessed One said, "Listen well, and I shall analyze and explain it.

"In times past, beyond countless eons ago, there was a great king who was fond of goose meat. He constantly had a hunter catch geese for him.

"At that time five hundred geese were flying through the sky from the north toward the south, and the king of the geese was caught in the hunter's bird net. Then the hunter, delighted, came out of his hut and was about to kill it, when a female goose flew up, came near, and gave a piteous cry.

"Then the hunter drew his bow, nocked an arrow and made ready to kill her, but she was not afraid of the arrow. Beating her two wings, she leaped onto the king of the geese. The other five hundred geese also circled and hovered in the sky above.

"At that time the hunter saw that female goose crying piteously, vomiting blood from her mouth, and, with no regard even for her own life, devoted to the king of the geese. Seeing this, the hunter thought, 'If even birds born in the animal realm are so devoted to one another that they would give up even body and life, what kind of heart would I have to kill this king of the geese?' Thinking this, he lifted the net and let it go.

"At that time the female goose, overjoyed, flew off after it, and surrounded by the five hundred geese they flew away through the sky.

"Then that hunter said to the king,

"I caught a king of the geese and was going to offer it to you, but a female goose, vomiting blood from her mouth and without fear even of my bow and arrow, leaped onto the king of the geese. My heart could not bear it, so I let him go, and surrounded by five hundred geese they flew away through the sky."

At that time, when the king heard these words, his mind became troubled and compassion arose in him. He thought, "Even those born in the animal realm cherish one another and do not begrudge even body and life." Thinking this, the king too vowed never again to eat the flesh of geese.

"The great king at that time, on that occasion, is now the great king yourself. The one who was the hunter at that time, on that occasion, is now Devadatta himself. The female goose who vomited blood from her mouth at that time, on that occasion, is now Ānanda. The five hundred geese at that time, on that occasion, are now the five hundred arhats. The one who was the king of the geese at that time, on that occasion, is now I.

"Therefore Ānanda's devotion to me now is just as it was before. And the five hundred arhats rising into the sky above at this time is just as it happened before. And at that time too, though Devadatta did me harm, I, through the force of great compassion, constantly acted for his benefit."

When this Dharma was taught, hundreds of thousands of gods and humans attained results ranging from the first fruit up to the fourth fruit. Some generated the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, and others generated the resolve for the levels of the śrāvakas and the pratyekabuddhas.

Furthermore, Devadatta's malice was not pacified, and he thought, "I will let the nails of my ten fingers grow long, put poison beneath the nails, go to where the Tathāgata is, and bow my head at his feet. When I bow in this way and take hold of his feet, the poison under my ten nails will be smeared on his feet. The poison will spread, and without a doubt he will die."

Having thought this, he went to where the Tathāgata was, just as he had planned. When he arrived there, he bowed his head at his feet, took hold of his feet with his hands, and smeared on the poison. At that moment the poison turned into sweet dew and did the Tathāgata no harm.

When things did not turn out as Devadatta had planned, he thought again, "The Tathāgata is now staying alone on Vulture Peak. I will go up that mountain, send a boulder rolling down, and kill him." Having thought this, he went to the summit of the mountain and sent a rockslide down.

"Though the little toe of the Buddha's foot was wounded, through the force of my great compassion I remain impartial toward enemy and friend.

"Furthermore, in times past, beyond countless eons ago, at that time, on that occasion, there appeared in the world the Buddha, the Blessed One called Rightly Teaching, a tathāgata, arhat, perfectly and completely awakened buddha, perfect in knowledge and conduct, a sugata, knower of the world, unsurpassed guide of those to be tamed, teacher, victor.

"After that Buddha had passed into nirvāṇa, during the time when the semblance of the Dharma was practiced, a monk who practiced meditative concentration was staying in a forest grove. At that time many lice were feeding on that monk. Then the monk made this agreement with the lice: 'When I am practicing meditative concentration, stay hidden and do not move.' When he had made this agreement, the lice did just that.

"Then at another time a flea came there and said to the lice, 'How is it that you have become so fat and glossy?' When it asked this, the lice said, 'Our host, who constantly practices meditative concentration, lets us eat our food and drink at the proper times without fail, and that is why we are glossy.'

"The flea said, 'I too will stay with you.' Having said this, it stayed there with them. At that time, while the monk was practicing meditative concentration, the flea caught the smell of flesh and blood and bit the monk. Then the monk, his mind disturbed, took off his robe, threw it into the fire, and burned it.

"The monk who practiced meditative concentration at that time, on that occasion, is the Buddha Kāśyapa. The flea at that time, on that occasion, is Devadatta himself. The one who was a louse at that time, on that occasion, is now I.

"That Devadatta did me harm for the sake of honor and gain, and even now that I have attained complete awakening, for the sake of honor and fame he drew blood from the body of the Buddha and fell into the great hell.

"The harms that Devadatta, with constant malice toward me, has done to the Tathāgata could not be told in full even in an eon, yet the Tathāgata has always protected him with great compassion, with a loving and tender heart. Through meeting with Devadatta I quickly attained buddhahood, and so, remembering his kindness, I have always protected him with compassion."

At that time the Tathāgata said to Ānanda, "Go into the hell and ask, 'Devadatta, are you greatly tormented by suffering?' and then come back."

Then Ānanda, as the Buddha had instructed, went into the hell. Arriving at the gate of the hell, he said to Abang, the guardian of the hell, "Call Devadatta."

Abang said, "The Devadatta of which buddha do you want? Every buddha of the past has also had someone called Devadatta."

Then Abang said to Devadatta, "Ānanda is now waiting outside the gate and needs to meet you."

Devadatta came there and said to Ānanda, "Have you come well? What does the Tathāgata say? For me now, the happiness here in the Avīci hell is like attaining the bliss of a monk who has entered the third dhyāna."

The Blessed One said, "For the sake of beings, the great bodhisattva, through great skill in means, experiences the countless great sufferings of saṃsāra, of birth and death, and still he does not regard them as harm. If anyone thinks that Devadatta is truly an evildoer who has fallen into the Avīci hell, that cannot be so."

At that time, when the great assembly heard the Tathāgata teach this most profound skill in means of Devadatta's conduct, countless hundreds of thousands of bodhisattvas attained acceptance of the nonarising of phenomena.

Countless hundreds of thousands of gods and humans gave rise to the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. Countless hundreds of thousands of gods and humans attained the fruits from the fruit of stream-entry up to the fruit of arhatship. At that time the gods of the intermediate space let divine flowers fall like rain from the sky above, played divine music, and lit everything with great rays of light, praising, "What the Tathāgata has taught is most wonderful and excellent!" When the Blessed One had spoken thus, the great assembly rejoiced, bowed their heads, and departed.

This concludes the sixth chapter, called Evil Purpose, from The Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha Repays Kindness.

The Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha Repays Kindness. Fifth fascicle.

At that time the great assembly paid service and reverence to the Blessed One, made offerings, extolled him and praised him, circled around him, and sat gazing at him. Then the Tathāgata, delighting in his immeasurable and most profound domain of activity, in order to rescue many beings from the unbearable suffering of the three realms of existence, to dispel the five obscurations, to loosen the fetters that bind on every side, to bring all sentient beings to liberation, and to establish them in the bliss of the unconditioned, distinguished and taught two kinds of field of merit. What are the two? The conditioned field of merit and the unconditioned field of merit. And what are these? They are father and mother, the preceptor, the teacher, the Buddha, the Dharma, the Saṅgha, and the bodhisattvas. If sentient beings make merit by offering toward them, they will attain the fruit of the path.

Then the Blessed One said to Śāriputra, foremost of the wise, and the other great close disciples, "Know this: before long the Tathāgata will pass into nirvāṇa."

When Śāriputra heard these words, he felt as if the limbs of his body were pierced with needles. Overwhelmed by grief, he fell to the ground and fainted. Cold water was sprinkled on his face, and after a long while he came to again. He rose, joined his palms, bowed, and praised him with these verses:

"In the nectar of the Buddha's teaching
No listener grows weary or tired;
