Moreover, the bodhisattva studies the treatises on the science of reasoning in order to know all faults and flaws, to demolish the doctrines of the wrong views of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), to know the skillful means for taming sentient beings, and to distinguish the meaning taught by the Tathāgata from the meaning of worldly speech.

"Why does a bodhisattva study the treatises on the science of language? Because speech is adorned by fully correct words, and the meaning cannot be clearly shown with incorrect words; in order to know and comprehend every meaning; so that, correct speech not declining, he is not undone by pride; and in order to turn others back from wrong views and know the skillful means for taming sentient beings. For these reasons the bodhisattva studies the treatises on the science of language.

"Why does a bodhisattva study the treatises on the science of medicine? In order to free sentient beings from the hundred and four unwholesome diseases; out of love and tender care for all sentient beings, in order to make sentient beings give rise to faith; in order that, being thus freed from disease, they may give rise to joy; so that, joy having thus arisen, they will always keep the Buddha in mind; and in order, through great compassion, to bring sentient beings across to the far shore. For these reasons the bodhisattva studies the treatises on the science of medicine.

"Why does a bodhisattva study the worldly arts and crafts? In order to benefit sentient beings; so that, since wealth and goods are then easily obtained, sentient beings may be brought to faith; in order, by knowing every kind of worldly activity, to overcome pride; in order to tame sentient beings; and in order to clear away every obscuration brought about by the things of worldly existence. For these reasons the bodhisattva studies the arts and crafts.

"If a bodhisattva has not trained in the five fields of knowledge, he will never be able to attain the wisdom of omniscience in unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. Therefore, in order to attain unsurpassed awakening, one should train in the five fields of knowledge.

"A bodhisattva's knowing kindness and repaying kindness means that he should explain and teach sentient beings what is to be explained. How should he explain it? In order that they may attain unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, he should explain in a way endowed with the five fields of knowledge. Explanation, moreover, is of two kinds: gradual explanation and completely pure explanation.

"What is gradual explanation? First one explains the perfection of generosity, and after that one explains everything from the perfection of moral discipline up to the perfection of wisdom; and, in order to know kindness and repay kindness, one attends to the meaning and abides in accordance with the Dharma. This is called gradual explanation.

"What is called completely pure explanation? If the listener is sitting while the one who explains is standing, one should not explain to him. If the listener looks for faults in the Dharma or looks for faults in the explanation, one should not explain to him. If the listener relies on the person and does not rely on the Dharma, then, since he does not rely on the Dharma, one should not explain to him. If the listener relies on the letter and does not rely on the meaning, one should not explain to him; and so on, up to: if he relies on the provisional meaning and does not rely on the definitive meaning, one should not explain to him. Why is that? Because such a person has no respect for the completely pure Dharma of the buddhas and bodhisattvas. When the one who explains and teaches also honors the Dharma, and the one who listens to the Dharma listens one-pointedly with a respectful mind and without any pride, that is called completely pure explanation.

"Furthermore, what is called gradual explanation is explaining everything. What is called explaining everything is explaining the twelve divisions of the scriptures, down to a single word, a single verse, or half a verse; and this, too, is endowed with the sound of the words, with meaning, and with the Dharma. In teaching the Dharma and its meaning, at the proper time one teaches so as to inspire, to praise and uplift, and to gladden; at certain times one rebukes and scolds; at certain times one teaches straightforwardly and directly; at certain times one teaches with examples, in whatever way suits the listener; at certain times one teaches in a way closer to them and easier than that; and at certain times one teaches what is easy to enter, in accordance with their inclinations. This is called the supreme gradual explanation of the Dharma, in which the bodhisattva is skilled in knowing kindness and repaying kindness.

"Furthermore, what is called completely pure explanation is this. The bodhisattva, the great being, cultivates a mind of loving-kindness toward enemies and those hostile to him, and with a mind of loving-kindness he teaches the Dharma through many kinds of means to sentient beings who commit misdeeds, to careless persons and to the lazy; through many kinds of means he teaches the Dharma to the arrogant and haughty, and so on, down to persons who are poor and destitute; and he does so not to praise himself or disparage others, nor for the sake of food and drink, gain and fame. This is called the completely pure explanation of the Dharma, in which the bodhisattva is skilled in knowing kindness and repaying kindness.

"How, then, does one abide in accordance with the Dharma? One cultivates the virtuous qualities of the actions of body, speech and mind, and is endowed with complete purity; and knowing kindness and repaying kindness is, moreover, the full revealing of unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

"Furthermore, the bodhisattva, the great being, who knows kindness and repays kindness attends to the meaning. In order to obtain dhāraṇī through great learning and to make the lamp of the Dharma blaze, he should strive, for the benefit and happiness of all sentient beings, in generosity, moral discipline and great learning. He should make offerings to and serve the one who teaches and explains the Dharma. He does not ascribe faults to the Dharma, and he does not ascribe faults to the one who teaches and explains it. To give fearlessness to sentient beings with a mind free of harm and violence is called knowing kindness. To enjoy the pleasures of gods and humans and to attain the path that passes beyond sorrow is called knowing kindness and repaying kindness.

"Furthermore, the bodhisattva cultivates patience by four factors and completely abandons impatience. In order to adorn awakening, he gathers many sentient beings and sets them in the cultivation of patience; he himself practices patience, and he also establishes others in practicing patience; and he is free of fear. This is called knowing kindness. Because he has practiced patience in this way, no angry thought arises in him, and so he has an excellent retinue. Since no suffering or affliction arises, he has no remorse. And when he has left this body, he enjoys the pleasures of gods and humans and attains nirvāṇa. This is called repaying kindness.

"Son of noble family, furthermore, the bodhisattva undertakes diligence by four factors. Having completely abandoned laziness, he gathers sentient beings in order to adorn awakening. Because he undertakes diligence for the sake of the path to awakening, he is at ease when he sleeps and at ease when he wakes; being free of afflictions, happy qualities arise and increase in him, and he enjoys happiness in his body. This is called accomplishing his own benefit. Though the bodhisattva undertakes diligence, he does not harm sentient beings; he does not beat, abuse or revile them. This is called accomplishing the benefit of others. And when he has left this body, he enjoys the pleasures of gods and humans and is endowed with power and might, and by attaining the path to awakening he will attain the great fruit. These are called the four factors of the bodhisattva's undertaking of diligence.

"By cultivating meditative absorption, the bodhisattva completely abandons the agitated mind and gathers sentient beings in order to adorn awakening. Because he has cultivated meditative absorption for the sake of the path to awakening, even in this life he experiences worldly happiness, and his body and mind are secluded and at peace. This is called knowing kindness. Since his body and mind are thus secluded and at peace, he does not harm sentient beings. This is called repaying kindness. And when the bodhisattva has left this body, he obtains a completely pure body, enjoys perfect bliss, and attains complete nirvāṇa. These are called the four factors of the bodhisattva's cultivation of meditative concentration.

"Furthermore, the bodhisattva who knows kindness and repays kindness destroys ignorance by being endowed with wisdom, and in order to adorn awakening completely he gathers sentient beings through the four means of attraction. Because, by cultivating wisdom for the sake of awakening, he knows and realizes the expanse of phenomena, he enjoys happiness in his body; this is called accomplishing his own benefit. Bringing about the worldly benefit and the world-transcending benefit of sentient beings is called accomplishing the benefit of others. Because he has completely abandoned the two obscurations, the afflictive obscuration and the obscuration to the knowable, it is called the great fruit. These are called the four inconceivable factors of wisdom of the bodhisattva who knows kindness and repays kindness.

"Furthermore, through the higher knowledge that recollects former lives, the bodhisattva realizes with wisdom the things of the past. He examines the virtuous and evil deeds of sentient beings, and for the benefit and happiness of all sentient beings who belong to the virtuous side, the bodhisattva, the great being, through skillful means was born in the Tuṣita heaven with a perfect span of life. There he possessed three supreme things: supreme life, supreme form and supreme fame. When first he descended from that abode, he sent forth great rays of light that illuminated the ten directions, and he knew the time of entering his mother's womb, how long he would remain in the womb, and the time of his birth from the womb. Toward each of the ten directions, unsupported by anyone, he took seven steps, and proclaimed: 'This present body of mine is the very last.' The gods, nāgas, yakṣas, gandharvas, asuras, garuḍas, kiṃnaras and great mahoragas made offerings to him with flowers, incense, music, cymbals, banners and excellent parasols.

"Because he was endowed with the thirty-two marks of a great being, there was no body more supreme than his. By the force of loving-kindness he vanquished the armies of Māra. Each of his joints had the strength of a mighty wrestler. When he was a young boy, without having studied the affairs of the world, he understood and knew them all. Without a teacher, seeking by himself, he spontaneously accomplished unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. Urged by Brahmā, lord of the gods, he turned the wheel of Dharma for sentient beings. Resting evenly in meditative absorption, he did not move even when thunder roared. Beasts of prey came close to him and regarded him with affection, as if he were their father and mother. When an animal offered him alms, the Buddha knew its intention. Gods and nāgas sent down rain and bathed his body. The branches of the trees bent toward him and sheltered him with their shade. Even after he had become fully awakened, for six years the wicked Māra constantly sought an opening, but found none. Because he constantly rested evenly in meditative absorption, his mind of close mindfulness was perfected, and he was supremely skilled in the arising and ceasing of conceptual thought. This is called the inconceivable common birth of the bodhisattva.

"The uncommon birth is this: for the benefit and happiness of sentient beings, for example, madmen regain their former minds through the condition of seeing the Tathāgata, the blind come to see, a child lying head-reversed in the womb is safely delivered, the deaf come to hear with their ears, and those possessed of attachment, aversion and delusion are purified of the three poisons. Such a thing is called the inconceivable uncommon birth.

"Furthermore, what is called the common birth is the inconceivable conduct of the Tathāgata: he always lies down on his right side; like the king of lions, he does not stir, as a clump of grass stirs; even the Vairambha wind, king of winds, does not move so much as the hem of his robe; his gait as he walks is like that of the lion, king of beasts, or of the king of geese; when he sets out, he puts his right foot forward first; wherever he goes, the ground, high or low, becomes level; his alms food has no fault of foul smell, and none of it falls back from his mouth. This is called the inconceivable common birth.

"Furthermore, the inconceivable common birth is as follows. The soles of his feet are level and full. On the soles of his feet are wheels with a thousand spokes. His fingers are slender and long. His heels are round and full. The spaces between his fingers are joined by webbing. His hands and feet are soft and supple. His calves are round and tapering, like those of the eṇa antelope, king of deer. His ankle bones do not protrude. His hands reach to his knees. Like the king of horses, his private parts are withdrawn within a sheath. Like the nyagrodha tree, the proportions of his body are perfect. The hairs of his body point upward. Each hair curls to the right. The color of his body is like refined gold. He has a constant halo of light a fathom wide. His skin is supple and smooth, and is not stained by dust or dirt. Seven places of his body are full. The upper part of his body is like a lion's. His forearms are round and tapering. His collarbones are evenly full. He has the mark of a broad upper and lower body. He has forty teeth. His teeth are well set and even. His teeth are white. His cheeks are full like those of the lion, king of beasts. He has the supreme sense of taste. He has the mark of the fleshy protuberance. His tongue is long and broad. He has the voice of Brahmā. His eyes are the color of sapphire. His eyes are like those of the king of bulls. Between his eyebrows is a white curl of hair.

"In the same way he is endowed with the eighty inconceivable excellent minor marks, and each excellent minor mark has some hundred thousand supreme and sublime excellences. For the sake of each of those excellent minor marks, the bodhisattva, from the time he first gave rise to the mind set on awakening, being utterly steadfast in awakening, practiced those sublime deeds in order to know kindness and repay kindness, and therefore he attained unsurpassed awakening.

The Buddha said: "Because, long ago, for countless and immeasurable eons, the Tathāgata kept moral discipline completely pure, he came to have the mark of soles that are level and full. Through the condition of having made offerings to and honored his father and mother, his teachers, his lamas and persons endowed with good qualities, he came to have the mark of wheels on the soles of his feet. He did not harm sentient beings and had no thought of robbing or stealing; he went out from afar to greet his father and mother, his teachers, his lamas and persons endowed with good qualities, and escorted them on their way; he spread out seats for them, and, abandoning pride, served them, honored them and bowed to them. By that cause and that condition he came to have the mark of fingers that are slender and long. Because he possessed the three deeds mentioned above, he came to have the mark of heels that are round and full.

"Because he gathered many beings through the four means of attraction, by that cause and that condition he came to have the mark of the spaces between his fingers being joined by webbing. Because he anointed and massaged with butter and sesame oil the bodies of his father and mother, his teachers, his lamas and persons endowed with good qualities, by that cause and that condition his hands and feet are supple, long and soft. Because he never tired of cultivating virtuous qualities, by that cause and that condition his shoulders are even and his forearms round and tapering. If one delights in hearing the Dharma, is eager also to teach the Dharma to others, and runs errands for the sake of the Dharma, then by that cause and that condition one has the mark of ankle bones that do not show. By the cause and condition of having kept his three doors completely pure, of having tended the sick and given the gift of medicine, of being without pride and content with food and drink, his hands reach to his knees when he stands upright.

"If one reconciles with gentle words those who have fallen into enmity, and oneself practices the qualities that go with a sense of shame and leads others to practice them too, then by that cause and that condition one's private parts are withdrawn within a sheath, like those of the king of horses. Because he kept his own three doors completely pure and purified those of others as well, and healed with medicine sentient beings who had fallen ill through the disturbance of the four great elements, by that cause and that condition the marks of his body are complete.

"Because he delighted in hearing the Dharma and was eager to teach the Dharma to others, he has the mark of the hairs of his body pointing upward. If one reflects on the profound meaning of the Dharma, is eager to cultivate virtuous qualities, makes offerings to one's father and mother, one's teachers, one's lamas and persons endowed with good qualities, and clears away the stones, pebbles, thorns and filth that lie on the roads and paths at the stupas of the Buddha and the dwellings of the Saṅgha, then by that cause and that condition one comes to have the mark of each hair curling to the right. If one gives food, drink, ornaments and the like to others and has no thought of hatred, then by that cause and that condition one has the color of refined gold and the characteristic of a constant radiance.

"If one asks by what deed he has the mark of a single hair growing from each pore, it arises from that very same deed as its cause; and by that very same karmic cause he has the mark of a body that is supple and smooth and is not stained by dust or dirt. Because he constantly gave sentient beings the goods they needed, by that cause and that condition he has the mark of the seven places being evenly full. If, having destroyed one's own pride and arrogance, one attunes oneself to the minds of sentient beings with a mind that is by nature gentle and mild, acts in accordance with the Dharma, and teaches virtuous qualities so that unvirtuous ones may be abandoned, then by that cause and that condition one has the mark of an upper body like that of the lion, king of beasts, the mark of the space between the brows and the collarbones being evenly full, the mark of long fingers, and the mark of the body's outline being broad and smooth.

"Because he completely abandoned slander and reconciled those in dispute and enmity, by that cause and that condition he has the mark of forty teeth, the mark of well-set teeth, and the mark of even teeth. Because he cultivated loving-kindness in the desire realm, by that cause and that condition he has the mark of white teeth. Because, when he saw beggars, he greeted them and saw them on their way with a joyful mind, by that cause and that condition he came to have the mark of cheeks like a lion's. Because he regarded all sentient beings equally, as one's only child, by that cause and that condition he has the mark of the supreme sense of taste.

"If one constantly gives sentient beings the taste of the unsurpassed Dharma, reminds those who have forgotten and do not remember, oneself keeps the five precepts and urges others to keep them too, and with a mind of loving-kindness makes the great gift of the Dharma, then by that cause and that condition one has the mark of the fleshy protuberance on the crown and the mark of a tongue that is long and broad. Because he spoke truthful words, words that bring delight in the Dharma, and gentle and mild words, and did not speak untimely words, by that cause and that condition he has the voice of Brahmā. Because he cultivated a mind of compassion and regarded sentient beings as his father and mother, by that cause and that condition he has the mark of eyes like sapphire and eyes like those of the king of bulls. Because he did not conceal the merits of those endowed with good qualities, but praised and extolled them, by that cause and that condition he has the mark of the white curl of hair.

"Although the causes and conditions of these thirty-two marks have been explained one by one, their true cause and condition are moral discipline and diligence. Why is that? If, without keeping moral discipline and without practicing diligence, one cannot obtain even a human body, what need is there to speak of obtaining the thirty-two marks? The crown of the head that cannot be seen and the mark of the fleshy protuberance on the crown are not two different things. Furthermore, the causes are not fixed one to each: if in whatever one does there is no regret, by that cause and that condition one obtains the mark of soles that are level and full.

"Again, if one acts single-mindedly, by that cause one has the mark of wheels with a thousand spokes, the third mark and the fourth, the joining of the fingers, the seven places being evenly full, suppleness and smoothness, round shoulders, full collarbones, an upright body, and the mark of a tongue that is long and broad. Again, if one acts with careful consideration, by that cause one obtains the mark of long fingers, the mark of the hands reaching to the knees, the mark of a constant halo a fathom wide, and the mark of teeth that are well set and not crooked. If one acts with complete purity, by that cause one will obtain the remaining marks.

"Furthermore, because he gave rise to nothing but wholesome thoughts toward sentient beings, by that cause he obtains the mark of hands and feet that are supple and smooth, and of skin that is supple and smooth and is not stained by dust or dirt. Because he meditated and practiced step by step, and meditated and practiced at the proper time, by that cause he obtains the second, third and fourth marks. Because he was eager to cultivate virtuous qualities and had no regret, by that cause he obtains the mark of a body the color of gold, a constant radiance, white teeth, and the curl of hair between the eyebrows. If, even when praised and extolled, one does not grow proud, but conceals one's own merits and has no conceit, then by that cause one obtains the mark of private parts withdrawn within a sheath, like a horse's. If one dedicates to awakening whatever virtuous deeds one has done, then by that cause one obtains the mark of a single hair growing from each pore, hairs pointing upward, forty teeth, and the mark of the supreme sense of taste. If one is constantly diligent, then by that cause one obtains the mark of rounded cheeks and of an upper body like a lion's. Because he regarded all sentient beings with affection, as one's only child, by that cause he obtains the mark of teeth well set and even, eyes like sapphire, and eyes like those of the king of bulls. Because he had no weariness or fatigue in accomplishing virtuous qualities, he obtains the remaining marks.

"When the bodhisattva, the great being, practices and abides in the characteristics, he performs the deeds for the thirty-two marks. When he abides in pure conduct, he has the thirty-two marks, but they are neither complete nor clear. When he abides in the thirteenth stage of conduct, the marks too become manifestly clear and complete. Although the marks of the Buddha's qualities are countless, they would not suit sentient beings, for beings are of superior, middling and inferior capacity, and those marks are inconceivable. Therefore the Buddha spoke of thirty-two excellent marks.

"If all the merit that all sentient beings possess were gathered together in one place and heaped up, it would equal the mark of a single hair of the Tathāgata. If all the merit of all the pores were gathered and heaped together, it would equal one excellent minor mark. If all the merit of all the excellent minor marks were multiplied a hundredfold, it would equal one major mark, apart from the mark of the white curl of hair between the eyebrows and the mark of the crown of the head that cannot be seen. If all the other marks besides these were gathered together and multiplied a thousandfold, they would amount to those two higher marks. If all the merit of the thirty-two marks and the eighty excellent minor marks were gathered and heaped together and multiplied ten million times, it would equal the mark of the Tathāgata's voice, deep like thunder and resounding far. That voice of his resounds through buddha fields as numerous as the atoms of the immeasurable, boundless, inconceivable buddha fields. Because he practices loving-kindness and great compassion for the sake of sentient beings, this knowing kindness and repaying kindness of his is inconceivable.

"Thus, through each of those marks and excellent minor marks, he benefited and brought happiness to immeasurable, countless hundreds of thousands of koṭis of ordinary beings, as many as the atoms of the great earth, and led them to give rise to the mind set on awakening. Having thus gradually brought his practice to completion, he obtained both the marks and the excellent minor marks. When the marks and excellent minor marks were complete, he went to the foot of the tree of awakening, subdued the armies of Māra, became fully awakened, and truly turned the wheel of Dharma. For the benefit and happiness of ordinary beings he delivered them from the river of desire and set them on the shore of the perfection of wisdom, and so brought to completion the benefit and happiness of all sentient beings."

Then the Blessed One spoke these words to Ānanda, to the bodhisattvas, the great beings, and to all the great assembly: "Sons of noble family, which of you, ever mindful of the Buddha's kindness and in order to protect the true Dharma, will write in volumes, read and recite this most profound sūtra, the Great Skillful Means Sūtra on Repaying Kindness? Who, in the evil times to come, will rouse great diligence and protect and uphold this unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening? Who, to protect the Dharma, is able to spread this sūtra far and wide and to work for the benefit and happiness of all sentient beings?"

When he had spoken thus, some hundred thousand myriads of bodhisattvas, great beings, who were in that very assembly rose from their seats, arranged their robes, draped their upper robes over one shoulder, placed their right knees on the ground, bowed with palms joined, and said to the Buddha: "Blessed One, in the evil times to come we are eager to protect this unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, to uphold the true Dharma, and to work for the benefit of many sentient beings."

Then the bodhisattva Siṃha said, "Blessed One, I too am eager to gather sentient beings through many different means."

The bodhisattva Vajra said, "I am eager to see to it, by whatever means, that sentient beings who would fall into the three lower realms do not fall."

The bodhisattva Mañjuśrī said, "I am eager to fulfill completely whatever sentient beings may wish for."

The bodhisattva Jñānaketu said, "I am eager to give sentient beings the victory banner of the Dharma of great wisdom."

The bodhisattva Dharmaketu said, "Blessed One, I am eager to give the Dharma completely to sentient beings."

The bodhisattva Sūryaprabha said, "Blessed One, I am eager to give happiness to sentient beings."

The bodhisattva Candraprabha said, "Blessed One, I am eager to urge sentient beings to engage in the work of merit."

The bodhisattva Śramaṇa said, "Blessed One, I am eager to urge all sentient beings not to dwell in heedlessness."

The bodhisattva Akṣayamati said, "Blessed One, I am eager to teach all sentient beings to see the meaning of the inexhaustible sphere."

The bodhisattva Moonlight Son said, "Blessed One, I am eager to give unsurpassed happiness to all sentient beings."

Thus each of those bodhisattvas made a pledge. In order to adorn awakening completely, to benefit all sentient beings and bring them happiness, remembering the Buddha's kindness, and in order to repay the Buddha's kindness, they rose from their seats, joined their palms, bowed, and said, "Please entrust this sūtra of the Buddha to us."

At that time the venerable Ānanda said this: "Blessed One, what is the name of this sūtra, and how should we uphold it?"

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "This sūtra is called The Compendium of All Grounds of Virtue. It is also called The Great Skillful Means. It is also called The Conduct of the Sublime Intention. It is also called The Repaying of the Buddha's Kindness."

The Blessed One then said this to Ānanda and to the bodhisattva great beings: "Practice it diligently, just as it has been taught!"

When this chapter of the Buddha's final instructions had been taught, seventy-two thousand śrāvakas gave rise to the mind set on unsurpassed awakening. Moreover, the gods, nāgas, yakṣas, gandharvas, kiṃnaras, mahoragas, humans, nonhumans and all the others rejoiced at what the Buddha had said.

This concludes the ninth chapter, called Close Attendance, from The Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha Repays Kindness.

This completes the sūtra called The Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha Repays Kindness.

Translator's notes

This sūtra was translated into Tibetan from Chinese, and the title line gives the Chinese title in Tibetan letters. Many of the bodhisattva names are Tibetan renderings of Chinese names whose Indian forms cannot be identified with confidence; these, and the disciple's name rendered "Fair Goodness," are translated literally. ལམ་ཆེན་བརྟན is taken as Mahāpanthaka, though the final element is unusual. The light is said to go to the east, and the bodhisattvas there see it coming from the west, which is consistent: from that eastern world, the Sahā world lies to the west. The world system, its buddha and his field (Highest Supreme, King of Joy, Perfectly Arrayed) are translated literally, since their Indian forms cannot be determined with confidence from this text, which was translated from Chinese. The names of the world system, its buddha and his field (Light of Qualities, Attending to Signs, Utterly Pure) are translated literally, since their Indian forms cannot be determined with confidence in this sūtra translated from Chinese. The name of the buddha ཉི་ཟླ་མར་མེའི་འོད has been rendered literally as "Sun, Moon, and Lamp Light"; this sūtra was translated into Tibetan from Chinese, and a confident Sanskrit form cannot be established. "The Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha Repays Kindness" renders the sūtra's own title as given in the passage. The names of the world system དབང་ཕྱུག་གྲགས་པའི་རྒྱལ་པོ ("King of Mighty Renown"), the buddha པད་མོ་ལེ་བརྒན་གྱི་འོད ("Crimson Lotus Light"; ལེ་བརྒན denotes a vermilion or deep red color), and the buddha field དྲི་མ་དང་བྲལ་བ ("Stainless") are translated by meaning, since this sūtra was rendered into Tibetan from Chinese and their Sanskrit forms cannot be established with confidence. Here the buddha's name appears as པད་མོའི་ལེ་བརྒན་གྱི་འོད་ཆགས་པ, a slight variant of the form in the previous passage; it has been kept as "Crimson Lotus Light" for consistency. ཀུན་ནས་གླགས་པ་བཅུས་ཀུན་དུ་གླགས appears to stand for the standard ཀུན་ནས་དཀྲིས་པ་བཅུ, the ten entanglements (paryavasthāna); it is rendered "entangled by the ten entanglements." The prince's name སུ་ཤ་དེ་བ is a phonetic transcription; since this sūtra was translated from Chinese, it is taken to represent the Chinese transcription of Sujāti, the prince of the parallel Chinese story, and rendered "Sujāti". The phrase rendered "no one could be said to go without" (གང་ཡང་གཏམས་ལས་ཆད་ཅེས་བྱ་བ་མེད) is somewhat obscure in the Tibetan. In this piece the prince is named both སུ་ཤ་དེ་བ (rendered Sujāti, as earlier) and རྒྱལ་བུ་དགེ་དོན ("Prince Good Aim"). The narrative makes clear they are the same boy, and དགེ་དོན appears to be a Tibetan semantic rendering of his name, so it is given as Sujāti throughout. The sentence beginning "If they rested even a little" reads བསྟིར་ནི; it is taken as "rest, stop" (སྟིར / བསྟི), which fits the context. "Measures" renders རྒྱ་མ, a unit of weight (corresponding to the Chinese jin, "catty," of the source of this sūtra, which was translated into Tibetan from Chinese). The prince's list names "eleven merits" but the items given do not clearly number eleven; the list is translated as it stands. In the final lines སྔ་མཁོ is read as སྔ་མ་ (ཇི་ལྟ་བ) བཞིན, "as before." In this piece the prince is called རྒྱལ་བུ་དོན་གྲུབ ("Prince Accomplished Aim," the usual Tibetan for Siddhārtha), where earlier pieces called him སུ་ཤ་དེ་བ and དགེ་དོན; since the narrative plainly concerns the same prince, Sujāti is kept throughout. The mother's name ལྷ་མཛེས ("Beautiful Goddess") is taken as the Buddha's mother Māyā, as the parallel identification with Śuddhodana requires; the Tibetan form is not the usual one for her name. In the comparison of lights, "or like night compared with the light of a precious jewel" renders a compressed phrase (མཚན་མོ་དང་ནོར་བུ་རིན་པོ་ཆེའི་འོད་དུ་བསྒྲུན་པ) whose exact construction is uncertain. The seventy bodhisattva names are Tibetan renderings, apparently made from the Chinese version of this sūtra, and most cannot be matched with confidence to Sanskrit forms; they are therefore translated into English by meaning. Only names whose Tibetan is a standard rendering of a known Sanskrit name are given in Sanskrit (Vimalakīrti, Brahmadatta, Jinadatta). Several renderings are approximate, notably Giver of Rebirth (སྲིད་ལེན་སྦྱིན), Not Passed Beyond read here as "Unsurpassed" (འདས་པ་མེད་པ, possibly "Unsurpassable"), and Trunk of Great Weight (རབ་ཏུ་ལྕི་བའི་སྡོང་པོ). The list as given contains sixty-nine names before "and others." "The mind of equality toward all those sentient beings, which is the antidote" renders མཉམ་སྙོམས་པའི་གཉེན་པོའི་སེམས; the word གཉེན་པོ ("antidote, counteragent") gives this chapter its title, and the construction could also be read as "the mind of the antidote that is equality." This sūtra was translated into Tibetan from Chinese, and several food and drink names are sound-spellings that are hard to identify. "Pana, pushena and khadane" seem to stand for the Indian categories of drinks, soft foods (bhojanīya) and hard foods (khādanīya). "Yulishi" and "bulashaka" are rendered as spelled. "Amragarbha" is probably aśmagarbha, emerald. The Tibetan word rendered "agate" (spug) is a gem whose exact identity is uncertain. The phrase rendered "in the early morning" (khye'u sus) is obscure in the Tibetan, and the rendering follows the context. The creature called smigs pa, here rendered "centipedes," is not securely identified. The donor is called a "householder" once in the Tibetan and is the same person. The name of the bodhisattva དགའ་བའི་རྒྱལ་པོ is rendered by its meaning, King of Joy, since this sūtra was translated from Chinese and no Sanskrit form can be identified with confidence. The names of the first three hells are cries of pain, here given as sounds; the list follows this sūtra's own sequence, which differs from the usual eight hot hells. The hell guardian's name ཨ་བང is a transcription, apparently of the Chinese name of the ox-headed hell jailer in the Chinese original of this sūtra; it is given here as Abang. The king's name ཨུ་ཏྲ་ཡ་ན is taken as Udayana, the king traditionally credited with the first sandalwood image of the Buddha. The disciple ཉེ་བར་སྐྱོང could not be identified with confidence and is translated by meaning as "Near Protector"; the one described as supreme in the divine eye is usually Aniruddha. ལྕགས་ཐོས is read as ལྕགས་ཐོ "iron hammer" with the instrumental. ཁྲི་བརྒྱ་སྟོང is rendered loosely as "hundreds of thousands of myriads," a large indefinite number. Here དོན་གྲུབ is plainly the Bodhisattva, son of Śuddhodana, and is given as Siddhārtha, unlike the Sujāti of an earlier chapter. Several names are Tibetan transcriptions of the Chinese forms in this sūtra, which was translated from Chinese: ཨ་ཁྱི་ཏ is taken as Agnidatta, the king who gave the Buddha horse fodder; ཐ་བྱེའུ as Dravya, the monk in charge of seating; ཕན་ཐིག as Panthaka; སུ་བར་ཏ་ལ as Subhadra, the aged last disciple. The daughter's name ཟིལ་དངར་གྱི་རོ is translated by meaning as "Taste of Nectar." In this text the bride given to Siddhārtha is called སྐྱེ་དགུའི་བདག་མོ, Prajāpatī, and is kept as such. སྦྲུལ་འཚོང་མ "snake-seller" is read as སྨད་འཚོང་མ "courtesan." ཞག་དུ་ན་ཚུལ་དུ་འཇུག is unclear; "until the observance begins" is a tentative rendering. ཤེད་མ་བྱེ་བ and བུས་པ in Cunda's description are read as "feeble" and བྱིས་པ "child." The low-caste sweeper ཕྱག་དར་ལུང་སྐྱེད་པ could not be identified with confidence (possibly Nītī) and is translated descriptively. The gandharva youth's name ཆོས་ཀྱི་ཕྲེང་བ could not be identified with confidence and is translated by meaning as "Garland of Dharma." The prince's name བཟོད་པ is likewise given in English as "Forbearance." In the list of Vipaśyin's epithets, སྟོན་པ་རྒྱལ་པོ stands where the stock list has "teacher of gods and humans, buddha, blessed one"; it is rendered as written, "the Teacher and King." བྱུར, rendered "bane," means a calamity or misfortune. ས་སྐད་འཐུན་པར is taken as "with one voice." ངས at the start of the sentence explaining the mountain's name is read as introducing the reason ("because"), not as "I." བལ་གྱི་གླང་པོ་ཆེ, literally "wool elephant," is read as རྒྱལ་གྱི་གླང་པོ་ཆེ "royal elephant." The mountain's name འཕགས་པ་གནས is translated by meaning, since the text itself explains it. This sūtra was translated into Tibetan from Chinese, and the narrative follows the Chinese story of the deer-born girl and her five hundred sons. The text reads "set her upon a wool (bal) elephant," which makes no sense here; it is taken as a slip for "royal (rgyal) elephant," matching the royal elephant the king rode in the preceding passage. The phrase "Thinking that what was said of the queen must be true" interprets a terse clause that literally says "thinking the queen would come out true," that is, believing the charge that she was born of an animal. The clause rendered "even the gods will follow their lead" is terse in the Tibetan and its sense is somewhat uncertain. "I take no notice of it" renders a phrase that literally says "I have no awareness of it." The text says that "the girl herself" went in search of food; since the context shows it was the mother who went foraging while the girl guarded the orchard, this is read as referring to the mother. "Semblance period" renders the phrase "practicing the Dharma with a semblance (gzugs brnyan)," that is, the period of the semblance Dharma after a buddha's passing. The steward's reply literally reads "we are gza'," which makes no sense here; it is read as gzhi, "the residents" (the resident community, as against the visiting monks). "Leave it the dry and take the wet for themselves" renders a compressed idiom describing the parents' self-sacrifice in caring for an infant. The young monk is said to be learned in the "mātṛkā," here the Abhidharma matrices (the third basket, alongside the Sūtras and the Vinaya), not the class of goddesses. The old monk's name, Mahala, is given as written; it seems to be a transcription and is not further identified. The mountain is rendered Īśvara from the Tibetan "Lord's Mountain"; its identification is uncertain. The princes' names, dge don and ngan don, are translated by meaning as "Good Purpose" and "Evil Purpose"; the parallel Chinese sūtra calls them "Good Friend" and "Bad Friend," and I could not identify a securely attested Sanskrit form. The king's name is given in transliteration as Mahārāja. "Nepalese elephants" renders bal gyi glang po che; bal could alternatively mean "wool," and the sense is uncertain. "Fowlers and fishermen" renders bya dang nya shor ba, literally "birds and fish lost/escaped"; the sense is taken from context as those who catch birds and fish. "The color of the waters" renders chu'i mdog literally; it may refer to dangerous discolored waters among the ocean's hazards. Several gem names in the description of the castle are uncertain: kekeru is kept as a gem name, and "agate" renders spug tentatively. "Crystal maidens" renders shel gyi bu mo literally. Lishibar renders the transcription li shi bar, which I cannot identify with a Sanskrit name; it is given in phonetic form. In the last sentence the Tibetan reads "Prince Lishibar" (rgyal bu), evidently a slip for "King" (rgyal po), as elsewhere in the passage. The instrument ཇེང is rendered "lute"; it appears to be a stringed instrument (possibly reflecting the Chinese zheng, since this sūtra was translated from Chinese). The phrase བཟའ་དང་མགྲོན is taken as "host and guest"; བཟའ could alternatively be read as "spouse", but that sense does not fit the context. སོ་ཚིས is taken as "provisions, means of support." དགའ་མགུ་རངས here is an ordinary verb phrase describing the king's joy, not the proper name used earlier, and is translated as such. The final sentence (ཕོ་ཉ་ལ་སྤྲིང་བ་ལྟར་བྱས་ནས ... ཕོ་ཉ་འོངས་པ་ལྟར་བྱས) is somewhat compressed; it is read as King Lishibar complying with the message and staging the prince's return as if a messenger were announcing it. ཕོ་སྨོས is read as "men and women alike" (as if ཕོ་མོ). In the prince's words to the people, ཉོན་མོངས is used in its ordinary sense of hardship, not as the technical term for mental affliction. The last line gives the name of this section of the sūtra, not the title of the whole text. ན་བུན (literally "mist") in the list of the Buddha's chief disciples could not be identified with confidence and is translated literally as "Mist". The phrase རྒྱལ་པོའི་ཁབ་ཡུལ་བཱ་རཱ་ཎ་སཱི ("Rājagṛha, the land of Vārāṇasī") is translated as it stands, though Rājagṛha and Vārāṇasī are normally distinct cities. The "mighty elephant" renders སྤོས་ཀྱི་གླང་པོ་ཆེ, the "fragrant elephant" (gandhahastin) of exceptional strength. ཆུ་གང་བྱེད in the king's reflection is unclear; it is taken in the sense of "cherish one another," which the following clause supports. The name of the past buddha, རིགས་པར་སྟོན་པ, could not be identified with a known Sanskrit name and is translated literally as "Rightly Teaching". The sentence about the Buddha's wounded toe shifts abruptly into the Buddha's own first-person speech, and is rendered as his words. བལ་གླང here is the old Tibetan word for "elephant" (literally "Nepalese ox"), not the place name Nepal; it is rendered "elephant" accordingly. The two fields of merit, མངོན་པར་འདུ་བྱེད་པའི and མངོན་པར་འདུ་བྱེད་པ་མ་ཡིན་པའི, are rendered "conditioned" and "unconditioned"; the text does not say which of the listed recipients belongs to which field. ཨེ་མ་འོ in Śāriputra's lament is an exclamation of grief here, rendered "ah me". The piece ends at the start of Śāriputra's speech, which continues in the next piece. The king's name འོད་ཟེར་ཆེན་པོ could not be confidently identified with a Sanskrit form (the sūtra is translated from Chinese), so it is rendered by its meaning, "Great Radiance". བལ་གྱི་གླང་པོ་ཆེ is the old Tibetan expression for "elephant", not a reference to Nepal. "The six observance days" renders དུས་དྲུག་གི་ཉི་མ, taken as the six monthly fast days. The king's reflection continues into the next piece. In the opening sentence, དམ་བྱིན་པར་གྱུར་ན is read in context as "if I were to break that vow"; the wording is unusual. The tree god's act, དམིག་ཚུགས་བྱས, is rendered "struck"; the exact sense of the expression is uncertain, but the result, the brahmin falling senseless, is clear. རི་ག་ན་དྷ is taken as Mount Gandha ("Fragrance"), fitting the sandalwood grove; the Tibetan spells it ga na dha. The emanated man's four-line verse is terse and its exact sense is uncertain: it seems to say that the "arrow" is drawn out not by strength but by much hearing of the Dharma; མི་བྱིན་ is read as "cannot remove it". བལ་གྱི་གླང་པོ་ཆེ and བལ་གླང are the old Tibetan words for "elephant", not references to Nepal. The king is called བཻ་དཱུརྱ (Vaiḍūrya) in this text, which was translated from Chinese, where the king usually known in Sanskrit as Virūḍhaka is called "Lapis King"; the text's form is kept. ཡུལ་སེར་སྐྱ་བ is taken as Kapilavastu, the Śākya homeland. In the trampling passage the text says བ་ལང་རྨིག་པ ("the oxen's hooves") although the animals were elephants; it is rendered neutrally as "the beasts". ཤཱཀ་རིགས་ལྷ, "the god of the Śākyas", is an epithet of the Buddha. བརྟོད་པ (twice) is read as བརྩོད་པ, "to contend, dispute," which the context plainly requires. The phrase ཁྲབ་ཀྱིས་ལ་ལྷགས་པར་བྱའོ is obscure as written; it is rendered by sense as the armor "falling loose," in line with the following sentence where the Śākyas leave the soldiers naked. སྲོག་ཕྱོགས་ན is taken as "as their life ebbs away." In the girls' praise, བྱམས་པའི་ཕ་མ is rendered "loving father and mother," not as the name Maitreya. The narration in this piece shifts between "we" and "they" for the Śākya daughters, as the Tibetan does; the shifts are kept. In this passage འོད་ཟེར་ཆེན་པོ is not a name but Utpalavarṇā's "great rays of light," and བལ in སྐྲ་ཡང་བལ་ནས is the verb "to pluck, tear out" (her hair), not the place name Nepal. མི་གཏོལ་ཏོ is taken as "does not compare." The first part of this piece continues Utpalavarṇā's own story; from the Śākya daughters' attaining the Dharma eye, the narrative returns to the frame, and Gautamī then speaks, recounting her own request in the first person. The quotation marks reflect this reading. ལྷའི་བདུད་དམ་ངན་པར་ལྟ་བ is read as a list ("divine māras, those with evil views"); the comparison it introduces is somewhat abrupt in the Tibetan and is kept as it stands. The phrase ལས་གྲངས་མེད་པས་གླགས་པ is taken as "open to," reading གླགས as "opening, opportunity." The eight rules of respect (gurudharmas) are the special conditions under which Mahāprajāpatī Gautamī was admitted. The exchange beginning "Most excellent!" is spoken by Gautamī to Ānanda; the Tibetan does not mark the change of speaker. Here སྟོན་པ, "teacher," is applied to Ānanda, not to the Buddha. དགའ་མགུ་རངས is used here as an ordinary phrase for joy, not as a name. ཚོར་བ, glossed elsewhere as "feeling," is here the etymological gloss of "buddha" as "awareness, being awake" (rendering the Chinese jue), and is translated accordingly. The four "collections" (གཞུང་གཅིག་ནས་འཕྲོས་པ, གཞུང་བར་མ, གཞུང་སིལ་བུ, གཞུང་རིང་པོ) are identified as the four Āgamas, following the Chinese original from which this sūtra was translated. "The three close applications of mindfulness" are the Buddha's three special applications of mindfulness, by which he is unmoved by disciples who listen, do not listen, or are mixed. This Kangyur text is a translation of a Chinese scripture (the Sūtra on Skillful Means and Repaying the Buddha's Kindness), and this passage is a scholastic question-and-answer. The monk named by the Tibetan "Ribs" or "Flank" is taken as Pārśva, the Sarvāstivāda master, whose Sanskrit name means "side, flank." The reasoning about which knowledge is "shared" by the three vehicles and by the two vehicles is obscure in the Tibetan; it has been translated as it stands. "Falling back" renders phyir ldog pa, regression or retrogression. The "path of contemplation" renders bsam pa'i lam, which here corresponds to the path of cultivation (bhāvanāmārga) set against the path of seeing. "The ninefold defilement" renders dgu pa'i kun nas nyon mongs pa as written; the count is unclear beside the seventy-two of the higher realms. The monk "Ever Chewing," who chews the cud like an ox, is very likely Gavāṃpati, but the Tibetan name is not the standard rendering of that name, so it is translated literally. In the mirror example, sbrul 'tshong ma ("snake-seller") is taken as a slip for smad 'tshong ma, "a woman who sold herself, a prostitute." The question about "Thus have I heard" refers to Ānanda's request to be taught what the Buddha had said in the twenty years before Ānanda became his attendant. "Manifest ritual" and "unmanifest ritual" render cho ga bstan pa and cho ga bstan pa med pa, which correspond to the Abhidharma pair of manifest and unmanifest action (vijñapti and avijñapti). In "he even set himself up against the Buddha," the verb sngo ba is unclear as written; the sense of rivalry or boasting is inferred from context. The sentence on Ajātaśatru's seven days in the Black Line hell is obscure in the Tibetan, and its comparison with heavy evil experienced among humans has been rendered as closely as the wording allows. The names "Kalandaka" and "city" in the gloss on place names are given as the Tibetan states them. "A wheel of speech" renders ngag gi 'khor lo literally; it appears to mean that the phrase "going for refuge" is a verbal formula whose real object is then specified. The faculty "of coming to know what was not known" and the faculty "of final knowledge" are the first two of the three undefiled faculties (anājñātamājñāsyāmīndriya and ājñendriya). "The ending of one's own body" renders bdag gi lus mthar phyin pa, apparently one's own final nirvāṇa as against that of others. De wi and Pa ri, given the three refuges before there was a Saṅgha, are taken as the two merchants Trapuṣa and Bhallika; the Tibetan forms reflect the Chinese transcriptions of their names. "The formal act of a motion and three proclamations" renders zhu ba dang gsol ba bzhi'i las, the Vinaya procedure for full ordination. "The four supports" and "the four root offenses" render gnas bzhi and rtsa ba bzhi, the four niśrayas and the four pārājikas. "The formal act of a motion and proclamations, four in all" renders གསོལ་བ་དང་ཞུ་བ་བཞིའི་ལས, the jñapticaturtha karman used for full ordination. "Probation that satisfies the Saṅgha" renders དགེ་འདུན་ལ་མགུ་བ (mānatva), and ཆད་པའི་ལས here is the penalty act of the saṅghāvaśeṣa procedure, not "annihilation". "Wrongs by nature" and "wrongs by prescription" render རང་བཞིན་གྱི་སྡིག and བཅས་པའི་སྡིག (prakṛtisāvadya and pratikṣepaṇasāvadya). The animal in the simile, བན་གླང, is uncertain; it is taken here as a bull elephant, as in the comparison of a drunkard to a maddened elephant, but it may mean an ox or bull. In the opening line the Tibetan spyan du rung ba is read as spyad du rung ba, "can be engaged in", as in the parallel phrase of the previous passage. "Manifest form" renders rnam par rig byed kyi gzugs, the Abhidharma vijñaptirūpa. The word rngub, "draught, sip", is taken as each measure of alcohol one refrains from drinking. The answer on the supreme discipline names meditative concentration first but then gives reasons for the prātimokṣa discipline's distinctiveness; it is translated as it stands. "Action" and "path of action" render las and las kyi lam (karman and karmapatha); "manifest form" renders rnam par rig byed kyi gzugs (vijñaptirūpa). The sentence on the first instant reads literally "is accompanied by the rite and is not without the rite either"; from the following sentence, the sense seems to be that the first instant has both aspects, the one tied to the rite and the one without it, while later instants have only the second. The "seven kinds" are presumably the seven assemblies of the Buddha's followers named at the start of the passage. The clause rendered "they are distinct" reads literally "is contradictory" and is taken as introducing the distinction drawn in what follows. "A small part," "a large part," and "the whole" render the three classes of layman, those of partial, greater, and complete practice. The sentence rendered "is not obtained in a twofold ranking" is obscure; the text goes on to give two ways of ranking the precepts, by sequence and by the quality of mind, and says they do not correspond. The place name ga bu sha is taken as Kapiśa, but the identification is uncertain. "The fast" renders phyem red, which is not a normal Tibetan word; it is taken here as a sound-spelling of a Chinese term for the fast of not eating after midday. The place name yog sha could not be identified and is given in phonetic form. The monk ig zhi is taken as Koṭikarṇa, whose story of meeting hungry ghosts and being guided by Kātyāyana this passage recalls; the Tibetan form looks like a sound-spelling from Chinese. This sūtra is a translation from the Chinese (Da fangbian fo bao'en jing), which explains some phrasing. In Maudgalyāyana's thought the Tibetan reads "I" (ང་ནི) where "now" (ད་ནི) is plainly meant; translated as "now". In Śakra's speech the subject "as for me" is supplied from context for the elliptical "sport and delight" (རྩེ་དགའ་ནི). The "pleasure grove of Supreme Delight" (རབ་ཏུ་དགའ་བའི་སྐྱེད་མོས་ཚལ) is probably the Nandana grove, but is translated literally. "Noble ones would dwell in assembly" (འཕགས་པའི་འདུ་ལ་གནས་པར) is obscure; the Chinese parallel has the Buddha predicting that Piṇḍola would dwell in Godānīya, which may lie behind it. In the sentence on later buddhas the Tibetan as transmitted lacks a negative (གང་ལས་ཀྱང་ཚུལ་ཁྲིམས་བརྙེས), but both the conclusion ("not equal") and the Kāśyapa example (མ་བརྙེས) require "do not obtain"; translated accordingly as an evident omission. མྱ་ངན་ལས་བཟླས་པ here means "caused to pass into nirvāṇa," not mantra recitation. The precepts are said to be obtained "from" (ལས) beings, i.e. with respect to them, rendered "with respect to". "Appropriated and not appropriated" (ཟིན་པ་དང་མ་ཟིན་པ) is the Abhidharma pair upātta and anupātta, matter bound up with consciousness and matter that is not, here parallel to sentient and non-sentient. The sentence "All the buddhas of the three times are equal..." is somewhat garbled in the Tibetan (དུས་གསུམ་མཉམ་གྱི) and is rendered by its evident sense. The "Dharma body of five parts" is the fivefold dharmakāya: moral discipline, concentration, wisdom, liberation, and the knowledge and vision of liberation. The second "I am a butcher" is spelled ཤན་པ, a variant of ཤེན་པ. "Moral discipline" for non-restraint (སྡོམ་པ་མ་ཡིན་པའི་ཁྲིམས, asaṃvara) is rendered "discipline" since it is a binding commitment to harmful livelihood, not ethical conduct. The chapter-opening sentence on bodhisattvas teaching and correcting at the right time is terse and rendered literally. "The man who was to carry back the report" (སྦྲོན་པའི་མི) is taken as the scout sent by the bandit chief; the Tibetan does not state explicitly whether he is the same person as the friend who gave the warning. ཁྱད་དུ་བསད is the idiom "to disregard, to make light of." In the final sentence ཤིས་པར་བྱ་བའི་ཕྱིར ("to bring good fortune") most naturally attaches to Śakra's offering, though the clause order leaves its scope slightly open. "Kauśika" (ཀཽ་ཤི་ཀ) is Śakra's clan name. In the list of eight things not to be heeded, the Tibetan gives gain (རྙེད་པ) and profit (ཁེ) as separate items, and the list is translated as it stands rather than as the usual eight worldly concerns. "Floggings" renders གཞུ་རྡེག, literally "beating with a bow," evidently meaning corporal punishment. The lion's name ཡི་དམ་བརྟན is rendered by its meaning, "Firm Vow" (Chinese parallel: Jianshi, "firm vow"); no Sanskrit form is confidently attested. The mountain རི་དྲང་སྲོང་འཕགས is likewise translated literally as "Noble Sage Mountain." In the hunter's words at home, the Tibetan places the shaving of hair and beard within his speech and the donning of the robe in the narrative; the division is followed as written. གདོན་མི་ཟ་བར is the idiom "without doubt", not the graha spirit of the glossary entry. The phrase ས་གཞི་ལས་ཀྱང་འབོག་ཅིང་འགྲོ is obscure; rendered as crawling along the ground. The number of diseases is given as written, བརྒྱ་རྩ་བཞི "one hundred and four", though the usual figure in this tradition is four hundred and four. The Buddha addresses Ānanda but uses the plural "sons of noble family", as the Tibetan has it. ཕ་རོལ་ཏུ་ཕྱིན་པར་བྱ་བ in the medicine section is taken in its literal sense, "bring across to the far shore", rather than as "perfection". In this passage བྱམས་པ is the quality loving-kindness, not the name Maitreya. The text speaks of "four factors" for patience, diligence and concentration but does not number them off explicitly; the translation follows the Tibetan without supplying a count. In the section on concentration the text moves between ཏིང་ངེ་འཛིན (meditative absorption) and བསམ་གཏན (meditative concentration), and both renderings are kept as written. The paired terms སྐྱེ་བ་ཐུན་མོང་བ and སྐྱེ་བ་ཐུན་མོང་མ་ཡིན་པ, rendered "common birth" and "uncommon birth", are unusual and probably reflect the Chinese source of this sūtra; their exact intended sense is uncertain. In the proclamation, ནངས་ཀྱི་ཐ་མ (literally "last of mornings") is taken as the familiar "this is my last birth"; ནངས may be a slip for a word meaning "birth" or "existence". ཤའི་དོ་ཀེར is taken as the fleshy protuberance on the crown (uṣṇīṣa), the mark expected at this point in the list. The list gives exactly thirty-two marks, in its own order, and the eyes (not the eyelashes) are compared to the king of bulls, as written. In this passage བྱམས་པ is the quality loving-kindness, not Maitreya, and འོད་ཟེར་ཆེན་པོ is "great rays of light", not the name Great Radiance. In this passage བྱམས་པ is the quality loving-kindness, not Maitreya. The phrase འདོད་པའི་ཁམས་ཀྱི་བྱམས་པ ("loving-kindness in the desire realm") is translated as written; its precise intent, perhaps loving-kindness directed toward beings of the desire realm, is unclear. ལུས་ཀྱི་མཐའ་ཡང་ཞིང་འཇམ is read with ཡངས "broad" for ཡང, matching the mark of a broad body. The final sentence (ཅི་བྱེད་དོ་ཅོག་ལ་གཅིག་ཏུ་མ་ངེས་ཏེ) is taken to mean that a mark does not have only one fixed cause. The "second", "third" and "fourth" marks are referred to by number only, apparently by their place in the list given earlier in the sūtra. བྱ་བརྟག་ཏུ་བྱས་ན is rendered "acts with careful consideration"; if བརྟག stands for བརྟན, it would mean "acts steadfastly". The phrases "practices and abides in the characteristics" and "the thirteenth stage of conduct" (སྤྱོད་པ་བཅུ་གསུམ་པ) follow the Tibetan; the latter probably refers to a scheme of thirteen stages of bodhisattva practice, the last being buddhahood. The bodhisattva names སེང་གེ and རྡོ་རྗེ are given as Siṃha and Vajra. In this passage བྱམས་པ is the quality loving-kindness, not Maitreya. This sūtra was translated into Tibetan from Chinese, and several bodhisattva names in this list cannot be matched with confidence to Indian forms. "Moonlight Son" (zla 'od bu) is a literal rendering, and "Śramaṇa" stands for dge sbyong, read as a name. Jñānaketu, Dharmaketu, Sūryaprabha, Candraprabha and Akṣayamati are the standard equivalents.
